What a wretched man I am! Who will rescue me from this body of death? Thanks be to God, through Jesus Christ our Lord! - Romans 7:24-25

Scripture is quoted from the Berean Standard Bible, kept closer to the author’s own Bible where his argument rests on its wording; capitals for God in quotations (He, His) follow the BSB. “You+” marks every plural “you” (the older “ye”); a plain “you” is singular or general.

The Preface

THE church of God has always acknowledged that the doctrine of original sin is one of the fundamental truths of the Christian faith we profess. It is, moreover, a special part of that distinctive possession of truth enjoyed by those whose religion toward God is built on divine revelation and rests finally on it. As the world by its wisdom never knew God rightly, so its wise men were always utterly ignorant of this inborn evil in themselves and in others. With us, the doctrine of it, and conviction of it, lie at the very foundation of all our dealings with God, both as to pleasing him here and as to obtaining the enjoyment of him hereafter. It is also known what bearing it has on the great truths concerning the person of Christ, his mediation, its fruits and effects, and all the benefits we are made partakers of by it. Unless original sin is supposed, not one of these can be truly known or savingly believed. For this reason it has been treated at length by many holy and learned men, both in ancient and in more recent times. Some have labored to lay open its nature, some its guilt and demerit; and they have also defended the truth about it against the opposition made to it in past and present ages. Most have considered these things in their full extent and breadth, as they concern all men by nature, together with the state and condition of those who are wholly under its power and guilt. Many have also fully shown how men are by it disabled and made incapable in themselves of giving the obedience required either in the law or in the gospel, so as to free themselves from the curse of the one or to make themselves partakers of the blessing of the other. Moreover, the Scripture abundantly testifies that remainders of it abide in believers after their regeneration and conversion to God, and this too has been fully taught and confirmed, as also how its guilt is pardoned to them, and by what means its power is weakened in them. All these things, I say, have been treated at length, to the great benefit and edification of the church. In what we now have in view, therefore, we take them all for granted, and only try to carry further the laying open of indwelling sin in its workings and its opposition to the law and grace of God in believers. Nor do I intend to discuss anything that has been disputed about it. What the Scripture plainly reveals and teaches about it, what believers clearly find by experience in themselves, and what they may learn from the examples and confessions of others, will be set out in a way suited to the capacity of the lowest and weakest who are concerned in it. And many things seem to make the handling of it at this time not unnecessary. Its effects and fruits, which we see in the apostasies and backslidings of many, the scandalous sins and failures of some, and the course and lives of most, seem to call for a due consideration of it. Besides, it will appear as we go on how much it concerns believers to have a full and clear acquaintance with the power of this indwelling sin (the subject to be opened), to stir them up to watchfulness and diligence, to faith and prayer, and to call them to repentance, humility, and self-abasement. These in general were the ends aimed at in the discourse that follows. It was first composed and delivered for the use and benefit of a few, and is now by the providence of God made public. And if the reader receives any benefit from these weak efforts, let him know that it is his duty both to give glory to God and to help with his prayers those who, in many temptations and afflictions, are willing to labor in the vineyard of the Lord, the work to which they are called.

Chapter I

Indwelling sin in believers, treated by the apostle in Rom 7:21. The place explained.

IT is indwelling sin that we intend to treat, and that in its remainders in persons after their conversion to God, with its power, efficacy, and effects. This is also the great aim of the apostle in chapter 7 of the Epistle to the Romans: to show and prove it. There are indeed many disputes about the apostle’s main purpose in that chapter, and about the state of the person whose condition he describes there, whether under the law or under grace. I will not enter into that dispute at present. I will take for granted what may be undeniably proved and shown, namely, that what is set out there is the condition of a regenerate person with respect to the remaining power of indwelling sin, and that it is illustrated by, and in, the person of the apostle himself. In that discourse of his, therefore, the foundation will be laid for what we have to offer on this subject. Not that I will go through an exposition of his revelation of this truth as it lies in its own context; I will only make use of what he delivers, as occasion offers. And here the first thing we meet is what he affirms in verse 21: “So I find this law: when I want to do good, evil is present with me.”

There are four things to be observed in these words.

1. First, the name he gives to indwelling sin, by which he expresses its power and efficacy: it is a law. For what he calls a law in this verse he calls in the verse before “sin that dwells in him.”

2. Secondly, the way by which he came to discover this law. He discovered it not absolutely and in its own nature, but in himself: he found it. “I find this law.”

3. Thirdly, the frame of his soul and inner man along with this law of sin, and under the discovery of it: “he wants to do good.”

4. Fourthly, the state and activity of this law when the soul is in that frame, when it wants to do good: “it is present with him.” For what ends and purposes we will show later.

The first thing to be observed is the name used here by the apostle: he calls indwelling sin a law. It is a law.

A law is taken either strictly, for a directive rule, or loosely, for an operative, effective principle which seems to have the force of a law. In its first sense it is a moral rule, which directs and commands, and in various ways moves and governs the mind and the will as to the things it requires or forbids. This is plainly the general nature and work of a law. It commands some things and forbids others, with rewards and penalties, which move and drive men to do the one and avoid the other. From this, in a secondary sense, an inward principle that moves and inclines constantly to any actions is called a law. The principle that is in the nature of every thing, moving and carrying it toward its own end and rest, is called the law of nature. In this respect every inward principle that inclines and urges to workings or actions suited to itself is a law. So in Rom 8:2 the powerful and effectual working of the Spirit and grace of Christ in the hearts of believers is called the law of the Spirit of life. And for this reason the apostle here calls indwelling sin a law. It is a powerful and effectual indwelling principle, inclining and pressing to actions agreeable and suited to its own nature. This, and nothing else, is the apostle’s meaning in this expression. The term “a law” may sometimes mean a state and condition. If it were used so here, the meaning of the words would be: “I find that this is my condition, this is how things stand with me,” that when I want to do good, evil is present with me. That makes no great change in the main meaning of the place. Yet strictly it can mean nothing here but the chief subject being treated. For although the apostle uses the name of a law in various ways in this chapter, yet when it relates to sin he nowhere applies it to the condition of the person, but only to express either the nature or the power of sin itself. So in verse 23: “But I see another law in my members, warring against the law of my mind and holding me captive to the law of sin that dwells within me.” What he here calls the law of his mind, from its chief subject and seat, is in itself nothing other than the law of the Spirit of life which is in Christ Jesus (Rom 8:2), or the effectual power of the Spirit of grace, as was said. But the law, as applied to sin, has a double sense. In the first place, “I see a law in my members,” it means the being and nature of sin. In the latter, “holding me captive to the law of sin that dwells within me,” it means its power and efficacy. And both of these are included in the same name used alone in verse 20. Now what we observe from this name or term of a law given to sin is this: There is an exceeding efficacy and power in the remainders of indwelling sin in believers, with a constant working toward evil.

So it is in believers. It is a law even in them, though not to them. Though its rule is broken, its strength weakened and impaired, and its root mortified, yet it is still a law of great force and efficacy. Where it is least felt, it is most powerful. Carnal men, with respect to spiritual and moral duties, are nothing but this law; they do nothing except from it and by it. In them it is a ruling and prevailing principle of all moral actions with respect to a supernatural and eternal end. I will not consider it in those in whom it has most power, but in those in whom its power is chiefly revealed and discerned, that is, in believers; in the others only so far as it serves the further conviction and display of it.

2. Secondly, the apostle sets out the way by which he discovered this law in himself: εὑρίσκω ἄρα τὸν νόμον, “I find then,” or therefore, a law. He found it. He had been told that there was such a law; it had been preached to him. This convinced him that there was a law of sin. But it is one thing for a man to know in general that there is a law of sin, and another for a man to have experience of the power of this law of sin in himself. It is preached to all, and all men who acknowledge the Scripture admit it, since it is declared there. But only a few know it in themselves; otherwise we would have more complaints about it than we have, more striving against it, and less of its fruits in the world. But this is what the apostle affirms: not that the doctrine of it had been preached to him, but that he found it by experience in himself. “I find this law”: I have experience of its power and efficacy. For a man to find his sickness, and his danger from it, by its effects is a different thing from hearing a discourse about a disease from its causes. And this experience is the great preserver of all divine truths in the soul. This is to know a thing indeed, in reality: to know it for ourselves, when we find in ourselves what we are taught from the word. From this we observe,

2. Secondly, believers have experience of the power and efficacy of indwelling sin. They find it in themselves; they find it as a law. It has an efficacy that makes itself evident to those who are alive to discern it. Those who do not find its power are under its dominion. Whoever strives against it will know and find that it is present with him and that it is powerful in him. He who swims against the stream will find it strong, though he who drifts along with it does not feel it.

3. Thirdly, the general frame of believers, in spite of the dwelling of this law of sin in them, is also expressed here: “they want to do good.” This law is present θέλοντι ἐμοὶ ποιεῖν τὸ καλόν [with me, who want to do good]. The habitual inclination of their will is toward good. This law in them is not a law to them, as it is to unbelievers. They are not wholly subject to its power, nor morally bound to its commands. Grace has the sovereignty in their souls, and grace gives them a will to good. “They want to do good,” that is, always and constantly. In 1 John 3:9, ποιεῖν ἁμαρτίαν, “to commit sin,” is to make a trade of sin, to make sinning a man’s business; and in that sense it is said that a believer does not “commit sin.” And so ποιεῖν τὸ καλόν, to do what is good, to will to do so, is to have the habitual bent and inclination of the will set on what is good, that is, morally and spiritually good, which is the proper subject being treated. From this comes our third observation:

There is in believers, and it is kept up in them through grace, a constant and ordinarily prevailing will to do good, in spite of the power and efficacy of indwelling sin to the contrary.

This distinguishes believers in their worst condition from unbelievers in their best. The will in unbelievers is under the power of the law of sin. The opposition they make to sin, whether in its root or its branches, comes from their light and their consciences; the will to sin is never taken away in them. Take away all other considerations and hindrances (which we will treat later), and they would always sin willingly. Their faint efforts to answer their accusing consciences are far from a will to do what is good. They will indeed plead that they would leave their sins if they could, and that they would gladly do better than they do. But what they mean by that expression is the working of their light and conscience, not any spiritual inclination of their wills. For where there is a will to do good, there is a choice of what is good for the sake of its own excellence, because it is desirable and suited to the soul, and therefore to be preferred before what is contrary to it. Now this is not in any unbelievers. They do not, and cannot, choose what is spiritually good in this way, nor is it so excellent or suited to any principle that is in them. They only have some desires to attain the end to which what is good leads, and to avoid the evil that the neglect of it tends to. And in many of them even these desires are, for the most part, so weak and feeble that they do not put them on any considerable effort. Witness the luxury, sloth, worldliness and complacency in which the generality of men are drowned. But in believers there is a will to do good, a habitual disposition and inclination in their wills toward what is spiritually good. And where this is, it is accompanied with corresponding effects. The will is the principle of our moral actions, and so the general course of our actions will be suited to its prevailing disposition. Good things will come from the good treasure of the heart. Nor can this disposition be shown to be in anyone except by its fruits. A will to do good without doing good is only a pretense.

4. Fourthly, there is yet another thing remaining in these words of the apostle. It arises from the relation that the presence of sin has to the time and season of duty: “When I want to do good” (he says), “evil is present with me.”

There are two things to be considered in the will to do good that is in believers.

(1.) First, there is its habitual residence in them. They always have a habitual inclination of will toward what is good. And this habitual readiness for good is always present with them, as the apostle expresses it in verse 18 of this chapter.

(2.) Secondly, there are special times and seasons for the exercise of that principle. There is a “when I want to do good”: a season in which this or that good, this or that duty, is to be performed and accomplished, in keeping with the habitual readiness and inclination of the will.

To these two, two things in indwelling sin are opposed. To the gracious principle residing in the will, inclining to what is spiritually good, it is opposed as a law, that is, a contrary principle inclining to evil, with an aversion from what is good. To the second, the actual willing of this or that particular good, to this “when I want to do good,” is opposed the presence of this law: “evil is present with me,” παράκειταί μοι τὸ κακόν. Evil is at hand and ready to oppose the actual accomplishment of the good aimed at. From this,

4. Fourthly, indwelling sin is effectually at work, rebelling and inclining to evil, when the will to do good is particularly active and inclining to obedience.

And this is the description of a man who is a believer and a sinner; for everyone who is the former is the latter also. These are the contrary principles and the contrary workings that are in him. The principles are a will to do good on the one hand, from grace, and a law of sin on the other. Their opposing actions and workings are hinted at in the words, “When I want to do good, evil is present with me.” And both are more fully expressed by the apostle in Gal 5:17: “For the flesh lusts against the Spirit, and the Spirit against the flesh. They are opposed to each other, so that I cannot do what I want.”

And here lie the springs of the whole course of our obedience. An acquaintance with these different principles and their workings is the chief part of our wisdom. In substance, next to the free grace of God in our justification by the blood of Christ, they are the only things in which the glory of God and our own souls are concerned. These are the springs of our holiness and of our sins, of our joys and troubles, of our refreshments and sorrows. It concerns all of us, then, who intend to walk with God or to glorify him in this world, to be thoroughly acquainted with these things.

And from this we may see what wisdom is required in guiding and managing our hearts and ways before God. Where the subjects of a ruler are in feuds and conflict with one another, unless great wisdom is used in governing the whole, everything in that state will quickly go to ruin. These contrary principles are in the hearts of believers, and if they do not labor to be spiritually wise, how will they be able to steer their course rightly? Many men live in the dark to themselves all their days; whatever else they know, they do not know themselves. They know their outward estates, how rich they are, and they are careful to examine the condition of their bodies as to health and sickness. But as to their inner man, and their principles with respect to God and eternity, they know little or nothing of themselves. Indeed, few labor to grow wise in this matter. Few study themselves as they ought, or are acquainted with the evil of their own hearts as they ought; and yet on this depends the whole course of their obedience, and so their eternal condition. This therefore is our wisdom, and it is a needful wisdom, if we have any intention to please God, or to avoid what is a provocation to the eyes of his glory.

We will also find in our inquiry into this what diligence and watchfulness is required for a Christian life. There is a constant enemy to it in everyone’s heart. What an enemy it is we will show later, for this is our aim: to expose him to the utmost. In the meantime we may well lament the woeful sloth and negligence that is in most people, even in professing Christians. They live and walk as though they intended to go to heaven blindfolded and asleep, as though they had no enemy to deal with. So their mistake and folly will be fully laid open as we go on.

What I will chiefly fix on from this place of the apostle, with respect to our present purpose, is what was laid down first, namely, that there is an exceeding efficacy and power in the remainder of indwelling sin in believers, with a constant inclination and working toward evil.

Awake, therefore, all of you+ in whose hearts there is anything of the ways of God. Your+ enemy is not only upon you+, as on Samson of old, but is in you+ too. He is at work by every means of force and cunning, as we will see. If you+ would not dishonor God and his gospel, nor give offense to the saints and the ways of God, nor wound your+ consciences and endanger your+ souls, nor grieve the good and Holy Spirit of God, the author of all your+ comforts; if you+ would keep your+ garments undefiled, and escape the woeful temptations and pollutions of the days in which we live; if you+ would be kept from the number of the apostates in these latter days: then awake to the consideration of this cursed enemy. It is the spring of all these and countless other evils, and also of the ruin of all the souls that perish in this world.

Chapter II

Indwelling sin a law. In what sense it is so called. What kind of law it is. An inward effective principle called a law. The power of sin shown from this.

What we have set out to consider is the power and efficacy of indwelling sin. There are many ways in which it may be shown. I will begin with the name given to it in the place mentioned before: it is a law. “I find this law,” says the apostle. It is called so because of its power and efficacy. In the same way the principle of grace in believers is called the law of the Spirit of life, as we observed before (Rom 8:2), which is “the surpassing greatness of the power of God in them” (Eph 1:19). Where there is a law, there is power.

We will therefore show both what belongs to it as a law in general, and also what is peculiar or proper to it as being such a law as we have described.

In general, two things go with every law as such.

1. First, dominion. Rom 7:1: “The law has dominion over a man as long as he lives”; κυριεύει τοῦ ἀνθρώπου, it lords it over a man. Wherever any law takes effect, κυριεύει, it has dominion. It is strictly the act of a superior, and it belongs to its nature to demand obedience by way of dominion. Now there is a twofold dominion, as there is a twofold law. There is a moral, authoritative dominion over a man, and there is a real, effective dominion in a man. The first is a property of the law of God, the latter of the law of sin. The law of sin does not have in itself a moral dominion; it has no rightful dominion or authority over any man. But it has what is equivalent to it. That is why it is said βασιλεύειν, to reign as a king (Rom 6:12), and κυριεύειν, to lord it or have dominion (verse 14), as a law in general is said to have (Rom 7:1). But because it has lost its complete dominion with respect to believers, of whom alone we speak, I will not dwell on it in this utmost extent of its power. But even in them it is still a law, though not a law to them. Yet, as was said, it is a law in them. And though it does not have a complete and, as it were, a rightful dominion over them, yet it will have a domination as to some things in them. It is still a law, and a law in them, so that all its actions are the actions of a law. That is, it acts with power, though it has lost its complete power of ruling in them. Though it is weakened, its nature is not changed. It is still a law, and therefore powerful. And as its particular workings (which we will consider later) are the ground of this name, so the term itself teaches us in general what we are to expect from it, and what efforts it will make for the dominion it has been used to.

2. Secondly, a law, as a law, has an efficacy to move those who are subject to it to the things it requires. A law has rewards and punishments that go with it. These secretly prevail on those to whom they are set out, even though the things commanded are not very desirable. And generally all laws have their efficacy on the minds of men from the rewards and punishments attached to them. Nor is this law without this spring of power: it has its rewards and punishments. The pleasures of sin are the rewards of sin, a reward that most men lose their souls to obtain. By this the law of sin contended against the law of grace in Moses (Heb 11:25, 26): “He chose to suffer oppression with God’s people rather than to experience the fleeting pleasures of sin … for he was looking ahead to the recompense of the reward.” The contest in his mind was between the law of sin and the law of grace. On the side of the law of sin, the motive by which it sought to draw him over, and by which it prevails on most men, was the reward it set before him, namely, that he would have the present enjoyment of the “pleasures of sin.” By this it contended against the reward attached to the law of grace, called the “recompense of the reward.”

By this sorry reward this law keeps the world in obedience to its commands, and experience shows us how powerful it is to influence the minds of men. It also has punishments with which it threatens those who labor to throw off its yoke. Whatever evil, trouble, or danger in the world goes with gospel obedience, and whatever hardship or violence must be done to the sensual part of our natures in a strict course of mortification, sin makes use of as if they were punishments that follow the neglect of its commands. By these it prevails on the fearful, who will have no share in eternal life (Rev 21:8). And it is hard to say by which of these, its pretended rewards or its pretended punishments, it prevails most, and in which of them its greatest strength lies. By its rewards it entices men to sins of commission, as they are called, in ways and actions that tend to satisfy its lusts. By its punishments it leads men to omit duties, a course that tends to no less ruinous an outcome than the former. By which of these the law of sin has its greatest success in and on the souls of men is not plain, and that is because they are seldom or never separated, but take effect equally on the same persons. But this is certain. By offers and promises of the pleasures of sin on the one hand, and by threats of the loss of all sensual satisfactions and the infliction of temporal evils on the other, it has an exceeding efficacy on the minds of men, often on believers themselves. Unless a man is prepared to reject the reasonings that will offer themselves from the one and from the other of these, there is no standing before the power of this law. The world falls before them every day. With what deceit and violence they are pressed and forced on the minds of men we will declare later, as well as what advantages they have to prevail on them. Look at the generality of men, and you will find them wholly at sin’s disposal by these means. Do the profits and pleasures of sin lie before them? Nothing can keep them from reaching after them. Do difficulties and hardships go with the duties of the gospel? They will have nothing to do with them. And so they are wholly given up to the rule and dominion of this law.

And this is the light we have in general into the power and efficacy of indwelling sin from the general nature of a law, which it shares.

We may next consider what kind of law in particular it is, which will further show the power of it that we are inquiring into. It is not an outward, written, commanding, directing law, but an inborn, working, driving, urging law. A law set before us is not to be compared for efficacy with a law inborn in us. Adam had a law of sin set before him in his temptation, but because he had no law of sin inborn and working in him, he might have withstood it. An inborn law must be effectual. Let us take an example from the law that is contrary to this law of sin. The law of God was at first inborn and natural to man. It was created together with his faculties, and was their rightness, both in being and in working, with respect to his end of living to God and glorifying him. So it had a special power in the whole soul to enable it to all obedience, and indeed to make all obedience easy and pleasant. Such is the power of an inborn law. And though this law, as to its rule and dominion, is now by nature cast out of the soul, yet its remaining sparks, because they are inborn, are very powerful and effectual, as the apostle declares (Rom 2:14, 15). Afterward God renews this law and writes it on tablets of stone. But what is the efficacy of this law? Now that it is external and set before men, will it enable them to perform the things it demands and requires? Not at all. God knew it would not, unless it were turned into an internal law again, that is, until it was turned from a moral outward rule into an inward real principle. Therefore, when he intends to give his law power to produce obedience in his people, God makes it internal again and plants it in the heart as it was at first (Jer 31:31, 32, 33): “I will put My law in their inward parts and inscribe it on their hearts.” This is what God fixes on, as it were on discovering that an outward law is not sufficient to lead men to obedience. The written law, he says, will not do it. Mercies and deliverances from distress will not bring it about. Trials and afflictions will not accomplish it. Then I will take another course, says the Lord. I will turn the written law into an internal living principle in their hearts, and that will have such efficacy that it will surely make them my people, and keep them so. Now such is this law of sin. It is an indwelling law. It is “sin that dwells in me” (Rom 7:17); “sin that dwells in me” (verse 20); it is “present with me” (verse 21); it is “in my members” (verse 23). Indeed, it is so far within a man that in some sense it is said to be the man himself (verse 18): “I know that nothing good dwells in me, that is, in my flesh.” The flesh, which is the seat and throne of this law, and which indeed is this law, is in some sense the man himself, as grace also is the new man. Now from this consideration of it, that it is an indwelling law inclining and moving to sin, as an inward habit or principle, it has various advantages that increase its strength and further its power. As,

1. First, it always abides in the soul; it is never absent. The apostle twice uses the expression, it dwells in me. There is its constant residence and home. If it came on the soul only at certain times, much obedience might be perfectly accomplished in its absence. Indeed, as men deal with usurping tyrants whom they intend to drive out of a city, the gates might sometimes be shut against it so that it could not return. The soul might fortify itself against it. But the soul is its home; there it dwells, and it is no wanderer. Wherever you are, whatever you are doing, this law of sin is always in you: in the best that you do, and in the worst. Men little consider what a dangerous companion is always at home with them. When they are in company or alone, by night or by day, it is all one: sin is with them. There is a live coal continually in their houses, which, if it is not watched, will set fire to them and perhaps consume them. Oh, the woeful complacency of poor souls! How little do most men think of this inborn enemy that is never away from home! How little, for the most part, does the watchfulness of any professing Christians answer to the danger of their state and condition!

2. Secondly, it is always ready to apply itself to every end and purpose that it serves. It does not only “dwell in me,” says the apostle, “but when I want to do good, it is present with me.” There is something more in that expression than mere indwelling. A lodger may live in a house and yet not always be meddling with what the master of the house has to do (to keep to the picture of indwelling used by the apostle). But with this law it is otherwise. It so dwells in us that it will be present with us in everything we do. Indeed, often when we most earnestly desire to be rid of it, it will force itself on us with most violence. “When I want to do good, it is present with me.” Would you pray? Would you hear the word? Would you give alms? Would you meditate? Would you be in any duty, exercising faith in God and love toward him? Would you work righteousness? Would you resist temptations? This troublesome, perplexing indweller will always force itself on you more or less, and be present with you, so that you cannot perfectly and completely accomplish the thing that is good, as our apostle speaks in verse 18. Sometimes men stir up, excite and provoke their lusts by listening to their temptations; and no wonder if then they find them present and active. But it will be so even when we labor with all our efforts to be free from them. This law of sin dwells in us; that is, it clings as a depraved principle to our minds in darkness and futility, to our affections in sensuality, and to our wills in a loathing of and aversion from what is good. And by some, more, or all of these it is continually forcing itself on us, in inclinations, impulses, or suggestions to evil, when we would most gladly be rid of it.

3. Thirdly, since it is an indwelling law, it applies itself to its work with great ease and readiness, like the sin that so “easily besets” us (Heb 12:1). It has great ease and readiness in applying itself to its work. It needs no doors to be opened to it; it needs no engines to work with. The soul cannot apply itself to any duty of a man except by the exercise of those faculties in which this law has its residence. Is the understanding or the mind to be applied to anything? There it is, in ignorance, darkness, futility, folly, madness. Is the will to be engaged? There it is also, in spiritual deadness, stubbornness, and the roots of obstinacy. Are the heart and affections to be set to work? There it is, in inclinations to the world and present things, and sensuality, with a proneness to every kind of defilement. So it is easy for it to work its way into all that we do, to hinder all that is good, and to further all sin and wickedness. It has an intimacy, an inwardness with the soul, and therefore it easily besets us in all that we do. It possesses the very faculties of the soul by which we must do whatever we do, good or evil. Now it has all these advantages as it is a law, an indwelling law, and they show its power and efficacy. It is always resident in the soul; it forces itself on all the soul’s actions, and that with ease and readiness.

This is the law that the apostle says he found in himself. This is the title he gives to the powerful and effectual remainders of indwelling sin even in believers. And these are the general evidences of its power that we have from that name. There are many in the world who do not find this law in themselves. Whatever they have been taught in the word, they have no spiritual sense and experience of the power of indwelling sin, and that is because they are wholly under its dominion. They do not find that there is darkness and folly in their minds, because they are darkness itself, and darkness will reveal nothing. They do not find deadness and disinclination toward God in their hearts and wills, because they are wholly dead in trespasses and sins. They are at peace with their lusts by being in bondage to them. And this is the state of most men in the world, which makes them woefully despise everything that concerns them eternally. Why is it that men follow and pursue the world with so much greed that they neglect heaven, and life, and immortality for it every day? Why is it that some pursue their sensuality with delight, and will drink, and revel, and have their amusements, let others say what they please? Why is it that so many live so unprofitably under the word, understand so little of what is spoken to them, practice still less of what they understand, and will by no means be stirred up to answer the mind of God in his calls to them? All these things come from this law of sin and its power, which rules and holds sway in men. But it is not such persons that we are particularly treating at present.

From what has been said it follows that if there is such a law in believers, it is without doubt their duty to find it out, to find that it is so.

The more they find its power, the less they will feel its effects. It will do a man no good at all to have a wasting fever and not discover it, or a fire lying hidden in his house and not know it. As much as men find of this law in themselves, so much they will abhor it and themselves, and no more. Their earnestness for grace will also be in proportion to their discovery of it, and will rise no higher. All watchfulness and diligence in obedience will also correspond to it. The whole course of our lives turns on this one hinge: finding out and experiencing the power and efficacy of this law of sin. Ignorance of it breeds senselessness, carelessness, sloth, complacency and pride, all of which the Lord’s soul abhors. Outbreaks into great, open, conscience-wasting, scandalous sins come from the lack of a due spiritual consideration of this law. Inquire, then, how it is with your+ souls. What do you+ find of this law? What experience do you+ have of its power and efficacy? Do you+ find it dwelling in you+, always present with you+, stirring itself up, or putting out its poison with ease and readiness, at all times, in all your+ duties, “when you+ want to do good”? What humiliation, what self-abasement, what intensity in prayer, what diligence, what watchfulness does this call for from you+? What spiritual wisdom do you+ need? What supplies of grace, what help of the Holy Spirit, will this also show you+ need? I fear that few of us have a diligence in proportion to our danger.

Chapter III

The seat or subject of the law of sin. The heart, and what is meant by it. Properties of the heart as possessed by sin. Unsearchable. Deceitful. Where that deceit comes from. Uses of these considerations.

Having shown that indwelling sin, which we are treating in its remainders in believers, is a law, and having shown its power in general from this, we will now go on to give particular instances of its efficacy and advantages from some things that relate to it generally as such. And these are three: first, its seat and subject; secondly, its natural properties; and thirdly, its workings and the manner of them, which is what we chiefly aim at and will attend to.

1. First, as for the seat and subject of this law of sin, the Scripture everywhere declares it to be the heart. There indwelling sin keeps its special residence. It has invaded and possessed the throne of God himself (Eccl 9:3): “the hearts of men are full of … madness while they are alive.” This is their madness, or the root of all the madness that appears in their lives (Matt 15:19): “For out of the heart come evil thoughts, murder, adultery, sexual immorality, theft, false testimony, slander,” and so on. Many outward temptations and provocations befall men which excite and stir them up to these evils. But they only, as it were, open the vessel and let out what is laid up and stored in it. The root, rise, and spring of all these things is in the heart. Temptations and occasions put nothing into a man; they only draw out what was in him before. From this comes that summary description of the whole work and effect of this law of sin in Gen 6:5: “Every inclination of the thoughts of man’s heart was altogether evil all the time”; so also in Gen 8:21. The whole work of the law of sin, from its first rise, from its first coining of actual sin, is described here, and its seat, its workhouse, is said to be the heart. And so our Savior calls it “the evil treasure of the heart” (Luke 6:45): “the evil man brings evil things out of the evil treasure of his heart.” This treasure is the prevailing principle of moral actions that is in men. So at the beginning of the verse our Savior calls grace the good treasure of the heart of a good man, from which what is good comes. It is a principle that constantly and abundantly incites and stirs men up to actions agreeable to it and like it, of the same kind and nature as itself, and so brings them forth. And it is also called a treasure because of its abundance. It will never be exhausted; it is not used up by men’s spending from it. Indeed, the more lavish men are with this stock, the more they draw out of this treasure, the more it grows and abounds. As men do not spend their grace but increase it by its exercise, no more do they spend their indwelling sin. The more men exercise their grace in duties of obedience, the more it is strengthened and increased. And the more men put out and bring forth the fruits of their lust, the more that lust is enraged and increased in them. It feeds on itself, swallows its own poison, and grows by it. The more men sin, the more they are inclined to sin. It is from the deceitfulness of this law of sin, which we will speak of at length later, that men persuade themselves that by this or that particular sin they will so satisfy their lusts that they will not need to sin any more. Every sin increases the principle and strengthens the habit of sinning. It is an evil treasure that increases by doing evil. And where does this treasure lie? It is in the heart; there it is laid up, there it is kept safe. All the men in the world and all the angels in heaven cannot take this treasure out of a man’s possession, it is stored so safely in the heart.

The heart is used in various ways in the Scripture: sometimes for the mind and understanding, sometimes for the will, sometimes for the affections, sometimes for the conscience, sometimes for the whole soul. Generally it means the whole soul of man and all its faculties, not absolutely, but as they are all one principle of moral working, as they all work together in our doing good or evil. The mind as it inquires, discerns, and judges what is to be done and what refused; the will as it chooses or refuses and avoids; the affections as they like or dislike, cling to or turn away in aversion from what is set before them; the conscience as it warns and decides: all these together are called the heart. And it is in this sense that we say the seat and subject of this law of sin is the heart of man. Only we may add that when the Scripture speaks of the heart as the principle of men’s good or evil actions, it usually hints along with it at two things belonging to the manner in which they are performed.

1. First, a fitness and pleasingness to the soul in the things that are done. When men take delight and are pleased in and with what they do, they are said to do it heartily, with their whole hearts. So when God himself blesses his people in love and delight, he says that he does it “with all My heart and with all My soul” (Jer 32:41).

2. Secondly, resolution and constancy in such actions. This also is meant by the metaphorical expression used before, a treasure, from which men constantly take out the things they either need or intend to use.

This is the subject, the seat, the dwelling place of the law of sin: the heart, as it is the entire principle of moral workings, of doing good or evil, as good or evil come out of it. Here our enemy dwells. This is the fort, the citadel of this tyrant, where it maintains a rebellion against God all our days. Sometimes it has more strength, and so more success; sometimes less of the one and of the other; but it is always in rebellion while we live.

So that we may take a brief look in passing at the strength and power of sin from this seat and subject of it, we may consider one or two properties of the heart that contribute greatly to it. It is like an enemy in war, whose strength and power lie not only in his numbers and the force of his men or arms, but also in the unconquerable forts he possesses. And such is the heart to this enemy of God and of our souls, as will appear from its properties, one or two of which will be mentioned.

1. First, it is unsearchable. Jer 17:9, 10: “Who can know it? I, the Lord, search the heart.” The heart of man is open to God alone. That is why he claims the honor of searching the heart as belonging to him alone, and as fully declaring him to be God as any other glorious attribute of his nature. We do not know one another’s hearts; we do not know our own hearts as we ought. There are many who do not know their hearts even as to their general bent and disposition, whether it is good or bad, sincere and sound, or corrupt and worthless. But no one knows all the secret intrigues, the windings and turnings, the acts and aversions of his own heart. Does anyone have the perfect measure of his own light and darkness? Can anyone know what acts of choice or aversion his will will bring forth when it is presented with the endless variety of objects it has to deal with? Can anyone trace all the changes of his affections? Do the secret springs of acting and refusing in the soul lie open before the eyes of any man? Does anyone know what the stirrings of his mind or will will be in this or that combination of things, with objects suited in such a way, such reasonings put forward, such an appearance of desirable things? All in heaven and earth except the infinite, all-seeing God are utterly ignorant of these things. In this unsearchable heart the law of sin dwells. And much of its security, and so of its strength, lies in this: it is past our finding out. We fight with an enemy whose secret strength we cannot discover, and whom we cannot follow into its hiding places. That is why we often find, when we are ready to think sin quite ruined, that after a while it was only out of sight. It has coverts and retreats in an unsearchable heart where we cannot pursue it. The soul may persuade itself that all is well, when sin may be safe in the hidden darkness of the mind, which it is impossible for the man to look into, for whatever makes things manifest is light. It may suppose that the will to sin is utterly taken away, when there is still an unsearchable reserve kept for a more suitable object, a more vigorous temptation than the one it is tried with at present. Has a man had a contest with some lust, and a blessed victory over it by the Holy Spirit in that present trial? When he thinks it is utterly driven out, he soon finds that it had only withdrawn out of sight. It can lie so hidden in the darkness of the mind, in the disinclination of the will, in the disorder and carnality of the affections, that no eye can discover it. The best of our wisdom is only to watch its first appearances, to catch its first heavings under the earth and workings, and to set ourselves against them; for we cannot follow it into the secret corners of the heart. It is true that there is still a relief in this case. He to whom the work of destroying the law of sin and the body of death in us is chiefly committed, namely the Holy Spirit, comes with his axe to the very root, and there is nothing in an unsearchable heart that is not open and naked to him (Heb 4:12). But from this we may see, by way of duty, what an enemy we have to deal with.

2. Secondly, as it is unsearchable, so it is deceitful, as in the place mentioned above: it is “deceitful above all things,” beyond all comparison. There is great deceit in the dealings of men in the world: great deceit in their counsels and schemes concerning their affairs, private and public, and great deceit in their words and actions. The world is full of deceit and fraud. But all this is nothing to the deceit that is in man’s heart toward himself; for that is the meaning of the expression in this place, and not deceit toward others. Now incomparable deceitfulness, added to unsearchableness, greatly adds to and increases the strength of the law of sin because of its seat and subject. I am not yet speaking of the deceitfulness of sin itself, but of the deceitfulness of the heart where it is seated. Prov 26:25: “seven abominations fill his heart”; that is, not only many, but an absolute, complete number, as seven signifies. And they are abominations that consist in deceitfulness, as the caution before it hints: “do not trust him.” For it is only deceit that should keep us from trusting a thing as far as its nature allows.

Now this deceitfulness of the heart, by which it is greatly helped in harboring sin, lies chiefly in these two things.

(1.) First, it abounds in contradictions, so that it cannot be found and dealt with by any constant rule and way of proceeding. There are some men who have much of this from their natural constitution, or from other causes in their way of life. They seem to be made up of contradictions. At times they are very wise in their affairs, at times very foolish; very open, and very reserved; very pliable, and very obstinate; very easy to be entreated, and very revengeful; all to a remarkable degree. This is generally thought a bad character, and it is seldom found except where it comes from some notable predominant lust. But in general, with respect to moral good or evil, duty or sin, it is so with the heart of every man: flaming hot and stone cold; weak, and yet stubborn; obstinate, and pliable. The frame of the heart is ready to contradict itself every moment. Now you would think you had it fixed in such a frame, such a way; soon it is quite otherwise, so that no one knows what to expect from it. The rise of this is the disorder that sin has brought on all its faculties. God created them all in a perfect harmony and union. The mind and reason were in perfect subjection and subordination to God and his will. The will, in its choice of good, answered to what the mind showed of it. The affections constantly and evenly followed the understanding and will. The mind’s subjection to God was the spring of the orderly and harmonious motion of the soul and all the wheels in it. When that was disturbed by sin, the rest of the faculties moved across and against one another. The will does not choose the good that the mind shows it; the affections do not delight in what the will chooses; but all of them jar and clash, cross and rebel against each other. This is what we have gained by our falling from God. So at times the will leads and the judgment follows. Indeed, commonly the affections, which should wait on all the rest, get the sovereignty and draw the whole soul captive after them. And that is why, as I said, the heart is made up of so many contradictions in its actions. At times the mind keeps its sovereignty, the affections are in subjection, and the will is ready for its duty. This puts a good face on things. Immediately the rebellion of the affections or the obstinacy of the will takes over and prevails, and the whole scene is changed. This, I say, makes the heart deceitful above all things. It does not agree with itself at all; it is not constant to itself; it has no order that it keeps to; it is under no certain and stable direction. Rather, if I may put it so, it turns round on itself, where often the feet lead and guide the whole.

(2.) Secondly, its deceit lies in its large promises at the first appearance of things. And this also comes from the same principle as the former. Sometimes the affections are touched and worked on, the whole heart appears in a good frame, and everything promises to be well. In a little while the whole frame is changed. The mind was not affected or turned at all; the affections played their part a little and then left, and all the fair promises of the heart left with them. Now add this deceitfulness to the unsearchableness mentioned before, and we will find that the difficulty of dealing effectually with sin in its seat and throne, to say the least, is greatly increased. A deceiving and deceived heart: who can deal with it? Especially when we consider that the heart employs all its deceits in the service of sin and contributes them all to its furtherance. All the disorder in the heart, all its false promises and fair appearances, promote the interest and advantages of sin. That is why God warns the people to take care, lest “their own hearts should entice and deceive them.”

Who can name all the treacheries and deceits that lie in the heart of man? It is not for nothing that the Holy Spirit expresses it so: “It is deceitful above all things”: uncertain in what it does, and false in what it promises. And this, moreover, among other causes, is why in carrying on our war against sin we not only have the old work to go over and over, but always have new work while we live in this world: ever new stratagems and tricks to deal with, as it will be wherever unsearchableness and deceitfulness have to be contended with.

There are many other properties of this seat and subject of the law of sin which might be dwelt on to the same end and purpose, but that would turn us too far aside from our particular aim. So I will pass these over with a few considerations.

Let us never reckon that our work of contending against sin, of crucifying, mortifying, and subduing it, is at an end. The place where it lives is unsearchable. When we may think we have thoroughly won the field, there is still some reserve remaining that we did not see, that we did not know of. Many conquerors have been ruined by their carelessness after a victory, and many have been spiritually wounded after great successes against this enemy. So was David. His great fall into sin, when he was taken unawares, came after a long profession of faith, many experiences of God, and a watchful keeping of himself from his iniquity. And this is partly why the faith that many professed has declined in their old age or riper years, which must be spoken of more particularly later. They gave up the work of mortifying sin before their work was at an end. There is no way for us to pursue sin in its unsearchable dwelling place except by never ending our pursuit. And on this account the command of the apostle in Col 3:5 is as necessary for those who are near the end of their race to obey as for those who are only at the beginning of it: “Put to death, therefore, the components of your+ earthly nature.” Be always doing it while you+ live in this world. It is true that great ground is gained when the work is carried on vigorously and constantly. Sin is much weakened, so that the soul presses on toward perfection. But still the work must be endless, I mean while we are in this world. If we give up, we will quickly see this enemy putting itself forth with new strength and vigor. Perhaps under some great affliction, perhaps in some eminent enjoyment of God, in the sense of the sweetness of blessed communion with Christ, we have been ready to say that there was an end of sin, that it was dead and gone forever. But have we not found the contrary by experience? Has it not shown that it had only withdrawn into some unsearchable recesses of the heart, as to its being in us and its nature, though it may be greatly weakened in its power? Let us reckon on it, then, that there is no way to have our work done except by always doing it. And he who dies fighting in this warfare surely dies a conqueror.

2. Secondly, since it has its residence in what is changeable, inconstant, deceitful above all things, this calls for constant watchfulness against it. An open enemy who deals by violence only always gives some respite. You know where to find him and what he is doing, so that at times you may sleep quietly without fear. But against adversaries who deal by deceit and treachery (which are long swords, and reach at the greatest distance), nothing will give security except constant watchfulness. In this case it is impossible for us to be too vigilant, wary, suspicious, or watchful. The heart has a thousand tricks and deceits, and if we are off our watch in the least, we may be sure to be taken by surprise. That is why commands and warnings are given again and again for watching, for being circumspect, diligent, careful, and the like. There is no living for those who have to deal with an enemy “deceitful above all things” unless they persist in such a frame. All the cautions given in this case are necessary, especially this one: “remember not to believe.” Does the heart make fair promises? Do not rest on it, but say to the Lord Christ, “Lord, undertake for me.” Does the sun shine fair in the morning? Do not count on a fair day because of it; the clouds may rise and fall. Though the morning gives a fair appearance of calm and peace, turbulent affections may rise and cloud the soul with sin and darkness.

3. Thirdly, then, commit the whole matter with all care and diligence to him who can search the heart to the uttermost and knows how to forestall all its treacheries and deceits. In the things mentioned before lies our duty, but here lies our safety. There is no treacherous corner in our hearts that he cannot search to the uttermost; there is no deceit in them that he cannot defeat. David takes this course in Ps 139. After he has set out the omnipresence of God and his omniscience (verses 8, 9, 10), he makes use of it in verse 23: “Search me, O God, … test me.” As if he had said, “I know only a little of my deceitful heart. Only I want to be sincere; I would not have any reserves for sin kept in it. So you who are present with my heart, who know my thoughts long before, undertake this work and perform it thoroughly, for you alone are able to do so.”

There are still other arguments that show the power and strength of indwelling sin, for which it is called a law, and we must go through them in the order in which we laid them down before.

Chapter IV

Indwelling sin is enmity against God. Its power shown from this. It admits of no peace or rest. It is against God himself. It shows itself in aversion from God and in a bent toward evil. It is universal: to all of God, in all of the soul. It is constant.

WE have seen the seat and subject of this law of sin. Next we might take a view of its nature in general, which will also show its power and efficacy. But I will not enlarge on this, since it is not my business to declare the nature of indwelling sin, and others have also done it. So, with respect only to the particular aim we have in hand, I will consider one property of it that belongs to its nature, and that always, wherever it is. This is what the apostle expresses in Rom 8:7: “the mind of the flesh is enmity against God.” What is called here φρόνημα τῆς σαρκός, “the wisdom of the flesh,” is the same as the law of sin that we are dwelling on. And what does he say of it? Why, it is ἔχθρα εἰς Θεόν, “enmity against God.” It is not only an enemy, for then some reconciliation of it to God might possibly be made; it is enmity itself, and so is not capable of accepting any terms of peace. Enemies may be reconciled, but enmity cannot. Indeed, the only way to reconcile enemies is to destroy the enmity. So the apostle tells us in another case (Rom 5:10): “when we were enemies of God, we were reconciled to Him.” That is a work achieved and brought about by the blood of Christ: the reconciling of the greatest enemies. But when he comes to speak of enmity, there is no way with it except that it must be abolished and destroyed (Eph 2:15): “by abolishing in His flesh the enmity.” There is no way to deal with any enmity whatever except by abolishing or destroying it.

And this also lies in it as it is enmity: every part and particle of it, if we may speak so, the least degree of it that can possibly remain in anyone, while and where there is anything of its nature, is still enmity. It may not be as effectual and powerful in working as where it has more life and vigor, but it is still enmity. As every drop of poison is poison and will infect, and every spark of fire is fire and will burn, so it is with everything of the law of sin. The last of it, the least of it, is enmity; it will poison, it will burn. What is anything in the abstract remains so while it has any being at all. Our apostle, who may well be supposed to have made as great progress in subduing it as anyone on earth, still, after all, cries out for deliverance as from an irreconcilable enemy (Rom 7:24). Its lowest action, its least and most imperceptible working, is the action and working of enmity. Mortification lessens its force, but does not change its nature. Grace changes the nature of man, but nothing can change the nature of sin. Whatever effect is worked on it, no effect is worked in it except that it is still enmity, still sin. This, then, is our state and condition because of it. “God is love” (1 John 4:8). He is so in himself, eternally excellent and desirable above all. He is so to us; he is so in the blood of his Son and in all its inexpressible fruits, by which we are what we are, and in which all our future hopes and expectations are wrapped up. Against this God we carry an enmity about with us all our days: an enmity that has this from its nature, that it is incapable of cure or reconciliation. Destroyed it may be, and it will be, but cured it cannot be. If a man has an enemy to deal with who is too mighty for him, as David had with Saul, he may take the course David took. He may consider what it is that has provoked his enemy against him, and so set about removing the cause and making his peace (1 Sam 26:19): “If the Lord has stirred you up against me, then may He accept an offering. But if men have done it, may they be cursed in the presence of the Lord!” Whether it comes from God or from man, there is still hope of peace. But when a man has enmity itself to deal with, nothing is to be expected except continual fighting until one party is destroyed. If it is not overcome and destroyed, it will overcome and destroy the soul.

And in this lies no small part of the power we are inquiring into: it can admit of no terms of peace, no settlement. There may be a settlement where there is no reconciliation; there may be a truce where there is no peace. But with this enemy we can obtain neither the one nor the other. It is never quiet, whether conquering or conquered, which was the only kind of enemy the famous warrior of old complained of. It is vain for a man to expect any rest from his lust except by its death, or absolute freedom except by his own. Some, when their corruptions are in an uproar, seek quiet by laboring to satisfy them, “making provision for the flesh to fulfill its lusts,” as the apostle speaks (Rom 13:14). This is to put out a fire with wood and oil. If all the fuel in the world, all the combustible fabric of the creation, were thrown into the fire, it would not satisfy it at all but increase it. So it is with the satisfaction given to sin by sinning: it only inflames and increases it. If a man will part with some of his goods to an enemy, that may satisfy him. But enmity will have everything, and is not one bit more satisfied than if it had received nothing at all, like the thin cows that were no less hungry for having devoured the fat ones. You+ cannot bargain with the fire to take only so much of your+ houses; you+ have no way except to put it out. In this case it is as it is in the contest between a wise man and a fool (Prov 29:9): “there will be raving and laughing with no rest.” Whatever frame or mood the fool is in, his persistent folly makes him troublesome. So it is with this indwelling sin. Whether it rages or laughs, whether it is in a violent uproar (as it will be on provocations and temptations, when it will rage in the soul) or whether it seems pleased and content, satisfied, it is all one: there is no peace, no rest to be had with it or by it. If it had been of any other nature, some other way might have been fixed on. But since it consists in enmity, all the relief the soul has must lie in its ruin.

2. Secondly, it is not only said to be enmity; it is said to be enmity against God. It has chosen a great enemy indeed. In various places it is set out as our enemy (1 Pet 2:11): “abstain from fleshly lusts, which war against the soul.” They are enemies to the soul, that is, to ourselves. At times it is set out as an enemy to the Spirit that is in us: “the flesh lusts against the Spirit,” or fights against it (Gal 5:17). It fights against the Spirit, or the spiritual principle that is in us, to conquer it; it fights against our souls to destroy them. It has special ends and designs against our souls and against the principle of grace that is in us. But its true and essential object is God: it is enmity against God. It is its work to oppose grace. To oppose our souls is a consequence of its work, which follows from what it does more than from what it intends. But its nature and essential design is to oppose God. God as the lawgiver, God as holy, God as the author of the gospel, a way of salvation by grace and not by works: this is the direct object of the law of sin. Why does it oppose duty, “so that the good we want to do, we do not do,” either as to its matter or its manner? Why does it make the soul carnal, disinclined, unbelieving, unspiritual, weary, wandering? It is because of its enmity to God, with whom the soul aims to have communion in duty. It has, as it were, the command from Satan that the Assyrians had from their king: “Do not fight with anyone, small or great, except the king of Israel” (1 Kgs 22:31). It is neither great nor small, but God himself, the King of Israel, whom sin sets itself against. There lies the secret essential reason for all its opposition to good: because good relates to God. Suppose a road, a routine, a way of duties is set up in which communion with God is not aimed at, but only the duty itself, as is the way of men in most of their superstitious worship. Then the opposition the law of sin raises against it will be very weak, easy and gentle. It is like the Assyrians, who attacked Jehoshaphat because he looked like a king, but turned back from pursuing him when they found that he was not Ahab. Because there is a show and appearance of the worship of God, sin may rise against it at first. But when the duty cries out in the heart that God is not really there, sin turns away to seek its true enemy, God himself, elsewhere. That is why many poor creatures spend their days in grim, wearying superstitions without any great resistance from within, while others cannot be allowed to watch with Christ freely in a spiritual manner for one hour. And it is no wonder that men fight with carnal weapons for their superstitious worship outside them, when they have no fighting against it within. For God is not in it, and the law of sin makes no opposition to any duty, but to God in every duty. This is our state and condition. All the opposition that arises in us to anything spiritually good, whether it comes from darkness in the mind, or aversion in the will, or sloth in the affections, and all the secret arguings and reasonings in the soul that go along with them, have God himself as their direct object. The enmity lies against him. Surely this consideration should move us to a constant, unbroken watchfulness over ourselves.

It is so also with respect to every inclination to sin, as well as to aversion from God. It is God himself who is aimed at. It is true that the pleasures, the wages of sin, greatly influence the sensual, carnal affections of men. But it is the holiness and authority of God that sin itself rises up against. It hates the yoke of the Lord. “You have grown weary of Me,” God says to sinners, and that while they are performing a great many duties. Every act of sin is a fruit of being weary of God. So Job tells us what lies at the bottom of the hearts of sinners: “they say to God: ‘Depart from us!’” It is enmity against him and aversion from him. Here lies the very essence of every sin. It is an opposition to God, a throwing off of his yoke, a breaking off of the dependence the creature ought to have on the Creator. And in Rom 8:7 the apostle gives the reason why he affirms “the mind of the flesh to be enmity against God”: namely, because “it does not submit to God’s law, nor can it do so.” It never submits to God, nor will, nor can; its whole nature consists in opposition to him. The soul in which it is may be subject to the law of God, but this law of sin sets itself up against that law and will not be in subjection.

To show a little further the power of this law of sin from this property of its nature, that it is enmity against God, we may consider one or two things that are inseparable from it, which will further prove it.

1. First, it is universal. Some contentions are limited to particular concerns: this one is about one thing, that one about another. It is not so here. The enmity is absolute and universal, as are all enmities that are grounded in the nature of the things themselves. Such enmity is against the whole kind of thing that is its object. And this enmity is such, for, first, it is universal to all of God; and secondly, it is universal in all of the soul.

(1.) First, it is universal to all of God. Suppose there were anything of God (his nature, his properties, his mind or will, his law or gospel, any duty of obedience to him or of communion with him) that sin had no enmity against. Then the soul might have a constant shelter and retreat within itself, by turning to that in God, to that duty toward him, to that communion with him, which sin would make no opposition to. But the enmity lies against God, and all of God, and everything in which or by which we have to do with him. It is not subject to the law, nor to any part or particle, word or tittle of the law. Whatever is opposed to anything as such is opposed to all of it. Sin is enmity to God as God, and therefore to all of God. Not his goodness, not his holiness, not his mercy, not his grace, not his promises: there is nothing of him that it does not rise against, and no duty, private or public, in the heart or in external works, that it does not oppose. And the nearer (if I may say so) anything is to God, the greater is sin’s enmity to it. The more spirituality and holiness there is in anything, the greater its enmity. Whatever has most of God has most of its opposition. Of those in whom this law is predominant God says, “you+ neglected all my counsel, and wanted none of my correction” (Prov 1:25). It is not this or that part of God’s counsel, his mind or will, that is opposed, but “all his counsel.” Whatever he calls for or guides to, in every particular of it, is all neglected, and none of his correction is attended to. A man would not think it very strange that sin should maintain an enmity against God in his law, which comes to judge it and condemn it. But it raises a greater enmity against him in his gospel, in which he offers mercy and pardon as a deliverance from it. And that is only because more of the glorious properties of God’s nature, more of his excellencies and his gracious stooping to us, are shown in the gospel than in the law.

(2.) Secondly, it is universal in all of the soul. If this law of sin had been content to subdue any one faculty of the soul, if it had left any one at liberty, any one affection free from its yoke and bondage, it might possibly have been opposed or subdued with more ease. But when Christ comes with his spiritual power on the soul to conquer it for himself, he has no quiet landing place. He can set foot on no ground that he does not have to fight for and conquer. Not the mind, not an affection, not the will: all is held against him. And when grace has made its entrance, sin will still dwell in all its borders. If anything in the soul were in perfect freedom and liberty, a stand might be made there to drive sin from all the rest of its strongholds. But it is universal, and wars in the whole soul. The mind has its own darkness and futility to wrestle with; the will its own stubbornness, obstinacy and perverseness; every affection its own contrariness and aversion from God, and its sensuality, to deal with. So one cannot give relief to another as they ought; they have, as it were, their hands full at home. That is why our knowledge is imperfect, our obedience weak, our love not unmixed, our fear not pure, our delight not free and noble. But I must not dwell on these particulars, or I could show at length how this principle of enmity against God is spread through the whole soul.

2. Secondly, to this might be added its constancy. It is constant to itself; it does not waver; it has no thought of yielding or giving up, in spite of the powerful opposition made to it by both the law and the gospel, as will be shown later.

This, then, is a third evidence of the power of sin, taken from its nature and properties. To illustrate it I have fixed on only one instance, namely, that it is enmity against God, and that universal and constant. If we were to enter on a full description of it, it would require more space and time than we have allotted to this whole subject. What has been said might give us a little sense of it, if it is the will of God, and stir us up to watchfulness. What could be a sadder thing to consider than that we should constantly carry about with us what is enmity against God, and that not in this or that particular, but in all that he is and in all in which he has revealed himself? I cannot say it is well with those who do not find it. It is well indeed with those in whom it is weakened and its power lessened. But as for those who say it “is not in them, they only deceive themselves, and the truth is not in them.”

Chapter V

The nature of sin further laid open as it is enmity against God. Its aversion from all good explained. Means prescribed to prevent its effects.

WE have considered something of the nature of indwelling sin, not absolutely, but with a view to showing its power. But its power shows itself more clearly in its actions and workings. Power is an act of life, and working is the only thing that reveals life. We do not know that anything lives except by the effects and works of life. And great and strong workings reveal a powerful and vigorous life. Such are the workings of this law of sin, and they are all demonstrations of its power.

What we have declared about its nature is that it consists in enmity. Now there are two general heads of the working or operation of enmity: first, aversion; secondly, opposition.

1. First, aversion. Our Savior, describing by its effects the enmity that was between himself and the teachers of the Jews, says in the prophet, “My soul loathed them, and their souls also abhorred me” (Zech 11:8). Where there is mutual enmity, there is mutual aversion, loathing, and abhorrence. So it was between the Jews and the Samaritans: they were enemies and abhorred one another, as in John 4:9.

2. Secondly, opposition, or contending against one another, is the next product of enmity. Isa 63:10: “So He turned and became their enemy, and He Himself fought against them,” speaking of God toward the people. Where there is enmity, there will be fighting; it is its proper and natural product. Now both these effects are found in this law of sin.

(1.) First, as for aversion, there is an aversion in it to God and to everything of God, as we have partly shown in handling the enmity itself, so we will not need to dwell on it much again. All disinclination to duty in which communion with God is to be obtained, all weariness of duty, all carnality or formality in duty, springs from this root. The wise man warns us against this evil in Eccl 5:1: “Guard your steps when you go to the house of God.” Do you have some spiritual duty to perform, and do you aim at attaining some communion with God? Look to yourself; take care of your affections. They will be roaming and wandering, and that from their aversion to what you have in hand. There is no good that we want to do in which we may not find this aversion at work. “When I want to do good, evil is present with me”: at any time, at all times, when I want to do anything spiritually good, it is present. That is, it is present to hinder me, to obstruct me in my duty; because it abhors and loathes the thing I have in hand, it will keep me from it if it possibly can. In those in whom it prevails, it comes at length to the frame described in Ezek 33:31. It will allow an outward bodily presence at the worship of God, in which it has no concern, but it keeps the heart entirely away.

Perhaps some will claim that they do not find it so in themselves, but that they have freedom and liberty in and toward all the duties of obedience they attend to. But I fear that on examination this claimed liberty will be found to arise from one or both of these causes. First, ignorance of the true state and condition of their own souls, of their inner man and its actions toward God. They do not know how it is with them, and so they are not to be believed in what they report. They are in the dark, and know neither what they are doing nor where they are going. It is likely that the Pharisee knew little of this matter, which made him boast of his duties to God himself. Or, secondly, perhaps whatever duties of worship or obedience such persons perform, they may have no communion with God in them, through lack of faith and of a share in Christ. And if so, sin will make only a little opposition to them in those duties. We are speaking of those whose hearts are wrestling with these things. And if, while they complain of them and groan for deliverance from them, others cry out to them, “Keep to yourselves+; … we are holier than you+,” they are willing to bear their condition. They know that their way may be safe, though it is troublesome, and they are willing to see their own dangers, so that they may avoid the ruin others fall into.

Let us, then, consider this aversion a little in those acts of obedience in which nothing is concerned but God and the soul. In public duties there may be a mixture of other considerations. They may be so influenced by custom and necessity that a right judgment of this matter cannot be made from them. But let us take into consideration the duties of solitude, such as private prayer and meditation and the like, or else extraordinary duties, or duties to be performed in an extraordinary manner.

1. First, in these this aversion and loathing will often show itself in the affections. There will be a secret struggle in them about close and heartfelt dealing with God. Conviction of sin, the sense of duty, a dear and real esteem for God and for communion with him, may have carried the soul into its private room. Yet unless the hand of God by his Spirit is high and strong on the soul, if the vigor and power of a spiritual life is not constantly at work, there will be a secret reluctance in the affections toward duty. Indeed, at times there will be a violent inclination to the contrary, so that the soul would rather do anything, would welcome any distraction, though it wounds itself by it, than vigorously apply itself to what it longs for in the inner man. It is weary before it begins, and says, “When will the work be over?” Here God and the soul are directly concerned, and it is a great conquest to do what we want to do, though we come far short of what we ought to do. Secondly, it shows itself in the mind also. When we come to God in Christ, we are, as Job speaks, “to fill our mouths with arguments” (Job 23:4), so that we may be able to plead with him, as he calls on us to do (Isa 43:26): “Remind Me, let us plead the matter together.” That is why the church is called on to take words or arguments with it in going to God (Hos 14:2). The sum is that the mind should be furnished with the considerations that prevail with God, and be ready to plead them, and to manage them in the most spiritual manner to the best advantage. Now is there no difficulty in getting the mind into such a frame that it spends itself to the utmost in this work? That it is clear, steady, and constant in its duty? That it draws out and makes use of its stores and supply of promises and experiences? It starts aside, wanders, flags, all from this secret aversion to communion with God, which comes from the law of indwelling sin. Some complain that they can make nothing of meditation; they cannot bend their minds to it. I admit that a great cause of this may be their lack of a right understanding of the duty itself and of the ways of managing the soul in it, and so I will speak to that a little later.
