But still this secret enmity also has its hand in the difficulty they are at, both in their minds and in their affections. Others find so little benefit and success in private duties that they are forced to live on family and public duties. And here has been the beginning of the apostasy of many professing Christians, and the source of many foolish, carnal opinions. They found this aversion in their minds and affections from closeness and constancy in private spiritual duties. Not knowing how to conquer and prevail against these difficulties through “him who gives us strength,” they were at first overcome so far as to neglect them, first partly, then wholly. At last, having lost all conscience about them, they had a door opened to all sin and licentiousness, and so to a full and utter apostasy. I am persuaded that there are very few who fall away from a profession of faith of any long standing (and our days abound with such) whose door of entrance into the folly of backsliding was not one of two things. Either it was some great and notorious sin that bloodied their consciences, tainted their affections, and cut off all delight in having anything more to do with God; or else it was a course of neglect of private duties, arising from weariness of contending against that powerful aversion to them that they found in themselves. And through the craft of Satan this too has been turned into many foolish and carnal opinions about living to God without, and above, any duties of communion. And we find that after men have choked and blinded their consciences with this pretense for a while, cursed wickedness or sensuality has been the end of their folly. And the reason for all this is that to give way to the law of sin in the least is to give it strength. To let it alone is to let it grow; not to conquer it is to be conquered by it.

As it is with private duties, so it is also with public duties that have anything extraordinary in them. What strivings, struggles, and pleadings there are in the heart about them, especially against their spirituality! Indeed, in and during them, will not the mind and affections at times be entangled with things odd, new and strange to them, things a man would not stoop to take into his thoughts at a time of the least serious business? But if the least looseness, liberty or advantage is given to indwelling sin, if it is not perpetually watched, it will work toward a strange and unexpected outcome. In brief, let the soul strip any duty whatever, private or public, anything that is called good, of all the outward considerations that secretly work their way into the mind. These give the mind some satisfaction in what it is doing, but do not make the duty acceptable to God. Let a man take them away, and he will surely find something of the power and some of the effects of this aversion. It begins in reluctance and disinclination, goes on to entangle the mind and affections with other things, and, if it is not prevented, will end in weariness of God, which he complains of in his people (Isa 43:22). They gave up duty because they were weary of God.

But this instance is of great importance to professing Christians in their walk with God, so we must not pass it over without some hints of direction for them in contending against it and opposing it. Only this must be said first. I am not giving directions for mortifying indwelling sin in general, which is to be done by the Spirit of Christ alone, by virtue of our union with him (Rom 8:13). I am giving directions only for our particular duty with respect to this special evil or effect of indwelling sin that we have dwelt on a little, or what the wisdom of faith seems to direct to and call for in this single case. And this will be our way and course as we go on to consider its other effects.

1. First, the great means of preventing the fruits and effects of this aversion is to keep the soul constantly in a universally holy frame. As this weakens the whole law of sin, so it weakens all its properties correspondingly, and this aversion in particular. It is this frame alone that will enable us to say with the psalmist (Ps 57:7), “My heart is steadfast, O God, my heart is steadfast.” It is utterly impossible to keep the heart in a prevailing holy frame in any one duty unless it is so in and toward all and every one. If the entanglements of sin get hold in one thing, they will force themselves on the soul in everything. A constant, even frame and disposition in all duties, in all ways, is the only safeguard for any one way. Let no man who is negligent in public duties persuade himself that all will be clear and easy in private, or the other way round. There is a harmony in obedience: break only one part and you interrupt the whole. Our particular wounds generally arise from negligence in the whole course. So David tells us in Ps 119:6: “Then I would not be ashamed when I have regard to all Your commandments.” A universal regard for all God’s commandments is the only safeguard from shame. And there is nothing we have more reason to be ashamed of than the shameful failures of our hearts in matters of duty, which come from the principle mentioned before.

2. Secondly, labor to prevent the very beginnings of the workings of this aversion. Let grace be beforehand with it in every duty. We are directed in 1 Pet 4:7 to “be clear-minded and watchful, so that you+ can pray”; and as it is with prayer, so with every duty. That is, we are to consider and take care that we are not hindered, from within or from without, in performing it as we should. Watch against temptations, to oppose them; watch against the aversion that is in sin, to prevent it. As we are not to “give the devil a foothold,” no more are we to give one to sin. If it is not prevented in its first attempts, it will prevail. My meaning is this. Whatever good, as the apostle speaks, we have to do, and “find evil present with us” (and we will find it present), we must prevent its parleying with the soul, its working its poison into the mind and affections. We must do it by a vigorous, holy, violent stirring up of the grace or graces that are to be exercised and set to work particularly in that duty. Let Jacob come into the world first; or if he is forestalled by the violence of Esau, let him take hold of his heel to overthrow him and obtain the birthright. At Peter’s very first suggestion to our Savior, when he cried, “Far be it from You, Lord!” our Savior immediately replied, “Get behind Me, Satan!” So we ought to say, “Be gone, you law of sin, you present evil,” and it may be of the same use to us. Get grace up early to duty, then, and be early in rebuking sin.

3. Thirdly, though it does its worst, make sure that it never prevails to a conquest. Make sure that you+ are not worn out by its persistence, nor driven from your+ hold by its pestering. Do not lose heart because of its opposition. Take the apostle’s advice in Heb 6:11: “We want each of you+ to show this same diligence to the very end, in order to make your+ hope sure. Then you+ will not be sluggish.” Keep holding out in the same diligence. There are many ways in which men are driven from a constant, holy performance of duties, and all of them are dangerous, if not deadly, to the soul. Some are drawn away by business, some by company, some by the power of temptations; some are discouraged by their own darkness. But none is so dangerous as this: when the soul gives up, in part or in whole, worn out by the aversion of sin to the duty, or to communion with God in it. This shows that the soul is giving itself up to the power of sin, which will surely prove ruinous unless the Lord breaks the snare of Satan in it. Our Savior’s instruction is that we ought “to pray at all times and not lose heart” (Luke 18:1). Opposition will arise, and none so bitter and sharp as that from our own hearts. If we lose heart, we perish. “Take care,” says the apostle, “so that you+ will not grow weary and lose heart” (Heb 12:3). Such a losing of heart as goes with a weariness, and with giving way to the aversion working in our hearts, is to be avoided if we would not perish. The caution is the same as that of the same apostle in Rom 12:12: “Be joyful in hope, patient in affliction, persistent in prayer.” And in general it is the same as Rom 6:12: “Therefore do not let sin reign in your+ mortal body so that you+ obey its lusts.” To give up duty, in part or in whole, because of sin’s aversion to its spirituality, is to give sin the rule and to obey it in its lusts. Do not yield to it, then, but hold out in the conflict. Wait on God and you+ will prevail (Isa 40:31): “But those who wait upon the Lord will renew their strength; they will mount up with wings like eagles; they will run and not grow weary, they will walk and not faint.” What is now so difficult will grow more difficult if we give way to it. But if we stay at our post, we will prevail; the mouth of the Lord has spoken it.

4. Fourthly, carry about a constant humbling sense of this hidden aversion to spirituality that still lies in our nature. If men find its efficacy, what consideration should, what consideration can, be more powerful to bring them to walk humbly with God? After all the revelations God has made of himself to them, all the kindness they have received from him, his doing them good and not evil in all things, there should still remain such a heart of unkindness and unbelief as to have an aversion lying in it to communion with him! How the thought of it ought to cast us into the dust and fill us with shame and self-abhorrence all our days! What have we found in God in any of our approaches or addresses to him, that it should be this way with us? What iniquity have we found in him? “Has he been a wilderness to us, or a land of dense darkness?” Did we ever lose anything by drawing near to him? No, has not all the rest and peace we have obtained lain in this? Is he not the fountain and spring of all our mercies, of all our desirable things? Has he not made us welcome when we came? Have we not received from him more than the heart can conceive or the tongue express? What is wrong, then, with our foolish and wretched hearts, that they harbor such a cursed secret dislike of him and his ways? Let us be ashamed and astonished when we consider it, and walk in a humbling sense of it all our days. Let us carry it about with us in the most secret of our thoughts. And as this is a duty acceptable to God in itself, who delights to dwell with those who are of a “humble and contrite spirit,” so it has exceeding efficacy in weakening the evil we are treating.

5. Fifthly, labor to fill the mind with the beauty and excellence of spiritual things, so that they may be presented to the soul as lovely and desirable; and this cursed aversion of sin will be weakened by it. It is an inborn, acknowledged principle that the soul of man will not keep on cheerfully in the worship of God unless it discovers a beauty and attractiveness in it. That is why, when men had lost all spiritual sense and relish of the things of God, they invented outwardly pompous and gorgeous ways of worship, to supply the lack that was in their own souls: images, paintings, pictures, and who knows what carnal ornaments, which they have called the “beauties of holiness.” This much, however, was revealed in it: the mind of man must see a beauty, a desirableness, in the things of God’s worship, or it will not delight in it, and aversion will prevail. Let the soul, then, labor to become acquainted with the spiritual beauty of obedience, of communion with God, and of all duties of immediate approach to him, so that it may be filled with delight in them. It is not my present work to lay open the heads and springs of the beauty and desirableness that is in spiritual duties: in their relation to God, the eternal spring of all beauty; to Christ, the love, desire, and hope of all nations; to the Spirit, the great beautifier of souls, who by his grace makes them all glorious within; in their fitness to the souls of men as to their actions toward their last end; and in the rightness and holiness of the rule by which they are to be performed. I only say at present, in general, that to acquaint the soul thoroughly with these things is an eminent way of weakening the aversion spoken of.

Chapter VI

The work of this enmity against God by way of opposition. First, it lusts. What the lusting of sin consists in. Its surprising of the soul. Its readiness to embrace temptations. Its fighting and warring: (1.) in rebellion against the law of grace; (2.) in assaulting the soul.

We have declared how this enmity works by way of aversion, and also the means the soul is to use to prevent its effects and its prevailing power. The second way in which it puts itself forth is opposition. Enmity will oppose and contend with whatever it is at enmity with. It is so in things natural and moral. As light and darkness, heat and cold, oppose each other, so do virtue and vice. So it is with sin and grace. These, says the apostle, are contrary to one another (Gal 5:17): ἀντίκειται ἀλλήλοις, they are “placed and set in mutual opposition,” and that continually and constantly, as we will see.

Now there are two ways in which enemies carry on an opposition: first, by force; and secondly, by fraud and deceit. So when the Egyptians became enemies to the children of Israel and carried on an enmity against them, Pharaoh said (Exod 1:10), “let us deal wisely with them,” or rather, cunningly and subtly with this people. For so Stephen, with regard to this word, expresses it in Acts 7:19, by κατασοφισάμενος: he used “every kind of fraudulent sophistry.” And to this deceit they added force, in their grievous oppressions. This is the way and manner of things where there is a prevailing enmity. And the law of sin makes use of both in its enmity against God and our souls.

I will begin with the first: its actions as it were by way of force, in an open, outright opposition to God and his law, or to the good that a believing soul wants to do in obedience to God and his law. And in this whole matter we must be careful to steer our course rightly, taking the Scripture as our guide, with spiritual reason and experience as our companions. For there are many sandbanks in our course, which must be diligently avoided, so that none who consider these things are troubled without cause or comforted without a just foundation.

In this first way in which this sin puts forth its enmity in opposition, namely, as it were by force or strength, there are four things, expressing so many distinct degrees in its progress and procedure in pursuing its enmity.

1. First, its general inclination: it lusts (Gal 5:17).

2. Secondly, its particular way of contending: it fights or wars (Rom 7:23; Jas 4:1; 1 Pet 2:11).

3. Thirdly, its success in this contest: it brings the soul into captivity to the law of sin (Rom 7:23).

4. Fourthly, its growth and rage upon success: it rises to madness, as an enraged enemy will (Eccl 9:3). We must speak to all of these in order.

1. First, in general it is said to lust (Gal 5:17): “The flesh lusts against the Spirit.” This word expresses the general nature of the opposition that the law of sin makes against God and the rule of his Spirit or grace in those who believe, and so it expresses the least degree of that opposition. Whenever it does anything, it lusts. Because burning is the general action of fire, whatever else fire does, it also burns. When fire does anything, it burns; and when the law of sin does anything, it lusts.

That is why all the actions of this law of sin are called the lusts of the flesh. Gal 5:16: “you+ will not gratify the lusts of the flesh.” Rom 13:14: “make no provision for the lusts of the flesh.” Nor are these lusts of the flesh only those by which men act out their sensuality in carousing, drunkenness, uncleanness, and the like. They include all the actions of the law of sin whatever, in all the faculties and affections of the soul. So in Eph 2:3 we have mention of the desires, or wills, or lusts of the mind, as well as of the flesh. The mind, the most spiritual part of the soul, has its lusts no less than the sensual appetite, which at times seems more properly to be called the flesh. And in the products of these lusts there are defilements of the spirit as well as of the flesh (2 Cor 7:1), that is, of the mind and understanding as well as of the appetite and affections, and of the body that serves them. And our holiness consists in the blamelessness of all these (1 Thess 5:23): “Now may the God of peace Himself sanctify you+ completely, and may your+ entire spirit, soul, and body be kept blameless at the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ.” Indeed, by the flesh in this matter the whole old man, or the law of sin, is meant (John 3:6): “What is born of the flesh is flesh”; that is, it is all flesh, and nothing else. And whatever remains of the old nature in the new man is still flesh. And this flesh lusts; this law of sin does so, and this is the general basis and foundation of all its opposition to God. And it does this in two ways.

1. First, in a hidden, secret inclination to all evil. This lies in it habitually. While a man is in the state of nature, fully under the power and dominion of this law of sin, it is said that “every figment of his heart is evil,” and that continually (Gen 6:5). It can frame, fashion, produce, or act nothing but what is evil, because this habitual inclination to evil that is in the law of sin is absolutely predominant in such a man. It is in the heart like poison that has nothing to lessen its venomous qualities, and so it infects whatever it touches. And where its power and dominion are broken, in its own nature it still has a habitual inclination to what is evil, and its lusting consists in this.

But here we must distinguish between the habitual frame of the heart and the natural inclination or habitual bent of the law of sin in the heart. The habitual inclination of the heart takes its name from the principle that bears the chief or sovereign rule in it. So in believers it is toward good, toward God, toward holiness, toward obedience. The heart is not habitually inclined to evil by the remainders of indwelling sin. But this sin in the heart has a constant habitual inclination to evil in itself, in its own nature. This is what the apostle means by its being present with us. It is present with me, that is, always, and for its own end, which is to lust to sin.

It is with indwelling sin as with a river. While its springs and fountains are open and water is continually supplied to its streams, set a dam before it, and the dam makes it rise and swell until it bears down everything or overflows the banks around it. Let these waters be lessened, dried up in good measure at their springs, and what remains may be held back and restrained. But as long as there is any running water, it will constantly press on whatever stands before it, according to its weight and strength, because it is its nature to do so. And if by any means it makes a passage, it will go on. So it is with indwelling sin. While its springs and fountains are open, it is vain for men to set a dam before it by their convictions of sin, resolutions, vows and promises. They may check it for a while, but it will increase, rise high, and rage at one time or another, until it bears down all those convictions and resolutions, or makes itself an underground passage by some secret lust that will give it full vent. But now suppose that its springs are much dried up by regenerating grace, and its streams or actions lessened by holiness. Still, while anything of it remains, it will constantly be pressing to have vent, to press forward into actual sin. And this is its lusting.

And this habitual inclination in it is shown in two ways.

(1.) First, in its unexpected surprisings of the soul into foolish, sinful figments and imaginations, which the soul did not look for and for which no occasion was given. It is with indwelling sin as it is with the contrary principle of sanctifying grace. Grace gives the soul, if I may say so, many a blessed surprise. It often produces and brings forth a holy, spiritual frame in the heart and mind when we have had no previous reasoned considerations to work them to it. And this shows it to be a habitual principle prevailing in the mind. So in Song 6:12: “Before I was aware, my soul made me like the chariots of Amminadib”; that is, free, willing and ready for communion with Christ. לא ידעתי, “I knew not”: it was done by the power of the Spirit of grace, so that I took no notice of it, as it were, until it was done. The frequent actings of grace in this way, stirring up acts of faith, love and delight in God, are evidences of its great strength and prevailing power in the soul. And so it is also with indwelling sin. Before the soul is aware, without any provocation or temptation, when it does not know it, it is thrown into a vain and foolish frame. Sin produces its figments secretly in the heart and gets ahead of the mind’s consideration of what it is doing. By this I mean those actus primo primi [first stirrings], the first acts of the soul. They are involuntary so far as they do not have the actual consent of the will, but they are voluntary so far as sin has its residence in the will. And unless the soul is awake to take speedy care to prevent where they tend, these surprisings often set everything on fire, as it were, and draw the mind and affections into actual sin. For as by grace we are often, before we are aware, made like the “chariots of a willing people,” and are far engaged in heavenly-mindedness and communion with Christ, making speed in it as in a chariot, so by sin we are often carried, before we are aware, into disordered affections, foolish imaginations, and a pleasing delight in things that are neither good nor profitable. That is the reason for the apostle’s caution in Gal 6:1: ἐὰν προληφθῇ, if a man is surprised unawares by a fault, or in a trespass. I do not doubt that the apostle takes into account there the subtlety of Satan and the power of temptation, which make him express a man’s falling into sin by προληφθῇ, “if he is surprised.” But this working of indwelling sin is also taken into account in it, and that in the chief place, since without it nothing else could surprise us. For without its help, whatever comes from outside, from Satan or the world, must have some parley in the mind before it is received. But it is from within, from ourselves, that we are surprised. By this we are thwarted and brought over to do what we do not want to do, and hindered from doing what we want to do.

That is why, often, when the soul is doing something quite different, as it were, wholly engaged in another purpose, sin starts something in the heart or its imaginations that carries it away into what is evil and sinful. Indeed, to show its power, sometimes when the soul is seriously engaged in the mortification of some sin, sin will by one means or another lead it away into a toying with that very sin whose ruin it is seeking and whose mortification it is engaged in. But since there is in this working of the law of sin a special enticing or entangling, we will speak to it fully later. Now these surprisings can come from nothing but a habitual inclination to evil in the principle they come from: not a habitual inclination to actual sin in the mind or heart, but a habitual inclination to evil in the sin that is in the mind or heart. This gets ahead of the soul with its figments. Some may have observed how much communion with God is hindered by this, how many meditations are disturbed, how much the minds and consciences of men have been defiled by this action of sin. I know no greater burden in the life of a believer than these involuntary surprisings of the soul: involuntary, I say, as to the actual consent of the will, but not so with respect to the corruption that is in the will and is their principle. And it is with respect to these that the apostle makes his complaint in Rom 7:24.

(2.) Secondly, this habitual inclination shows itself in its readiness and promptness, without dispute or argument, to join with and embrace every temptation by which it may possibly be stirred up. We know it is the nature of fire to burn, because it immediately takes hold of whatever is combustible. Let any temptation whatever be set before a man, one that is a temptation because its matter suits his corruptions or because of the way it is put to him. Immediately he has to deal not only with the temptation as it is set before him from outside, but also with his own heart about it. Without further consideration or debate, the temptation has gained a friend in him. Not a moment passes between the proposal and the necessity that lies on the soul to look to its enemy within. And this too shows a constant habitual inclination to evil. Our Savior said of the assaults and temptations of Satan, “the prince of this world is coming, and he has nothing in Me” (John 14:30). He had more temptations from Satan and the world, both in intensity and in extent, in number, quality and fierceness, than any of the sons of men ever had. Yet in all of them he had to deal only with what came from outside. His holy heart had nothing that liked them, that suited them, or that was ready to receive them: “the prince of this world had nothing in him.” So it was with Adam. When a temptation befell him, he had only its outward proposal to look to; all was well within until the outward temptation took hold and prevailed. With us it is not so.

In a city that is united in itself, compact and whole, without divisions and parties, if an enemy approaches it, the rulers and inhabitants have no thought at all except how they may oppose the enemy outside and resist his approaches. But if the city is divided against itself, if there are factions and traitors within, the very first thing they do is to look to the enemies at home, the traitors within. They must cut off the head of Sheba if they want to be safe. All was well with Adam within doors when Satan came, so that he had nothing to do but look to Satan’s assaults and approaches. But now, at the approach of any temptation, the soul must instantly look within, where it will find this traitor at work, taking the baits of Satan and stealing away the heart. And it always does this, which proves a habitual inclination. In Ps 38:17 David says, “I am ready to fall,” or “for falling”: כי אני לצלע נכון, “I am prepared” and disposed to slipping, to the sliding of my foot into sin (verse 16), as he explains the meaning of that phrase in Ps 73:2, 3. From indwelling sin there was a continual disposition in him to be slipping, stumbling, limping on every occasion or temptation. There is nothing so vain, foolish, ridiculous, or silly, nothing so vile and abominable, nothing so atheistic or detestable, that if it is set before the soul by way of temptation, there is not something in this law of sin ready to answer it before grace condemns it. And this is the first thing in the lusting of the law of sin: it consists in its habitual inclination to evil, which shows itself by the involuntary surprisings of the soul into sin, and by its readiness to join, without dispute or consideration, with all temptations whatever.

2. Secondly, its lusting consists in its actual pressing after what is evil, and its actual opposition to what is good. The former instances showed its constant readiness for this work; this now treats of the work itself. It is not only ready; for the most part it is always engaged. It lusts, says the Holy Spirit; it does so continually. It stirs in the soul constantly by one act or another, almost as the spirits do in the blood, or the blood in the veins. The apostle calls this its tempting (Jas 1:14): “each one is tempted … by his own lust.” Now what is it to be tempted? It is to have something set before a man’s consideration which, if he embraces it, is evil; it is sin to him. This is sin’s trade: ἐπιθυμεῖ, “it lusts.” It is raising up in the heart, and setting before the mind and affections, what is evil, trying, as it were, whether the soul will accept its suggestions, or how far it can carry them on, even if it does not wholly prevail. Now when such a temptation comes from outside, it is to the soul an indifferent thing, neither good nor evil, unless it is consented to. But the very proposal from within, being the soul’s own act, is its sin. And this is the work of the law of sin. It is restlessly and continually raising up and setting before the soul countless different forms and appearances of evil, of this or that kind, indeed of every kind in which the nature of man is capable of exercising corruption. It hatches something or other, in matter, manner or circumstance, that is disordered, unspiritual, not answering to the rule, and sets it before the soul. And the apostle may have in view this power of sin to beget figments and ideas of actual evil in the heart in 1 Thess 5:22: ἀπὸ παντὸς εἴδους πονηροῦ ἀπέχεσθε, keep yourselves+ from every evil figment or idea of sin in the heart. For the word used there nowhere means an outward form or appearance; it is not “the appearance of evil” but an evil idea or figment that is meant. And this lusting of sin is what the prophet describes in wicked men, in whom its law is predominant (Isa 57:20): “the wicked are like the storm-tossed sea, for it cannot be still, and its waves churn up mire and muck.” It is a picture that expresses most vividly the lustings of the law of sin, restlessly and continually bubbling up in the heart with wicked, foolish and filthy imaginations and desires. This, then, is the first thing in the opposition that this enmity makes to God, namely, in its general inclination: it lusts.

2. Secondly, there is its particular way of contending: it fights or wars. That is, it acts with strength and violence, as men do in war. First it lusts, stirring and moving disordered figments in the mind, and desires in the appetite and the affections, and setting them before the will. But it does not rest there; it cannot rest. It urges, presses and pursues its proposals with earnestness, strength and vigor, fighting, contending and warring to obtain its end and purpose. If it merely stirred up and proposed things to the soul, and then immediately submitted to the sentence and judgment of the mind that the thing is evil, against God and his will, and not to be pressed any further, much sin that is now produced might be prevented. But it does not rest here. It goes on to carry out its design, and that with earnestness and contention. By this means wicked men inflame themselves (Isa 57:5). They are self-inflamers to sin, as the word means. Every spark of sin is cherished in them until it grows into a flame; and so it will do in others where it is cherished in the same way.

Now this fighting or warring of sin consists in two things.

(1.) First, in its rebellion against grace, or the law of the mind.

(2.) Secondly, in its assaulting the soul, contending for rule and sovereignty over it.

The first is expressed by the apostle in Rom 7:23: “I find,” he says, “another law,” ἀντιστρατευόμενον τῷ νόμῳ τοῦ νοός μου, “rebelling against the law of my mind.” There are, it seems, two laws in us: the law of the flesh, or of sin, and the law of the mind, or of grace. But contrary laws cannot both hold sovereign power over the same person at the same time. In believers the sovereign power is in the hand of the law of grace. So the apostle declares in verse 22: “For in my inner man I delight in God’s law.” Obedience to this law is performed with delight and pleasure in the inner man, because its authority is lawful and good. He says it more expressly in Rom 6:14: “For sin shall not have dominion over you+, because you+ are not under law, but under grace.” Now to war against the law that has a just sovereignty is to rebel. And that is what ἀντιστρατεύεσθαι means: it is to rebel, and it ought to have been translated so, “rebelling against the law of my mind.” And this rebellion consists in a stubborn, obstinate opposition to the commands and directions of the law of grace. Does the law of the mind command anything as duty? Does it rise up severely against anything that is evil? When the lusting of the law of sin rises to this degree, it contends against obedience with all its might. The effect of this, as the apostle tells us, is “the doing of what we do not want to do, and the not doing of what we want to do” (verses 15, 16). And from this place we may gather a notable instance of the power of sin in this rebellion of it. The law of grace prevails on the will, so that it wants to do what is good: “the desire to do what is good is present with me” (verse 18); “when I want to do good” (verse 19, and again verse 21); and it does not want to do “the evil I do not want to do” (verses 19, 20). And it prevails on the understanding, so that it approves or disapproves according to the dictates of the law of grace. Verse 16: “I agree that the law is good”; and verse 15. The judgment always lies on the side of grace. It prevails also on the affections (verse 22): “in my inner man I delight in God’s law.” Now if this is so, if grace has the sovereign power in the understanding, will, and affections, why does it not always prevail? Why do we not always do what we want to do, and abstain from what we do not want to do? Is it not strange that a man should not do what he chooses, wills, likes, and delights in? Is anything more required to enable us to do what is good? The law of grace does all that can be expected of it, all that is in itself abundantly sufficient for perfecting all holiness in the fear of the Lord. But here lies the difficulty: in the entangling opposition made by the rebellion of this law of sin. Nor can it be expressed with what vigor and variety sin acts in this matter. At times it suggests distractions; at times it causes weariness; at times it finds out difficulties; at times it stirs up contrary affections; at times it breeds prejudices. One way or another it entangles the soul, so that it never allows grace to have an absolute and complete success in any duty. Verse 18: τὸ κατεργάζεσθαι τὸ καλὸν οὐχ εὑρίσκω, I do not find the way perfectly to work out or accomplish what is good, for that is what the word means; and that is because of this opposition and resistance made by the law of sin. Now this rebellion appears in two things.

(1.) First, in the opposition it makes to the general purpose and course of the soul.

(2.) Secondly, in the opposition it makes to particular duties.

(1.) First, in the opposition it makes to the general purpose and course of the soul. There is no one in whom the Spirit of Christ is, who is his, whose general aim and purpose is not to walk in a universal conformity to him in all things. So it is with him, from the inward frame of the heart to the whole compass of his outward actions. God requires this in his covenant (Gen 17:1): “Walk before Me and be blameless.” Accordingly, his aim is to walk before God, and his frame is sincerity and uprightness in it. This is called “clinging to the Lord with purpose of heart” (Acts 11:23); that is, in all things. And it is not a lazy, dead, ineffectual purpose, but one that is at work and sets the whole soul to work in pursuing it. The apostle sets this out in Phil 3:12, 13, 14: “Not that I have already obtained all this, or have already been made perfect, but I press on to take hold of that for which Christ Jesus took hold of me. Brothers, I do not consider myself yet to have taken hold of it. But one thing I do: Forgetting what is behind and straining toward what is ahead, I press on toward the goal to win the prize of God’s heavenly calling in Christ Jesus.” He uses three words that excellently express the soul’s universal pursuit of this purpose of heart in clinging to God. First, he says, διώκω (verse 12), “I press on,” I pursue. The word strictly means to persecute, and we know with what earnestness and diligence that is usually done. Secondly, ἐπεκτείνομαι, “straining toward,” reaching out with great intensity of spirit and affections; it is a great and constant effort that is expressed in that word. Thirdly, κατὰ σκοπὸν διώκω, “I press on toward the goal,” that is, just as men running for a prize. All these set out the vigor, earnestness, diligence and constancy used in pursuing this purpose. And the nature of the principle of grace requires this in those in whom it is. But still we see with what failings, indeed fallings, their pursuit of this course is attended. The frame of the heart is changed, the heart is stolen away, the affections are entangled, outbreaks of unbelief and disordered passions appear, and carnal wisdom with all its retinue is set to work, all contrary to the general principle and purpose of the soul. And all this comes from the rebellion of this law of sin, stirring up and provoking the heart to disobedience. The prophet gives this description of hypocrites in Hos 10:2: “Their heart is divided; therefore they will be found faulty.” Now though this is wholly so, with respect to the mind and judgment, in hypocrites only, yet it is partly so in the best, in the sense described. They have a division, not of the heart, but in the heart; and that is why they are so often found at fault. So the apostle says, “so that we cannot do what we want” (Gal 5:17). We cannot accomplish the aim of walking closely by the law of grace, because of the contrariness and rebellion of this law of sin.

(2.) Secondly, it rebels also with respect to particular duties. It raises an uproar in the soul against the particular commands and designs of the law of grace. “You+ cannot do what you+ want”; that is, the duties you+ judge to be required of you+, which you+ approve and delight in in the inner man, you+ cannot do as you+ want. Take prayer as an instance. A man sets himself to that duty. He wants not only to perform it, but to perform it in the manner the nature of the duty and his own condition require. He wants to “pray in the Spirit,” fervently, with sighs and groans too deep for words; in faith, with love and delight, pouring out his soul to the Lord. This is what he aims at. Now he will often find a rebellion in this matter, a fighting of the law of sin. He who thought to do all things will find it hard to get anything done. I do not say that it is always so; but it is so when sin wars and rebels, which is a special exercise of its power. Poor creatures often meet with woeful entanglements on this account. Instead of the free, enlarged communion with God that they aim at, the best their souls arrive at is to go away mourning for their folly, deadness and unfitness. In a word, there is no command of the law of grace that is known, liked, and approved by the soul, but when it comes to be obeyed, this law of sin rises against it and rebels one way or another. And this is the first way of its fighting.

2. Secondly, it does not only rebel and resist; it assaults the soul. It attacks the law of the mind and of grace, which is the second part of its warring. 1 Pet 2:11: στρατεύονται κατὰ τῆς ψυχῆς, “they fight,” or war, “against the soul.” Jas 4:1: στρατεύονται ἐν τοῖς μέλεσιν ὑμῶν, “they fight,” or war, “in your+ members.” Peter shows what they oppose and fight against, namely the soul and the law of grace in it; James shows what they fight with, or by, namely the members, or the corruption that is in our mortal bodies. ἀντιστρατεύεσθαι is to rebel against a superior; στρατεύεσθαι is to assault, or make war for superiority. Sin takes the part of an assailant as well as of a resister. It makes attempts for rule and sovereignty, as well as opposing the rule of grace. Now all war and fighting has something of violence in it, and so there is some violence in that action of sin which the Scripture calls fighting and warring. And this assailing efficacy of sin, as distinguished from its rebelling treated before, consists in the following things.

(1.) First, all its positive actions in stirring up to sin belong under this head. Often, by the futility of the mind, or the sensuality of the affections, or the folly of the imaginations, it attacks the soul at a time when the law of grace is not actually putting it to duty; so in this it does not rebel, but assault. That is why the apostle cries out in Rom 7:24, “Who will deliver me” from it? Who will rescue me out of its hand, as the word means? When we pursue an enemy and he resists us, we do not cry out, “Who will rescue us?” for we are the assailants. But “Who will rescue me?” is the cry of one who is attacked by an enemy. So it is here. A man is assaulted by his own lusts, as James speaks. By the wayside, in his work, in the middle of a duty, sin attacks the soul with vain imaginations and foolish desires, and would gladly employ the soul in making provision for its satisfaction. The apostle warns us against this in Rom 13:14: τῆς σαρκὸς πρόνοιαν μὴ ποιεῖσθε εἰς ἐπιθυμίας, do not carry out the provision or the plans of the flesh for its own satisfaction.

(2.) Secondly, its persistence and urgency seem to be indicated in this expression of its warring. Enemies in war are restless, pressing and persistent. So is the law of sin. Does it attack the soul? Throw off its impulses, and it comes back again. Rebuke them by the power of grace, and they withdraw for a while and then come back again. Set the cross of Christ before them, and they do as those did who came to arrest him: at the sight of him they drew back and fell to the ground, but they got up again and laid hands on him. Sin gives way for a time, but returns and presses on the soul again. Remind it of the love of God in Christ: though it is struck, it still does not give up. Set hell-fire before it: it rushes into the middle of those flames. Reproach it with its folly and madness: it knows no shame, but still presses on. Let the thoughts of the mind try to flee from it: it follows as on the wings of the wind. And by this persistence it wearies and wears out the soul; and if the great remedy of Rom 8:13 does not come in time, it prevails to a conquest. There is nothing more amazing or dreadful in the working of sin than this persistence of it. The soul does not know what to make of it. It dislikes, abhors, and detests the evil sin tends to; it despises the thoughts of it and hates them as hell. And yet it has them forced on it by itself, as if it were another person, an outright enemy that had got inside him. The apostle lays all this open in Rom 7:15, 17: “what I hate, I do.” He is not speaking of outward actions, but of the inward risings of the mind. “I hate them,” he says; I detest them. But why, then, will he have anything more to do with them? If he hates them and abhors himself for them, let him leave them alone, have nothing more to do with them, and so end the matter. “Alas,” he says (verse 17), “‘it is no longer I who do it, but it is sin dwelling in me that does it.’ I have one within me who is my enemy, who with endless, restless persistence forces these things on me, even the things that I hate and detest. I cannot be rid of them; I am weary of myself; I cannot flee from them. ‘What a wretched man I am! Who will rescue me?’” I do not say that this is the ordinary condition of believers, but it is often so when this law of sin rises up to war and fighting. It is not so with them with respect to particular sins, this or that sin, outward sins, sins of life or conduct. But with respect to futility of mind and inward, spiritual disorders, it is often so. I know some claim great perfection, but I am resolved to believe the apostle before every one of them.

(3.) Thirdly, it carries on its war by entangling the affections and drawing them into a league against the mind. Let grace be enthroned in the mind and judgment; yet if the law of sin lays hold on and entangles the affections, or any of them, it has gained a fort from which it continually assaults the soul. That is why the great duty of mortification is chiefly directed to take effect on the affections (Col 3:5): “Put to death, therefore, your+ members that are on the earth: sexual immorality, impurity, lust, evil desires, and covetousness, which is idolatry.” The members that are on the earth are our affections. For sin is not seated in the outward part of the body; in particular, covetousness is not, and yet it is listed there to be mortified among our “members that are on the earth.” Indeed, after grace has taken possession of the soul, the affections become the chief seat of the remainders of sin. And so Paul says that this law “is in our members” (Rom 7:23), and James that it wars in our members (Jas 4:1); that is, in our affections. And there is no right estimate to be made of the work of mortification except by the affections. Every day we may see persons of very eminent light who still visibly have unmortified hearts and lives. Their affections have not been crucified with Christ. Now, then, when this law of sin can possess any affection whatever it is, love, delight, fear, it will make fearful assaults on the soul from it and by it. For instance, has it got someone’s love entangled with the world or the things of it, “the lust of the flesh, the lust of the eyes, or the pride of life”? How it will take advantage of every occasion to break in on the soul! The soul will do nothing, attempt nothing, be in no place or company, perform no duty, private or public, but sin will have one blow or another at it; one way or another it will be pleading for itself.

This is the sum of what we will offer about this action of the law of sin by way of fighting and warring against our souls, which is so often mentioned in the Scripture. And a due consideration of it is of no small benefit to us, especially to bring us to self-abasement, to teach us to walk humbly and mournfully before God. There are two things suited to humble the souls of men: first, a due consideration of God, and then of themselves. Of God in his greatness, glory, holiness, power, majesty and authority; of ourselves in our lowly, abject, and sinful condition. Now of all things in our condition, nothing is so suited to this end and purpose as what lies before us: the vile remainders of enmity against God that are still in our hearts and natures. And it is no small evidence of a gracious soul when it is willing to search itself in this matter and to be helped in it by a word of truth, when it is willing that the word should dive into the secret parts of the heart and rip open whatever evil and corruption lies there. The prophet says of Ephraim in Hos 10:11 that he “loves to thresh”: he loved to work when he could eat, to have the grain always before him. But God, he says, would make him plow, a labor no less needful, though not so pleasant at present. Most men love to hear of the doctrine of grace, of the pardon of sin, of free love, and suppose they find food in it; at any rate it is evident that they grow and thrive in the life and notion of these things. But they do not delight so much in breaking up the fallow ground of their hearts, in inquiring after the weeds and briars that grow in them, though this is no less necessary than the other. This path is not so beaten as that of grace, nor so much trodden, though it is the only way to come to a true knowledge of grace itself. Perhaps some who are wise and grown in other truths may still be so little skilled in searching their own hearts that they are slow in perceiving and understanding these things. But this sloth and neglect must be shaken off if we have any regard for our own souls. It is more than probable that many a false hypocrite, who has deceived himself as well as others because he thought the doctrine of the gospel pleased him and so supposed he believed it, might be delivered from his soul-ruining deceits if he would diligently apply himself to this search of his own heart. Or would other professing Christians walk with as much boldness and complacency as some do, if they considered rightly what a deadly, watchful enemy they continually carry about with them and in them? Would they indulge as much as they do in carnal joys and pleasures, or pursue their perishing affairs with as much delight and greed as they do? It is to be wished that we would all apply our hearts more to this work, to come to a true understanding of the nature, power and subtlety of this our adversary, so that our souls may be humbled; and that,

1. First, in walking with God. His delight is with the humble and contrite, those who tremble at his word, the mourners in Zion; and we are such only when we have a due sense of our own vile condition. This will produce reverence for God, a sense of our distance from him, wonder at his grace in stooping so low to us, and a due valuing of mercy, far above those light, merely verbal, airy attainments that some have boasted of.

2. Secondly, in walking with others. It lays in provision to prevent those great evils of judging, spiritual unmercifulness, and harsh censuring, which I have observed many to make a show of, men who at the same time, as afterward appeared, were guilty of greater or worse crimes than those they raved against in others. This, I say, will lead us to meekness, compassion, and readiness to forgive and to pass over offenses, when we consider what our own state is, as the apostle plainly declares in Gal 6:1. The man who understands the evil of his own heart, how vile it is, is the only useful, fruitful, and solidly believing and obedient person. Others are fit only to delude themselves and to disturb families, churches, and all relationships whatever. Let us, then, consider our hearts wisely, and then go and see whether we can be proud of our gifts, our graces, our reputation and esteem among professing Christians, our enjoyments. Let us go then and judge, condemn, and reproach others who have been tempted; we will find a great inconsistency in these things. And many things of the same kind might be added here from considering this woeful effect of indwelling sin. The way of opposing and defeating its design in this will be considered later.

Chapter VII

The captivating power of indwelling sin, and what it consists in. The prevailing power of sin, when it is from itself, and when from temptation. The rage and madness that is in sin.

The third thing ascribed to this law of sin in its opposition to God and the law of his grace is that it takes the soul captive (Rom 7:23): “I see another law … holding me captive (making me captive) to the law of sin.” This is the utmost height to which the apostle in that place carries the opposition and warring of the remainders of indwelling sin. He closes his consideration of it with a complaint of the state and condition of believers because of it, and an earnest prayer for deliverance from it (verse 24): “What a wretched man I am! Who will rescue me from this body of death?” What is contained in this expression and meant by it will be declared in the following observations.

1. First, it is not directly the power and actions of the law of sin that are expressed here, but its success in and by its actions. But success is the greatest evidence of power, and taking captive in war is the height of success. No one can aim at greater success than to take his enemies captive. And it is a particular expression in the Scripture for great success. So the Lord Christ, on his victory over Satan, is said to have “led captivity captive” (Eph 4:8); that is, to have conquered him who had conquered and prevailed over others. And he did this when by his death he “destroyed him who holds the power of death, that is, the devil” (Heb 2:14). Here, then, a great prevailing power and strength of sin in its warring against the soul is revealed. It wars so as to take captive. It could not do this if it did not have great power, especially against the resistance of the soul that is included in this expression.

2. Secondly, it is said to take the soul captive “to the law of sin”: not to this or that sin, a particular sin, an actual sin, but to the law of sin. For the most part God orders things so, and gives out such supplies of grace to believers, that they are not made a prey to this or that particular sin. He does not allow it to prevail in them and compel them to serve it in its lusts, to have dominion over them, so that they are its captives and slaves. This is what David prays against so earnestly in Ps 19:12, 13: “Cleanse me from my hidden faults. Keep Your servant also from presumptuous sins; may they not have dominion over me. Then I will be blameless.” He supposes that the law of sin continues in him (verse 12), which will bring forth errors of life and secret sins; and against these he finds relief in pardoning and cleansing mercy, which he prays for. “This,” he says, “will be my condition. But as for sins of pride and boldness, such as all sins are that get dominion in a man and make a captive of him, Lord, restrain your servant from them.” For whatever sin gets such power in a man, whether it is small or great in its own nature, becomes in the man in whom it is a sin of boldness, pride, and presumption. For these things are not reckoned from the nature or kind of the sin, but from its prevailing power and habitual practice, in which its pride, boldness, and contempt of God consist. Jabez prays to the same purpose, if I am not mistaken (1 Chr 4:10): “If only You would bless me and enlarge my territory! May Your hand be with me and keep me from evil, so that it may not grieve me.” The holy man took occasion from his own name to pray against sin, that it might not be a grief and sorrow to him by its power and prevailing. I admit that it may sometimes come to this with a believer: for a time he may be taken captive by some particular sin. It may have so much prevailing power in him as to have power over him. So it seems to have been with David when he lay so long in his sin without repentance. And it was plainly so with those in Isa 57:17, 18: “I was enraged by his sinful covetousness, so I struck him and hid My face in anger; yet he went on stubbornly in the way of his heart. I have seen his ways, but I will heal him.” They continued under the power of their covetousness, so that none of God’s dealings with them for so long a time could reclaim them. But for the most part, when any lust or sin prevails in this way, it is from the advantage and help it has gained from some powerful temptation of Satan. He has poisoned it, inflamed it, and entangled the soul. So the apostle, speaking of those who had fallen away from their holiness through sin, says they were in “the snare of the devil, who has taken them captive to his will” (2 Tim 2:26). Though it was their own lust that they served, they were brought into bondage to it by being entangled in some snare of Satan. And so they are said to be taken alive, like a poor beast in a trap.

And here, in passing, we may inquire a little whether the prevailing power of a particular sin in anyone comes from itself or from the influence of temptation on it. On this, take only these two observations for the present.

(1.) First, much of the prevailing power of sin over the soul certainly comes from Satan when the perplexing and captivating sin has no particular footing or advantage in the nature, constitution, or condition of the sinner. When any lust grows high and prevails more than others on its own account, it is from the particular advantage it has in the natural constitution of the person, or in his station or condition in the world. For otherwise the law of sin gives an equal inclination to all evil and an equal vigor to every lust. So when it cannot be discerned that the captivating sin is particularly fixed in the nature of the sinner, or is helped by his upbringing or his work in the world, its prevailing power comes particularly from Satan. He has got to its root and has given it poison and strength. Indeed, perhaps at times what may seem to the soul to be the corrupt lusting of the heart is nothing but Satan forcing his suggestions on the imagination. If, then, a man finds a persistent rage from some corruption that is not evidently seated in his nature, let him, as the Papists say, cross himself, or flee by faith to the cross of Christ, for the devil is close at hand.

(2.) Secondly, when a lust prevails to captivity where it brings in no advantage to the flesh, it is from Satan. All that the law of sin does of itself is to serve the provision of the flesh (Rom 13:14), and it must bring in to the flesh something of the profits and pleasures that are its object. Now if the prevailing sin does not act in this way, if it is more spiritual and inward, it comes much more from Satan, by way of the imagination, than from the corruption of the heart itself. But this is in passing.

I say, then, that the apostle is not dealing here with our being taken captive to this or that sin, but to the law of sin. That is, we are compelled to bear its presence and burden whether we want to or not. At times the soul thinks or hopes that through grace it may be utterly freed from this troublesome lodger. After some sweet enjoyment of God, some full supply of grace, some return from wandering, some deep affliction, some thorough humiliation, the poor soul begins to hope that it will now be freed from the law of sin. But after a while it sees that it is quite otherwise. Sin acts again and holds its old position, and the soul finds that whether it wants to or not, it must bear its yoke. This makes it sigh and cry out for deliverance.

3. Thirdly, this taking captive shows a prevailing against the resistance, or the contrary actions, of the will. This is plainly implied in this expression, namely, that the will opposes, and as it were rises up against, the working of sin. The apostle declares this in the expressions he uses in verses 15, 19, 20. And this is what the lusting of the Spirit against the flesh consists in (Gal 5:17), that is, the contending of grace to drive it out and subdue it. The spiritual habits of grace that are in the will resist it and act against it in this way, and the stirring up of those habits by the Spirit is directed to the same purpose. This taking captive, I say, is contrary to the inclinations and actions of the renewed will. No man is made a captive except against his will. Captivity is misery and trouble, and no man willingly puts himself into trouble. Men choose it in its causes, and in the ways and means that lead to it, but not in itself. So the prophet tells us in Hos 5:11: “Ephraim was not willingly oppressed and crushed in judgment”; that was his misery and trouble. But he willingly followed the command of the idolatrous kings, which brought him to it. Whatever consent, then, the soul may give to sin, which is the means of this captivity, it gives none to the captivity itself; that is wholly against the will. From this the following things result.

(1.) First, the power of sin is great, which is what we are demonstrating. This appears in its prevailing to captivity against the actions and strivings of the will for liberty from it. If no opposition were made to it, or if its adversary were weak, negligent and lazy, it would be no great evidence of its power that it made captives. But its prevailing against diligence, activity, watchfulness, and the constant resistance of the will proves its efficacy.

(2.) Secondly, this taking captive implies many particular successes. If it had no success in particulars, it could not be said to take captive at all. It might rebel, it might assault, but it cannot be said to take captive without some successes. And there are several degrees of the success of the law of sin in the soul. At times it carries the person into outward actual sin, which is its utmost aim. At times it obtains the consent of the will, but is cast out by grace and goes no further. At times it wearies and entangles the soul so that it turns aside, as it were, and gives up contending, which is a success too. One or more or all of these must be present where captivity takes place. The apostle ascribes this kind of course to covetousness in 1 Tim 6:9.

(3.) Thirdly, this taking captive shows this condition to be miserable and wretched. To be yoked and dealt with like this against the judgment of the mind, against the choice and consent of the will, against its utmost strivings and contendings: how sad it is! When the neck is sore and tender from former burdens, to be compelled to bear the yoke again: this pierces, this grieves, this even breaks the heart. When the soul has been given by grace a principle of loathing sin and every evil way, of hatred for the least disagreement between itself and the holy will of God, then to be forced by this law of sin, with all the enmity and folly, the deadness and filth that go with it: what condition could be more dreadful? All captivity is dreadful in its own nature, and what makes it worst of all comes from the character of the tyrant to whom anyone is made captive. Now what can be worse than this law of sin? That is why the apostle, having once mentioned this captivity, cries out like one quite weary and ready to faint (verse 24).

(4.) Fourthly, this condition is found only in believers. Unregenerate men are not said to be taken captive to the law of sin. They may indeed be taken captive to this or that particular sin or corruption; that is, they may be forced to serve it against the power of their own conscience. They are convicted of its evil (an adulterer of his uncleanness, a drunkard of his abomination), and perhaps they make some resolutions against it. But their lust is too strong for them; they cannot stop sinning, and so they are made captives or slaves to this or that particular sin. But they cannot be said to be taken captive to the law of sin, and that is because they are willingly subject to it. It has, as it were, a rightful dominion over them, and they do not oppose it, except when it breaks out to the disturbance of their consciences. And then the opposition they make to it does not come from their wills; it is merely the action of a frightened conscience and a convicted mind. They do not regard the nature of sin, but its guilt and consequences. But to be brought into captivity is what befalls a man against his will. This is all that will be said about this degree of the actions of the power of sin, as it shows itself in its success.

The Rage and Madness of Sin

The fourth and last degree of the opposition the law of sin makes to God and the law of his will and grace is in its rage and madness. There is madness in its nature (Eccl 9:3): “the hearts of men are full of evil, and madness is in their hearts.” The evil that the heart of man is full of by nature is the indwelling sin we are speaking of. And it is so in their heart that it rises up to madness. The Holy Spirit expresses this rage of sin by a fitting picture which he uses in various places, such as Jer 2:24 and Hos 8:9. It makes men like a wild donkey: she roams her ways, sniffs the wind, and runs wherever her mind or lust leads her. And he says of idolaters enraged with their lusts that they “go mad over idols” (Jer 50:38). We may consider a little what lies in this madness and rage of sin, and how it rises up to it.

1. First, as for its nature, it seems to consist in a violent, headlong, stubborn pressing toward evil or sin. There is violence, persistence and stubbornness in it. It is the tearing and torturing of the soul by some sin to force its consent and obtain satisfaction. It rises up in the heart, is refused and rebuked by the law of grace. It returns and puts out its poison again. The soul is startled and throws it off. It returns again with new violence and persistence. The soul cries out for help and deliverance, looks around to all the springs of gospel grace and relief, trembles at the furious assaults of sin, and throws itself into the arms of Christ for deliverance. And if it is not able to take that course, it is beaten and dragged up and down through the mire and filth of foolish imaginations and corrupt, foul lusts, which rend and tear it as if they would devour its whole spiritual life and power. See 1 Tim 6:9, 10; 2 Pet 2:14. It was not much different with those we gave as an instance before (Isa 57:17). They had an inflamed, enraged lust working in them, namely covetousness, or the love of this world, by which, as the apostle speaks, men “pierce themselves with many sorrows.” God is angry with them and shows his wrath by all the ways and means by which it was possible to make them aware of it. He was angry and struck them; and though perhaps this staggered them a little, still they went on. “He hides his face in anger” from them; he deserts them as to his gracious, helping, comforting presence. Does this have its effect? No, they still go on stubbornly, like men mad on their covetousness. Nothing can put a stop to their raging lusts. This is plain madness and fury. We need not look far for instances; we see men mad on their lusts every day. And, which is the worst kind of madness, their lusts do not rage so much in them as they rage in pursuing them. Are those greedy pursuits of things in the world that we see some men engaged in, though they have other pretenses, really anything but plain madness in pursuing their lusts? God, who searches the hearts of men, knows that most of the things done in the world under other pretenses are nothing but the actions of men mad and furious in pursuing their lusts.

2. Secondly, sin does not ordinarily rise to this height except when it has gained a double advantage.

(1.) First, that it is provoked, enraged and heightened by some great temptation. Though it is a poison in itself, yet because it is inborn in our nature, it does not grow into violent rage unless Satan contributes some new poison to it in a suitable temptation. It was the advantage that Satan gained against David by a suitable temptation that raised his lusts to the rage and madness they went on to in the matter of Bathsheba and Uriah. Though sin is always a fire in the bones, it does not flame up unless Satan comes with his bellows to blow it up. And let anyone in whom the law of sin rises to this height of rage consider it seriously, and he may find out where the devil stands and puts his hand into the matter.

(2.) Secondly, it must be helped by some former welcome and prevailing. Sin does not grow to this height at its first assault. If it had not been allowed to make its entrance, if there had not been some yielding in the soul, this would not have come about. The great wisdom and security of the soul in dealing with indwelling sin is to put a violent stop to its beginnings, its first stirrings and actions. Venture everything on the first attempt. Die rather than yield one step to it. Through the deceit of sin, or the negligence of the soul, or its carnal confidence that it can set bounds to the actions of lust at other times, sin may make some entrance into the soul and find some welcome. If it does, it gets strength and power, and rises imperceptibly to the frame we are considering. You would never have experienced the fury of sin if you had not been content with some of its dalliances. If you had not brought up this servant, this slave, so pampered, it would not now presume to be more than a son. Now when the law of sin in any particular has gained this double advantage, the help of a vigorous temptation and some prevailing gained before, by which it is let into the strongholds of the soul, it often rises up to the frame we are speaking of.

3. Thirdly, we may see what goes with this rage and madness, what its properties are, and what effects it produces.

Casting Off the Rule of Grace

1. First, there is in it the casting off, for a time at least, of the yoke, rule and government of the Spirit and law of grace. Where grace has the dominion, it will never be utterly driven from its throne; it will still keep its right and sovereignty. But its influences may be cut off for a time, and its government suspended, by the power of sin. Can we think that the law of grace had any actual ruling influence on the heart of David when, upon the provocation he received from Nabal, he was so carried away with the desire to avenge himself that he cried to his companions, “Strap on your+ swords!” and resolved not to leave one man of his whole household alive (1 Sam 25:34)? Or that Asa was in any better frame when he struck the prophet who spoke to him in the name of the Lord and put him in prison? In this case sin is like an untamed horse, which, having first thrown its rider, runs away with fierceness and rage. It first throws off a present sense of the yoke of Christ and the law of his grace, and then drives the soul wherever it pleases. Let us consider a little how this is done.

The seat and residence of grace is in the whole soul. It is in the inner man; it is in the mind, the will, and the affections. For “the whole soul is renewed by it to the image of God” (Eph 4:23, 24), and the whole man is a “new creation” (2 Cor 5:17). And in all these it puts forth its power and efficacy. Its rule or dominion is the carrying on of its effectual working in all the faculties of the soul, as they are one united principle of moral and spiritual workings. So, then, the interrupting by the law of sin of the exercise of grace, of its rule and power, must consist in sin’s contrary action in and on the faculties and affections of the soul, on which and by which grace should put forth its power and efficacy. And this sin does. It darkens the mind, partly through countless vain prejudices and false reasonings, as we will see when we come to consider its deceitfulness, and partly through the fumes of the affections, heated with the foul lusts that have laid hold on them. So the saving light that is in the mind is clouded and stifled, so that it cannot put forth its transforming power to change the soul into the likeness of Christ revealed to it, which is its proper work (Rom 12:2). The habitual inclination of the will to obedience, which is the next way the law of grace works, is first weakened, then cast aside and made useless by the continual pleadings of sin and temptation. So the will first lets go its hold and debates whether it will yield or not, and at last gives itself up to its adversary. And as for the affections, the beginning of this evil is commonly in them. They cross one another and torture the soul with their headlong violence. In this way the rule of the law of grace is cut off by the law of sin, which forces itself on the whole seat of grace’s government. When this is done, sin will make sad work in the soul. The apostle warns believers to beware of this in Rom 6:12: “Therefore do not let sin reign in your+ mortal body so that you+ obey its lusts.” See to it that it does not get the dominion, that it does not usurp rule, not even for a moment. It will labor to push itself onto the throne; watch against it, or a woeful state and condition lies at the door. This, then, goes with this rage and madness of the law of sin: while it prevails, it wholly casts off the rule of the law of grace. The law of grace speaks in the soul, but is not heard; it commands the contrary, but is not obeyed. It cries out, “Do not do this detestable thing” that the Lord hates, but is not regarded. That is, it is not regarded so far as to be able to put a present stop to the rage of sin and recover its own rule, which God in his own time restores to it by the power of his Spirit dwelling in us.

Fearlessness and Contempt of Danger

2. Secondly, madness or rage goes with fearlessness and contempt of danger. It takes away the power of consideration, and all the influence consideration ought to have on the soul. That is why sinners who are wholly under the power of this rage are said “to rush at God, at the thick bosses of his shield” (Job 15:26), the very thing with which he is armed for their utter ruin. They despise the worst he can do to them, being secretly resolved to carry out their lusts even if it costs them their souls. A few considerations will make this clearer to us.

(1.) First, often, when the soul has broken loose from the power of renewing grace, God deals with it to keep it within bounds by preventing grace. So the Lord declares he will deal with Israel (Hos 2:6), as if he said: Since you have rejected me, I will take another course with you. I will lay obstacles before you, so that you will not be able to go on where the fury of your lusts would drive you. He will set before them, from outside, what will obstruct them in their progress.

(2.) Secondly, these hindrances that God lays in the way of sinners, as will be declared at length later, are of two sorts.

[1.] First, rational considerations taken from the consequences of the sin and evil the soul is being urged to and is troubled with. Such are the fear of death, judgment, and hell, and of falling into the hands of the living God, who is a consuming fire. While a man is under the power of the law of the Spirit of life, “Christ’s love constrains him” (2 Cor 5:14). The principle of his doing good and abstaining from evil is “faith, expressed through love,” together with following Christ because of the sweet fragrance of his name. But now, when this blessed “easy yoke” is thrown off for a time, as was shown before, God sets a hedge of terror before the soul. He reminds it of death and the judgment to come, flashes the flames of hell-fire in its face, and fills the soul with the consideration of all the evil consequences of sin, to deter it from its purpose. To this end he makes use of all the threatenings recorded in the law and the gospel. Under this head also may be placed all the considerations that may be taken from temporal things, such as shame, reproach, scandal, punishments, and the like. By the consideration of these things, I say, God sets a hedge before them.

[2.] Secondly, the Lord uses providential dealings to the same purpose, and these are of two sorts.

1st. First, such as are suited to work on the soul and cause it to stop and give up its lustings and pursuit of sin. Such are afflictions and mercies. Isa 57:17: “I was enraged … so I struck him”; I showed my displeasure at their ways by afflictions. So also Hos 2:9, 11, 12. “God chastens men with pain” on their bodies, he says in Job, “to turn them from their purpose and to hide sin from them” (Job 33:17, 19). And he has other ways of coming to them and touching them, as in their names, relationships, estates and desirable things. Or else he heaps mercies on them, so that they may consider whom they are rebelling against. Perhaps remarkable, distinguishing mercies are made their portion for many days.

2nd. Secondly, such as actually hinder the soul from pursuing sin, though it is resolved to do so. We must consider later the various ways in which God does this.

These, I say, are the ways in which the soul is dealt with after the law of indwelling sin has thrown off for a time the influencing power of the law of grace. But now, when lust rises up to rage or madness, it will also despise all of these, even the rod and the One who ordained it. It will rush on shame, reproaches, wrath, and whatever may befall it. That is, though these are set before it, it will venture on them all. Rage and madness are fearless. And it does this in two ways.

(1.) First, it takes possession of the mind, so that it does not allow the consideration of these things to rest on it, but makes the thoughts of them slight and fleeting. Or if the mind forces itself to consider them, sin still comes between the mind and the affections, so that they are not influenced by it in any proportion to what is required. The soul in such a condition will be able to consider such things and not be moved by them at all. And where they do prevail for a time, yet they are imperceptibly worn off the heart again.

(2.) Secondly, by secret, stubborn resolutions to venture everything on the way it is in.

And this is the second branch of this evidence of the power of sin, taken from the opposition it makes to the law of grace, as it were by way of force, strength and violence. The consideration of its deceit follows next.

Chapter VIII

Indwelling sin proved powerful from its deceit. Proved to be deceitful. The general nature of deceit. Jas 1:14 explained. How the mind is drawn away from its duty by the deceitfulness of sin. The principal duties of the mind in our obedience. The ways and means by which it is turned from them.

The second part of the evidence of the power of sin from its manner of working is taken from its deceitfulness. In its working it adds deceit to power. Where power and deceit are combined, the efficacy of that combination must be great, especially when it is helped and assisted by all the ways and means already dwelt on. All who value their souls must carefully watch against it.

Before we come to show what the nature of this deceitfulness of sin consists in, and how it prevails by it, some testimonies will briefly be given to the thing itself, and some light given on its general nature.

That sin, indwelling sin, is deceitful, we have the express testimony of the Holy Spirit (Heb 3:13): “Take care that none of you+ is hardened by sin’s deceitfulness.” It is deceitful. Beware of it, watch against it, or it will produce its utmost effect in hardening the heart against God. It is on account of sin that the heart is said to be “deceitful above all things” (Jer 17:9). Take a man in other things, and, as Job says, though he “would be wise and crafty, he is like the colt of a wild donkey” (Job 11:12): a poor, vain, empty nothing. But consider his heart on account of this law of sin, and it is crafty and deceitful above all things. “They are skilled in doing evil,” says the prophet, but they do not know how to do good (Jer 4:22). The apostle speaks to the same purpose in Eph 4:22: “the old man, which is being corrupted by its deceitful lusts.” Every lust, which is a branch of this law of sin, is deceitful. And where there is poison in every stream, the fountain must be corrupt. No particular lust has any deceit in it except what is passed on to it from this fountain of all actual lust, this law of sin. And the coming of the man of sin is said to be in and with “the deception of unrighteousness” (2 Thess 2:10). Unrighteousness is generally condemned and spoken against among men, so it is not easy to see how any man could make use of it to gain a reputation. But there is a deceptiveness in it, by which the minds of men are turned aside from a proper consideration of it, as we will show later. And so the account the apostle gives of those who are under the power of sin is that they are “deceived” (Titus 3:3). And the life of evil men is nothing but “deceiving and being deceived” (2 Tim 3:13). So we have sufficient testimony given to this quality of the enemy we have to deal with: he is deceitful. Of all things, this consideration puts the mind of man at a loss in dealing with an adversary. He knows he can have no security against one who is deceitful except by standing on his own guard and defense all his days.

To show further the strength and advantage that sin has by its deceit, we may observe that the Scripture for the most part places deceit as the head and spring of every sin, as though no sin followed except where deceit went before.
