So it is in 1 Tim 2:13, 14. The reason the apostle gives why Adam, though he was formed first, was not first in the transgression, is that he was not first deceived. The woman, though made last, was deceived first, and so she was first in the sin. Even that first sin began in deceit, and until the mind was deceived, the soul was safe. Eve, therefore, truly expressed the matter in Gen 3:13, though she did not do it to a good end. “The serpent deceived me,” she says, “and I ate.” She thought to lessen her own crime by laying the charge on the serpent. And this was a new fruit of the sin she had thrown herself into. But the fact of the matter was true: she was deceived before she ate, and deceit went before the transgression. And the apostle shows that sin and Satan still take the same course (2 Cor 11:3). There is, he says, the same way of working toward actual sin as there was of old. Deceiving and beguiling go before, and sin, which is its actual accomplishment, follows after. That is why the devil does all the great works he does in the world, to stir men up to oppose the Lord Jesus Christ and his kingdom, by deceit (Rev 12:9): “the devil … the deceiver of the whole world.” It would be utterly impossible for men to be persuaded to remain in his service, carrying out his designs to their eternal ruin, and sometimes their temporal ruin too, if they were not exceedingly deceived. See also Rev 20:10.

That is why we are given so many warnings to take heed that we are not deceived, if we would take heed that we do not sin. See Eph 5:6; 1 Cor 6:9; 1 Cor 15:33; Gal 6:7; Luke 21:8. From all these testimonies we may learn the influence that deceit has on sin, and so the advantage the law of sin has for putting forth its power by its deceitfulness. Where it succeeds in deceiving, it does not fail to bring forth its fruit.

The ground of this efficacy of sin by deceit is taken from the faculty of the soul that is affected by it. Deceit, strictly speaking, affects the mind; it is the mind that is deceived. When sin attempts any other way of entrance into the soul, as by the affections, the mind keeps its right and sovereignty and is able to check and control it. But where the mind is tainted, sin’s prevailing power must be great. For the mind, or understanding, is the leading faculty of the soul. What the mind fixes on, the will and affections rush after, since they are capable of no consideration except what the mind presents to them. That is why, though the entanglement of the affections in sin is often most troublesome, the deceit of the mind is always most dangerous. This is because of the place the mind holds in the soul with regard to all its workings. Its office is to guide, direct, choose, and lead. And if the light that is in us is darkness, how great is that darkness!

This will appear further if we consider the nature of deceit in general. It consists in presenting things to the soul, or mind, otherwise than they are, either in their nature, causes, effects, or present relation to the soul. This is the general nature of deceit, and it prevails in many ways. It hides what ought to be seen and considered. It conceals circumstances and consequences. It presents what is not, or presents things as they are not, as we will show later in particular. It was shown before that Satan beguiled and deceived our first parents; that is the term the Holy Spirit gives to his temptation and seduction. And the Scripture relates how he deceived them (Gen 3:4, 5). He did it by representing things otherwise than they were. The fruit was desirable; that was plain to the eye. Satan takes advantage of this to suggest secretly that what God aimed at in forbidding them to eat of it was merely to cut short their happiness. He hides from them that it was a trial of their obedience, and that certain, though not immediate, ruin would follow upon their eating it. He puts forward only the present advantage of knowledge, and so presents the whole case to them quite otherwise than it really was. This is the nature of deceit. It is a representation of a matter under disguises. It hides what is undesirable and puts forward what is really not in the matter, so that the mind may make a false judgment of it. In this way Jacob deceived Isaac by his brother’s clothing and the skins on his hands and neck.

Again, deceit has an advantage from the way of managing things that is inseparable from it. It is always carried on by degrees, little by little, so that the whole of the design and aim in hand is not revealed all at once. That is how Satan dealt in that great deceit just mentioned. He goes on in it by steps and degrees. First he removes an objection and tells them they will not die. Then he sets before them the good of knowledge and their being like God by it. To hide and conceal ends, to go on by steps and degrees, to make use of what has been gained and from there to press on to further effects: this is the true nature of deceit. Stephen tells us that the king of Egypt dealt craftily, or deceitfully, with their kindred (Acts 7:19). We may see how he did it in Exod 1. He did not at first fall to killing and slaying them, but says (v. 10), “Come, let us deal shrewdly with them,” and begins to oppress them. This brings about their bondage (v. 11). Having gained this ground and made them slaves, he goes on to destroy their children (v. 16). He did not fall on them all at once, but by degrees. This may be enough to show in general that sin is deceitful, and the advantages it has by it.

For the way, manner, and progress of sin in working by deceit, we have it fully expressed in Jas 1:14, 15: “Each one is tempted when he is drawn away and enticed by his own lust. Then, when lust has conceived, it brings forth sin; and sin, when it is finished, brings forth death.” Since this place declares the whole of what we aim at in this matter, we must dwell on it in particular.

In the previous verse the apostle shows that men are willing to carry on the old trade that our first parents set up at the entrance of sin, that is, excusing themselves in their sins and casting the occasion and blame of them on others. It is not, they say, from themselves, from their own nature and inclinations or their own designs, that they have committed such and such evils, but merely from their temptations. And if they do not know where to lay the evil of those temptations, they will lay it on God himself rather than go without an excuse or something to lessen their guilt. The apostle rebukes this evil in the hearts of men in verse 13: “When tempted, no one should say, ‘God is tempting me.’ For God cannot be tempted by evil, nor does He tempt anyone.” And to show how just this rebuke is, in the words mentioned he uncovers the true causes of the rise and whole progress of sin. He shows that the whole guilt of it lies on the sinner, and that the whole punishment of it will also be his lot, unless it is graciously prevented.

So, as was said, in these words we have the whole progress of lust, or indwelling sin, by way of subtlety, fraud, and deceit, expressed and marked out by the Holy Spirit. From them we will show the particular ways and means by which it puts forth its power and efficacy in the hearts of men by deceitfulness and subtlety. And we may observe in the words:

1. First, the utmost end aimed at in all the actings of sin, or what it tends to in its own nature. That is death: “sin, when it is finished, brings forth death,” the everlasting death of the sinner. Whatever it claims, this is the end it aims at and tends to. Hiding ends and designs is the principal property of deceit. Sin does this to the utmost. It pleads countless other things, but it never once declares that it aims at the death, the everlasting death, of the soul. And a fixed grasp of this end of every sin is a blessed means of preventing its prevailing power in its way of deceiving or beguiling.

2. Secondly, the general way it acts toward that end is by temptation; “each one is tempted by his own lust.” I do not intend to speak in general of the nature of temptations. That does not belong to our present purpose, and besides, I have done it elsewhere. For now it is enough to observe that the life of temptation lies in deceit. So in the business of sin, to be effectually tempted and to be beguiled or deceived are the same thing. So it was in the first temptation. As was shown before, it is everywhere called the serpent’s beguiling or deceiving: “Eve was deceived by the serpent’s cunning,” that is, he prevailed over her by his temptations. So every man is tempted, that is, every man is beguiled or deceived, by his own lust, or indwelling sin, which we have often declared to be the same thing.

The Degrees of Sin’s Deceit

The degrees by which sin goes on in this work of tempting or deceiving are five. For we showed before that it belongs to the nature of deceit to work by degrees, using its advantage at one step to gain another.

The first of these consists in drawing off, or drawing away: “each one is tempted when he is drawn away by his own lust.”

The second is in enticing: “and enticed.”

The third is in the conception of sin: “when lust has conceived.” When the heart is enticed, then lust conceives in it.

The fourth is the bringing forth of sin in its actual accomplishment: “when lust has conceived, it brings forth sin.” In all this there is a hidden allusion to an adulterous straying from marriage duties, and to conceiving and bringing forth children of whoredom and fornication.

The fifth is the finishing of sin, its completing, the filling up of its measure, by which the end lust first designed is brought about: “sin, when it is finished, brings forth death.” As lust conceiving naturally and necessarily brings forth sin, so sin finished infallibly brings about eternal death.

The first of these relates to the mind, which is drawn off, or drawn away, by the deceit of sin. The second relates to the affections, which are enticed or entangled. The third relates to the will, in which sin is conceived; the consent of the will is the formal conception of actual sin. The fourth relates to the conduct, in which sin is brought forth; it shows itself in the lives and courses of men. The fifth concerns a hardened course in sinning, which finishes, completes, and closes up the whole work of sin, and on which death, or eternal ruin, follows.

I will chiefly consider the first three, in which the main strength of the deceit of sin lies. I do so because in believers, whose state and condition is chiefly set forth for consideration here, God is pleased for the most part graciously to prevent the fourth instance, that is, the bringing forth of actual sins in their conduct. And he always and wholly prevents the last, that is, their becoming hardened in a course of sin until it is finished. What ways God in his grace and faithfulness uses to stifle the conceptions of sin in the womb, and to hinder its actual production in the lives of men, must be spoken of later. We will dwell fully, then, on the first three instances, as those in which believers are chiefly concerned in this matter.

Drawing the Mind Away from Its Duty

The first thing sin is said to do, working by way of deceit, is to draw away, or draw off. So a man is said to be drawn off, or drawn away and diverted, that is, from attending to that course of obedience and holiness which, in opposition to sin and its law, he is bound to attend to with diligence.

Now it is on the mind that this effect of the deceit of sin is worked. The mind, or understanding, as we have shown, is the guiding, leading faculty of the soul. It goes before in discerning, judging, and determining, to make the way of moral actions plain and smooth for the will and affections. It is to the soul what Moses told his father-in-law he might be to the people in the wilderness, eyes to guide them, keeping them from wandering in that desolate place. It is the eye of the soul. Without its guidance the will and affections would wander perpetually in the wilderness of this world, after any object that offered or presented itself to them with the appearance of a present good.

So the first thing sin aims at in its deceitful working is to draw off and divert the mind from carrying out its duty.

Two things belong to the duty of the mind in the special office it has in and concerning the obedience God requires.

1. First, to keep itself and the whole soul in such a frame and posture as will make it ready for all duties of obedience, and watchful against all enticements to the conception of sin.

2. Secondly, in particular, to attend carefully to all particular actions, so that they are performed as God requires, in matter, manner, time, and season, agreeable to his will; and also to head off all particular offers of sin in things forbidden. The whole duty of the mind of a believer consists in these two things. And indwelling sin tries to divert it and draw it off from both of them.

The first of these is the duty of the mind with regard to the general frame and course of the whole soul. Two things may be considered here. First, that it is founded on a proper and constant consideration: 1. of ourselves, of sin and its vileness; 2. of God, of his grace and goodness. And sin labors to draw the mind off from both of these. Secondly, that it consists in attending to those duties that are suited in a special way to head off the working of the law of sin.

1. First, sin tries to draw the mind off from a proper consideration, understanding, and sense of sin’s own vileness and of the danger that goes with it. We will give this as our first instance. A proper and constant consideration of sin ought always to stay with us: of sin in its nature, in all its aggravating circumstances, in its end and tendency, especially as it is represented in the blood and cross of Christ. Jer 2:19: “Consider and realize how evil and bitter it is for you to forsake the Lord your God.” Every sin is a forsaking of the Lord our God. If the heart does not know, if it does not consider, that sin is an evil and bitter thing, evil in itself and bitter in its effects, fruit, and outcome, it will never be secured against it. Besides, the frame of heart most accepted by God in any sinner is the humble, contrite, self-abasing frame. Isa 57:15: “For thus says the One who is high and lifted up, who inhabits eternity, whose name is Holy: ‘I dwell in a high and holy place, and with the contrite and humble in spirit, to restore the spirit of the lowly and revive the heart of the contrite.’” See also Luke 18:13, 14. This is fitting for a sinner; no garment sits so becomingly on him. “Clothe yourselves+ with humility,” says the apostle (1 Pet 5:5). It is what befits us, and it is the only safe frame. He who walks humbly walks safely. This is the design of Peter’s advice in 1 Pet 1:17: “conduct yourselves+ in reverent fear during your+ stay as foreigners.” After he himself had fallen through another frame of mind, he gives this advice to all believers. It is not a bondage, a servile fear, that disquiets and perplexes the soul. It is such a fear as may keep men constantly calling on the Father with regard to the final judgment, so that they may be preserved from sin, which they were in such great danger of. That is what he urges on them: “Since you+ call on a Father who judges each one’s work impartially, conduct yourselves+ in reverent fear during your+ stay as foreigners.” This is the humble frame of soul. And how is it obtained? How is it preserved? In no other way than by a constant, deep awareness of the evil, vileness, and danger of sin. That is how it was produced and kept up in the approved tax collector. “God,” he says, “have mercy on me, a sinner!” A sense of sin kept him humble, and humility opened his way to a testimony of the pardon of sin.

And by grace this is the great preservative from sin, as we have an example in Joseph (Gen 39:9). Under the pressure of his great temptation, he falls back at once into this frame of spirit. “How,” he says, “could I do this great evil and sin against God?” A constant, steady sense of the evil of sin gives him such preservation that he risks liberty and life in opposing it. To fear sin is to fear the Lord. So the holy man tells us that they are the same (Job 28:28): “The fear of the Lord, that is wisdom, and to turn away from evil is understanding.”

So this, first of all and in general, is what the law of sin puts forth its deceit about: to draw the mind away from this frame, which is the strongest fort of the soul’s defense and security. It labors to divert the mind from a proper understanding of the vileness, abomination, and danger of sin. It secretly and imperceptibly slips in lessening, excusing, extenuating thoughts of it. Or it draws the mind off from pondering it, from dwelling on it in its thoughts as much as it ought and as much as it once did. Suppose the heart of a man has been made tender, soft, and deeply aware of sin through the word, Spirit, and grace of Christ. If afterward, on any account or by any means whatever, it comes to have less, fewer, slighter, or less moving thoughts of sin or about it, then the mind of that man is drawn away by the deceitfulness of sin.

The Abuse of Gospel Grace

There are two ways among others by which the law of sin tries deceitfully to draw the mind off from this duty, and from the frame that follows from it.

1. First, it does it by a horrible abuse of gospel grace. In the gospel a remedy is provided against the whole evil of sin: its filth, its guilt, and all its dangerous consequences. It is the doctrine of the deliverance of the souls of men from sin and death, a revelation of the gracious will of God toward sinners by Jesus Christ. Now what is the true tendency of this doctrine, of this revelation of grace? And what ought we to use it for, and turn it to? The apostle declares this in Titus 2:11, 12: “For the grace of God has appeared, bringing salvation to everyone. It instructs us to renounce ungodliness and worldly lusts, and to live sensible, upright, and godly lives in the present age.” This is what it teaches; this is what we ought to learn from it and by it. That is why universal holiness is called “a manner of life that befits the gospel” (Phil 1:27). It befits the gospel as what answers to its end, aim, and design, as what the gospel requires and what it ought to be turned to. And accordingly it produces this effect where its word is received and preserved in a saving light (Rom 12:2; Eph 4:20, 21, 22, 23, 24). But here the deceit of sin puts itself in between. It separates the doctrine of grace from its use and end. It stops at its notions and intercepts its influence in its proper application. From the doctrine of the assured pardon of sin, it slips in a disregard of sin. God in Christ lays down the premise, and Satan and sin draw the conclusion. For the apostle shows that the deceitfulness of sin is apt to plead for disregarding sin on the ground of the grace of God by which it is pardoned. He shows it in his rebuke and abhorrence of such a suggestion (Rom 6:1): “What then shall we say? Shall we continue in sin so that grace may increase? Certainly not!” Men’s deceitful hearts, he says, are apt to draw that conclusion, but far be it from us to give it any welcome. Yet Jude declares (v. 4) that some have plainly made use of that deceit to their own eternal ruin: “ungodly ones … who turn the grace of our God into a license for immorality.” And we have had dreadful instances of it in the days of temptation in which we have lived.

Indeed, much of the wisdom of faith and the power of gospel grace lies in opposing this deceit. When the mind is fully possessed with the notion and doctrine of gospel truth about the full and free forgiveness of all sins in the blood of Christ, and cast habitually and firmly into its mold, then to be able to keep the heart always in a deep, humbling sense of sin, in abhorrence of it and self-abasement for it, is a great effect of gospel wisdom and grace. This is the trial and touchstone of gospel light. If it keeps the heart aware of sin, humble, lowly, and broken on that account; if it teaches us to water a free pardon with tears, to detest forgiven sin, to watch diligently for the ruin of that which we are nevertheless assured will never ruin us, then it is divine, from above, from the Spirit of grace. If it secretly and imperceptibly makes men loose and light in their thoughts about sin, it is adulterated, selfish, and false. If it will be everything and answer every end, it is nothing.

So it comes about that at times we see men walking in a bondage frame of spirit all their days, low in their light and poor in their understanding of grace, so that it is hard to tell to which covenant they belong in their principles, whether they are under the law or under grace. Yet they walk with a more conscientious tenderness about sinning than many who have advanced to higher degrees of light and knowledge than they. It is not that the saving light of the gospel is not the only principle of saving holiness and obedience. It is that through the deceitfulness of sin this light is abused in various ways, to favor the soul in many neglects of duties, and to draw the mind off from a proper consideration of the nature, ill desert, and danger of sin. And this is done in several ways.

(1.) First, the soul often needs relief by gospel grace against a sense of the guilt of sin and the accusation of the law. So at length it comes to make this a common and ordinary thing, something that may be done lightly. It has found a good medicine for its wounds, one whose efficacy it has experienced, and it comes to apply it lightly, so that it skins over its sores rather than cures them. A little less earnestness, a little less diligence serves each time, until perhaps the soul begins to assure itself of pardon as a matter of course. And this tends directly to draw the mind off from its constant and universal watchfulness against sin. Suppose a man’s light has made his way of access plain for obtaining pardon. Unless he is very watchful, he is far more apt to become superficial, formal, and careless in his work than the man who, because of mists and darkness, has to search about to find his way rightly to the throne of grace. A man who has often traveled a road passes on without paying attention or asking the way. But a man who is a stranger to it notices every turning and asks every passerby, and so makes his journey safer than the other does.

(2.) Secondly, the deceitfulness of sin takes advantage of the doctrine of grace, by many ways and means, to extend the bounds of the soul’s liberty beyond what God has assigned to it. Some have never thought themselves free from a legal bondage frame until they have been brought to the borders of sensuality, and some into its depths. How often sin will plead: “This strictness, this exactness, this anxious care is in no way necessary. Relief is provided in the gospel against such things. Would you live as though there were no need of the gospel? As though the pardon of sin were to no purpose?” But we will have occasion to speak more of these pleas of sin from gospel grace later, in particular.

(3.) Thirdly, in times of temptation this deceitfulness of sin will argue expressly for sin from gospel grace. At least it will plead for these two things.

[1.] First, that there is no need for such a tenacious and severe contending against sin as the principle of the new creature is set on. If it cannot divert the soul or mind wholly from attending to temptations so as to oppose them, it will still try to draw them off with regard to the manner of their attending. They need not use the diligence that the soul at first thinks necessary.

[2.] Secondly, it will keep offering relief about the outcome of sin: that it will not turn to the ruin or destruction of the soul, because it is, or will be, or may be pardoned by the grace of the gospel. And this is true. This is the great and only relief of the soul against sin whose guilt it has already contracted, the blessed and only remedy for a guilty soul. But when it is pleaded and called to mind by the deceitfulness of sin, in compliance with a temptation to sin, then it is poison. Poison is mixed into every drop of this balm, to the danger, if not the death, of the soul. This is the first way by which the deceitfulness of sin draws the mind off from properly attending to that sense of sin’s vileness which alone is able to keep the mind in the humble, self-abased frame that is acceptable to God. It makes the mind careless, as though its work were needless because grace abounds. This is like a soldier who neglects his post because he trusts in a reserve, when that reserve is provided only on the condition that he keeps his own proper place.

2. Secondly, in this matter of drawing the mind off from a proper sense of sin, sin takes advantage to work by its deceit from the state and condition of men in the world. I will give only one instance of how it goes about this. In their younger days men naturally have affections that are quicker, more vigorous and active, and more perceptibly at work in them than afterward. As to their felt working and operation, the affections naturally decay, and many things befall men in their lives that take off their edge and keenness. But as men lose in their affections, they grow and improve in their understandings, resolutions, and judgments, unless they are besotted with sensuality or with the corruption that is in the world through lust. So if what once had a place in their affections does not take a place in their minds and judgments, they lose those things entirely; such things have no more place in their souls. So men have no regard for, indeed they utterly despise, the things their affections were set on with delight and greed in their childhood. But if those things have by some means come to be fixed in their minds and judgments, men keep a high esteem for them and cling to them as closely as they did when their affections were more vigorous. It is only as if those things have changed their seat in the soul. It is the same in spiritual things. The first and chief seat of the sense of sin is in the affections. As the affections are great and large in natural youth, so they are in spiritual youth (Jer 2:2): “I remember the devotion of your youth, your love as a bride.” Besides, such persons have newly come out of their conviction of sin, in which they have been cut to the heart and so made tender. Whatever touches a wound is felt thoroughly. So it is with the guilt of sin before the wound given by conviction is thoroughly healed. But now, when the affections begin to decay naturally, they begin to decay also in their felt actings and stirrings in spiritual things. Though the affections improve in grace, they may decay in feeling. At least, spiritual feeling is not rooted in the affections, but is only passed on to them. Now in these decays, unless the soul takes care to fix a deep sense of sin on the mind and judgment, so that it may continually affect the heart and affections, that sense will decay. And here the deceit of the law of sin puts itself in. It lets the sense of sin decay in the affections, and it diverts the mind from holding a proper, constant, fixed consideration of sin. We may consider this a little in persons who never make any progress in the ways of God beyond conviction. How aware of sin they will be for a season! How they will mourn and weep then under a sense of its guilt! How heartily and sincerely they will resolve against it! Their affections are vigorous and, as it were, rule in their souls. But they are like a plant that will flourish for a day or two with watering, though it has no root. For a while later we see that the more experience these men have had of sin, the less they fear it, as the wise man suggests (Eccl 8:11). At length they come to be the greatest despisers of sin in the world. There is no sinner like the man who has sinned away his convictions of sin. What is the reason for this? In their convictions the sense of sin was fixed on their affections. As it decayed there, they took no care to have it fixed deeply and graciously on their minds. The deceitfulness of sin robbed them of this, and so ruined their souls. In some measure it is the same with believers. As the feeling of the affections decays, as they grow heavy and dull, great decays will follow, unless great wisdom and grace are used to fix a proper sense of sin on the mind and judgment, which may provoke, excite, enliven, and stir up the affections every day. At first sorrow, trouble, grief, and fear affected the mind and would give it no rest. If afterward the mind does not affect the heart with sorrow and grief, the whole will be cast out, and the soul will be in danger of being hardened. These are some of the ways by which the deceit of sin diverts the mind from the first part of its safe and preserving frame, or draws it off from its constant watchfulness against sin and all its effects.

The Holy Consideration of God and His Grace

The second part of this general duty of the mind is to keep the soul in a constant, holy consideration of God and his grace. This plainly lies at the spring of gospel obedience. The way by which sin draws the mind off from this part of its duty is open and well enough known, though it is not watched against well enough. The Scripture everywhere declares it to be the filling of men’s minds with earthly things. It sets this in direct opposition to the heavenly frame of the mind, which is the spring of gospel obedience (Col 3:2): “Set your+ affections on things above, not on earthly things,” or set your+ minds. It is as if he had said, “You+ cannot be set or fixed on both together, so as to mind them both principally and chiefly.” And the affections toward the one and the other, which come from these different principles of minding the one or the other, are set against each other as directly inconsistent (1 John 2:15): “Do not love the world or anything in the world. If anyone loves the world, the love of the Father is not in him.” And actions in a course that suits these affections are also set forth as contrary: “You+ cannot serve both God and money.” These are two masters whom no man can serve at the same time to the satisfaction of both. Every inordinate minding of earthly things, then, is opposed to the frame in which our minds ought to be fixed on God and his grace in a course of gospel obedience.

There are several ways by which the deceitfulness of sin draws the mind off in this particular. But the chief of them is by pressing these things on the mind as things that are lawful, and perhaps necessary. So all those in the parable who excused themselves from coming in to the wedding feast of the gospel did it on the ground that they were engaged in their lawful callings: one with his farm, another with his oxen, the means by which he plowed in this world. By this plea the minds of men were drawn off from the frame of heavenliness required for our walking with God, and the rules of not loving the world, or of using it as though we did not use it, are neglected because of it. It is not my present business to declare what wisdom, what watchfulness, what serious and frequent trial and examination of ourselves is required to keep our hearts and minds in a heavenly frame in the use and pursuit of earthly things. This much is evident: the engine by which the deceit of sin draws off and turns aside the mind in this matter is the pretense of the lawfulness of the things it would have the mind occupy itself with. And very few are armed against this with sufficient diligence, wisdom, and skill. This is the first and most general attempt that indwelling sin makes on the soul by deceit. It draws the mind away from diligently attending to its course, in a proper sense of the evil of sin and a proper and constant consideration of God and his grace.

Chapter IX

The deceit of sin in drawing the mind away from properly attending to special duties of obedience, shown in meditation and prayer.

We have shown how sin by its deceit tries to draw the mind away from attending to that holy frame in walking with God in which the soul ought to be preserved. Let us now go on to show how it does the same work with regard to those special duties by which its designs, workings, and prevailing power may in a special way be headed off and prevented. Sin indeed keeps up an enmity against all duties of obedience, or rather against God in them. “When I want to do good,” says the apostle, “evil is present with me.” Whenever I want to do good, or whatever good I want to do, good that is spiritual, good with regard to God, sin is present with me to hinder me from it and to oppose me in it. On the other side, all duties of obedience lie directly against the actings of the law of sin. For as the flesh in all its actings lusts against the Spirit, so the Spirit in all its actings lusts against the flesh. And so every duty performed in the strength and grace of the Spirit is contrary to the law of sin. Rom 8:13: “if by the Spirit you+ put to death the deeds of the flesh.” The actings of the Spirit of grace in duties do this work. These two are contrary. But there are some duties which, in their own nature and by God’s appointment, have a special influence on weakening and subduing the whole law of sin in its very principles and its chief strengths. These are the duties the mind of a believer ought to attend to principally in his whole course. And these are the duties sin in its deceit tries principally to draw the mind away from. In diseases of the body, they say, some remedies have a specific quality against particular disorders. So in this disease of the soul there are some duties that have a special power against this sinful disorder. I will not dwell on many of them, but will give only two as instances, which seem to me to be of this nature: that is, by God’s design they have a special tendency toward the ruin of the law of sin. Then we will show the ways, methods, and means the law of sin uses to divert the mind from properly attending to them. Now these duties are, first, prayer, especially private prayer, and secondly, meditation. I put them together because they agree closely in their general nature and end, and differ only in the manner of performing them. For by meditation I mean meditating on how the word and our own hearts stand related and suited to each other, with this end, that our hearts may be brought into a more exact conformity to the word. It is our pondering the truth as it is in Jesus, to find its image and likeness in our own hearts. So it has the same intent as prayer, which is to bring our souls into a frame that answers in all things to the mind and will of God. They are like the blood and the spirits in the veins, which have the same life, motion, and use. But people are generally at a great loss in this duty of meditation. So, having declared it to be of such great efficacy for controlling the actings of the law of sin, I will briefly give in passing two or three rules to direct believers to the right performance of this great duty. They are these.

1. First, meditate on God with God. That is, when we would take up thoughts and meditations of God, of his excellencies, his attributes, his glory, his majesty, his love, his goodness, let it be done by way of speaking to God, in a deep humbling and abasement of our souls before him. This will fix the mind, and lead it on from one thing to another to give glory to God in a proper way. It will affect the soul until it is brought into that holy wonder at God and delight in him which is acceptable to him. My meaning is that it should be done by way of prayer and praise, speaking to God.

2. Secondly, meditate on the word in the word. That is, in reading it, consider the sense of the particular passages we dwell on, looking to God for help, guidance, and direction in discovering his mind and will in them. Then labor to have our hearts affected by it.

3. Thirdly, whatever we fall short of in evenness and constancy of thought in these things, let it be made up in frequency. Some are discouraged because their minds do not steadily supply them with thoughts to carry on their meditations, through the weakness or imperfection of their powers of invention. Let this be made up by frequent returns of the mind to the subject set out for meditation; in this way new senses of it will keep being supplied. But this is in passing.

These duties, I say, among others, make a special opposition to the very being and life of indwelling sin; or rather faith in them does. (We have chosen them only as an instance, not excluding some other duties from the same place, office, and usefulness.) They are continually designing its utter ruin. So, in pursuing our present purpose with this instance, I will do these two things.

(1.) First, show the suitableness and usefulness of this duty, or these duties (as I will treat them together), for ruining sin.

(2.) Secondly, show the means by which the deceitfulness of sin tries to draw the mind away from properly attending to them.

The Usefulness of Prayer against Sin

For the first, observe:

1. First, it is the proper work of the soul in this duty to consider all the secret workings and actings of sin: what advantages it has gained, what temptations it is joined with, what harm it has already done, and what further harm it is ready to do. That is why David gives this title to one of his prayers, Ps 102: “A prayer of one who is afflicted, when he grows faint and pours out his lament before the Lord.” I speak of the prayer that goes with a proper consideration of all the needs, straits, and emergencies of the soul. Without this, prayer is not prayer. That is, whatever show or appearance of that duty it has, it is of no use either to the glory of God or to the good of men’s souls. It is a cloud without water, driven by the wind of men’s breath. And no more ready and effectual poison for souls was ever found than binding them to a constant form and use of I know not what words in their prayers and supplications, words they themselves do not understand. Bind men in this way in their trades or their business in this world, and they will quickly find the effect of it. By this means they are made unable to consider properly what is good for them at present or what is evil to them. And without that, what use can prayer serve, except to mock God and delude men’s own souls? But in the kind of prayer we are speaking of, the Spirit of God comes in to give us his help, and in this very matter of finding out and uncovering the most secret actings and workings of the law of sin. Rom 8:26: “We do not know what we ought to pray for … the Spirit helps us in our weakness.” He shows us our needs, and where we chiefly need help and relief. And we find by daily experience that in prayer believers are led into such revelations and convictions of the secret, deceitful work of sin in their hearts as no considerations could ever have led them into. So it was with David in Ps 51. He set out to confess his actual sin, and in his prayer his wound was searched by the skillful hand of the Spirit of God. Then the root of all his failures was revealed to him in his original corruption (v. 5). In this duty the Spirit is like the “lamp of the Lord” to the soul, enabling it to search all its inmost parts. He gives a holy, spiritual light to the mind, enabling it to search the deep and dark recesses of the heart and to find out the subtle and deceitful schemes, fictions, and imaginations of the law of sin there. Whatever notion there is of sin, whatever power and prevailing force in it, it is laid hold of, seized, brought into the presence of God, judged, condemned, and mourned over. And what could possibly be more effectual for its ruin and destruction? For together with its discovery, the soul applies itself to all the relief provided in Jesus Christ against it, all the ways and means by which it may be ruined. That is why it is the duty of the mind “to be watchful in prayer” (1 Pet 4:7): to attend diligently to the state of our souls, and to deal fervently and effectually with God about it. The same may also be said of meditation, wisely managed to its proper end.

2. Secondly, in this duty a deep, full sense of the vileness of sin is worked on the heart, with a constantly renewed detestation of it; and if anything tends to its ruin, this undoubtedly does. This is one design of prayer, one end of the soul in it: to draw sin out, to set it in order, and to present it to itself in its vileness, abomination, and aggravating circumstances, so that it may be loathed, abhorred, and cast away as a filthy thing (as in Isa 30:22). He who pleads with God for the remission of sin also pleads with his own heart for its detestation (Hos 14:3). In this also sin is judged in the name of God. For in its confession the soul puts its name to God’s detestation of sin, and to the sentence of his law against it. There is indeed a round of these duties that persons convicted of sin give themselves up to, as a mere cover for their lusts. They cannot sin quietly unless they perform their duty constantly. But the prayer we speak of is a thing of another nature. It will allow no compromise with sin, much less will it serve the ends of sin’s deceit, as that other formal prayer does. It will not be bribed into a secret compliance with any of the enemies of God or of the soul, no, not for a moment. That is why often in this duty the heart is raised to the most sincere and effectual sense of sin, and detestation of it, that the soul ever attains in its whole course of obedience. And this plainly tends also to the weakening and ruin of the law of sin.

3. Thirdly, this is the way God has appointed and blessed for obtaining strength and power against sin. Jas 1:5: “If any of you+ lacks …, he should ask God.” Prayer is the way of obtaining from God by Christ a supply of all our needs, and help against all opposition, especially the opposition made against us by sin. This, I suppose, need not be dwelt on; it is clear to every believer, both in its notion and in practice. It is the means by which we call, and on which the Lord Jesus comes in to our aid with suitable help in a time of need (Heb 2:17).

4. Fourthly, faith in prayer countermines all the workings of the deceit of sin. That is because in prayer the soul constantly binds itself to God to oppose all sin whatever. Ps 119:106: “I have sworn and confirmed that I will keep Your righteous judgments.” This is the language of every gracious soul when it comes before God. Its inmost parts bind themselves to God, to cling to him in all things and to oppose sin in all things. He who cannot do this cannot pray. To pray in any other frame is to flatter God with our lips, which he abhors. And this helps a believer exceedingly in pursuing sin to its ruin. For:

(1.) First, if there is any secret lust lurking in the heart, he will find it either rising up against this engagement or using its tricks to keep itself safe from it. By this the lust is discovered, and the heart’s conviction of its evil is furthered and strengthened. Sin reveals itself most certainly, and never more plainly than when it is most severely pursued. Lusts in men are compared to harmful and noxious beasts, or men themselves are, because of their lusts (Isa 11:4–6). Now such beasts keep to their dens and hiding places, and never show themselves, at least never so much in their own nature and rage, as when they are most earnestly pursued. So it is with sin and corruption in the heart.

(2.) Secondly, if any sin is prevailing in the soul, it will weaken the soul and draw it back from the universality of this engagement to God. It will breed a shrinking back from it, a lightness in it. Now when this is noticed, it will greatly awaken a gracious soul and stir it up to look about it. A spontaneous lassitude, that is, a causeless weariness and indisposition of the body, is looked on as the sign of an approaching fever or some dangerous disorder, and it stirs men up to take timely and vigorous measures, so that they are not seized by it. So it is in this case. When the soul of a believer finds in itself an indisposition to make fervent, sincere engagements of universal holiness to God, it knows there is some prevailing disorder in it. It finds where the disorder lies and sets itself against it.

(3.) Thirdly, while the soul can constantly bind itself to God in this way, it is certain that sin can rise to no ruinous prevailing power. Indeed, it is a conquest over sin, a most considerable conquest, when the soul comes through such an engagement fully and clearly, without any secret reserve, with eagerness and resolution, as in Ps 18:23. And upon such a success it may triumph in the grace of God, and have good hope through faith that it will have a final conquest, and that what it so resolves will be done; that it has decreed a thing, and it will be established. And this tends to the defeat, indeed to the ruin, of the law of sin.

(4.) Fourthly, unless the heart is deceived by cursed hypocrisy, this engagement to God will greatly move it to a special diligence and watchfulness against all sin. There is no greater evidence of hypocrisy than to have a heart like the adulterous woman (Prov 7:14), to say, “I have paid my vows; now I may give myself to my sin.” Or to be careless about sin, as being satisfied that it has prayed against it. It is otherwise in a gracious soul. The sense and conscience of engagements against sin made to God make it watchful in every way against all sin’s impulses and workings. On these and various other accounts faith in this duty puts itself forth in a special way, to weaken the power and stop the progress of the law of sin.

How Sin Draws the Mind Off from Prayer

If, then, the mind is diligent in its watch and charge to preserve the soul from the efficacy of sin, it will carefully attend to this duty and to its proper performance, since it is of such singular advantage to the mind’s end and purpose. So here sin puts forth its deceit in its own defense. It labors to divert and draw the mind away from attending to this duty and others like it. Among others, there are three engines, three ways and means, by which it tries to accomplish its design.

1. First, it takes advantage of the weariness this duty brings to the flesh. As has been shown, there is an aversion in the law of sin to all immediate communion with God. Now this duty is such communion. Nothing goes with it by which the carnal part of the soul may be gratified or satisfied, as there may be something of that kind in most public duties, in most things a man can do beyond pure acts of faith and love. So since no relief or advantage comes by it except what is purely spiritual, it becomes wearisome and burdensome to flesh and blood. It is like traveling alone without companion or diversion, which makes the way seem long, but brings the traveler to his journey’s end with most speed. So our Savior declares. When he expected that his disciples, in keeping with their duty and their present distress, would have been engaged in this work, he found them “fast asleep” (Matt 26:41). “The spirit,” he says, “is willing, but the flesh is weak.” And out of that weakness grew their indisposition toward their duty and their weariness of it. So God complains of his people in Isa 43:22: “You have grown weary of Me.” And at length it may come to the height mentioned in Mal 1:13: “You+ also say, ‘Oh, what a weariness!’ And you+ sniff at it, says the Lord of Hosts.” The Jews suppose it was the language of men when they brought their offerings or sacrifices on their shoulders. They claimed these wearied them, and they panted and puffed like men ready to faint under them, when they brought only the torn, the lame, and the sick. But this duty is often like that to the flesh. And the deceitfulness of sin makes use of this to draw the heart away by imperceptible degrees from a constant attending to it. It puts in a plea for the relief of the weak and weary flesh. In this matter there is a collusion between spiritual flesh and natural flesh. They help each other, and an aversion to this duty is the effect of their collusion. So it was with the bride in Song 5:2, 3. She was asleep, drowsing in her spiritual condition, and she pleads her natural unfitness to rouse herself from that state. If the mind is not diligently watchful to prevent suggestions from this quarter, if it does not dwell constantly on the considerations that show that attending to this duty is indispensable, if it does not stir up the principle of grace in the heart to keep its rule and sovereignty and not to be trifled with by foolish pretenses, it will be drawn away, which is the effect sin aims at.

2. Secondly, the deceitfulness of sin makes use of corrupt reasonings taken from the pressing and urgent business of life. “If we attended strictly to all duties of this kind,” it says in the heart, “we would neglect our chief business and be useless to ourselves and others in the world.” And on this general ground, particular pieces of business push particular duties out of their proper place and time. Men have no leisure to glorify God and save their own souls. It is certain that God gives us time enough for all that he requires of us of any kind in this world. No duties need to jostle one another, I mean constantly. Special occasions must be decided according to special circumstances. But if in anything we take more on ourselves than we have time to perform well without robbing God, and our own souls, of what is due to them, God does not call us to it, and he does not bless us in it. It would be more tolerable for our duties of holiness and regard to God to encroach on the duties of our callings and employments in this world than the other way around. And yet God does not require even this of us in an ordinary way or course. How little, then, will he bear with what is plainly so much worse on every account whatever! But through the deceitfulness of sin, this is how the souls of men are beguiled. By several degrees they are at length driven from their duty.

3. Thirdly, it works on the mind to draw it away from attending to this duty by offering a compensation to be made in and by other duties, as Saul thought to make up for his disobedience by sacrifice. “Might not the same duty performed in public, or in the family, be enough?” And if the soul is so foolish as not to answer, “Those things ought to be done, and this not left undone,” it may be ensnared and deceived. For besides the command to it, that is, that we should personally be watchful in prayer, there are, as has been shown, various advantages against the deceit and efficacy of sin in this duty when it is performed in this way, which the more public attending to it does not have. Sin strives to rob the soul of these by this exchange, which it offers the soul by its corrupt reasonings.

4. Fourthly, I may add here what has a place in all the workings of sin by deceit: its feeding the soul with promises and purposes of attending more diligently to this duty when occasions allow. By this means it brings the soul to say to its convictions of duty what Felix said to Paul: “You may go for now. When I find the time, I will call for you.” And by this means the present season and time, which alone is ours, is often lost beyond recovery.

These are some of the ways and means by which the deceit of sin tries to draw the mind away from properly attending to this duty, which is so specially suited to prevent sin’s progress and prevailing power, and which aims so directly and immediately at its ruin. I might give instances also from other duties with the same tendency. But this may be enough to uncover the nature of this part of the deceit of sin. And this is the first way by which it makes way for further entangling the affections and for the conception of sin. When sin has worked this effect on anyone, he is said to be drawn away, to be diverted from what he ought constantly to attend to in his mind as he walks before the Lord.

And this will teach us to see and discern where the beginning of our declines and failings in the ways of God lies, whether in our general course or in our attending to special duties. This is of great importance and concern to us. When the beginnings and occasions of a sickness or disorder of the body are known, it is a great advantage in directing toward its cure. To call Zion back to himself, God shows her where the beginning of her sin was (Mic 1:13). Now this is for the most part the beginning of sin to us: the drawing of the mind away from properly attending in all things to carrying out its duty. The chief care and charge of the soul lies on the mind. If the mind fails in its duty, the whole is betrayed, either in its general frame or in particular failures. The failing of the mind is like the failing of the watchman in Ezekiel; the whole is lost by his neglect. So in the self-scrutiny and search we are called to, this is what we are to inquire after most diligently. God does not look at what duties we perform by their number and count, or merely by their nature, but at whether we do them with the attention of mind and spirit that he requires. Many men perform duties in a routine or course, and do not so much as think of them, as it were. Their minds are filled with other things; duty merely takes up so much of their time. This is nothing but an attempt to mock God and deceive their own souls. If you+ would take the true measure of yourselves+, then, consider how it is with you+ as to the duty of your+ minds that we have inquired into. Consider whether you+ have not been diverted and drawn away by some of the deceits mentioned. If there are any declines in you+ of any kind, you+ will find that this is where they began. In one way or another your+ minds have been made heedless, careless, slothful, and unsettled, beguiled and drawn away from their duty. Consider the charge in Prov 4:23, 25, 26, 27. Might not such a soul say: “If I had attended more diligently, if I had considered more wisely the vile nature of sin, if I had not allowed my mind to be possessed with vain hopes and foolish imaginations by a cursed abuse of gospel grace, if I had not let it be filled with the things of the world and become careless in attending to special duties, I would not be so sick, weak, unprofitable, wounded, decayed, and defiled as I am this day. My careless, deceived mind has been the beginning of sin and transgression to my soul”? And this discovery will direct the soul to a suitable way of healing and recovery. That will never be brought about by multiplying particular duties, but by restoring the mind (Ps 23:3).

And from this it also appears that the great means of preserving our souls according to the mind and will of God, both in their general frame and in particular duties, is to seek after a sound and steadfast mind. It is a remarkable grace to have the Spirit of power and love and of a sound mind (2 Tim 1:7): a stable, solid, resolved mind in the things of God, not easily moved, diverted, or changed, not drawn aside; a mind not apt to listen to corrupt reasonings, vain suggestions, or pretenses that would draw it away from its duty. This is what the apostle urges believers to in 1 Cor 15:58: “Therefore, my beloved brothers, be steadfast and immovable. Always excel in the work of the Lord.” The steadfastness of our minds, abiding in their duty, is the cause of all our immovability and fruitfulness in obedience. And so Peter tells us that those who are by any means led away, or “enticed, fall from their own steadfastness” (2 Pet 3:17). And the great charge laid against backsliders is that they are “not steadfast” (Ps 78:37): “their spirit was not steadfast.” For if the soul is safe unless the mind is drawn away from its duty, then the soundness and steadfastness of the mind is its great preservative. There are three parts to this steadfastness of the mind. First, a full purpose of clinging to God in all things. Secondly, a daily renewing and quickening of the heart to carry out this purpose. Thirdly, resolutions against all trifling or bargaining about negligence in carrying it out. But these are not to be spoken of here.

Chapter X

The deceit of sin in drawing the mind away from attending to particular duties further uncovered. Several things required in the mind of believers with regard to particular duties of obedience. The actings of sin by way of deceit to divert the mind from them.

We have not yet brought to a conclusion the first way in which the deceit of sin works, that is, in drawing the mind away from carrying out its duty. We dwell on it the longer for two reasons.

1. First, because of its importance and the concern it carries. If the mind is drawn away, if it is tainted, weakened, or turned aside from a proper and strict attending to its charge and office, then the whole soul, will, and affections are certainly entangled and drawn into sin, as has been shown in part and will appear further later. So we ought to give diligent heed to this, which is the design of the apostle’s exhortation in Heb 2:1: “We must pay closer attention, therefore, to what we have heard, so that we do not drift away” from it. What he warns us against is a failure of our minds, through the deceitfulness of sin, by which we lose the life, power, sense, and impression of the word. And there is no way to prevent it except by paying the closest attention to what we have heard, which expresses the whole duty of our minds in attending to obedience.

2. Secondly, because the actings and workings of the mind are spiritual, and so the conscience is not affected by them so as to take proper notice of them, unless it is clearly enlightened and properly roused and stirred up. Conscience is not apt to turn back and act on the mind’s failures, since it chiefly regards the acts of the whole soul. When the affections are entangled with sin (of which more later), or the will begins to conceive it by its express consent, conscience is apt to raise an uproar in the soul and give it no rest or quiet until the soul is reclaimed, or until conscience itself is in one way or another bribed or corrupted. But these neglects of the mind are spiritual, and without very diligent attention they are seldom noticed. In the Scriptures our minds are often called our spirits, as in Rom 1:9: “whom I serve with my spirit.” And they are distinguished from the soul, which chiefly means the affections, in the division made in 1 Thess 5:23: may he sanctify you+ completely, “your+ entire spirit and soul,” that is, your+ mind and affections. It is true that where spirit is used to express spiritual gifts, it is set against our understanding with regard to those gifts (1 Cor 14:15), where understanding is taken for the first act of the mind in a rational perception of things. But when that word is applied to any faculty of our souls, it is the mind that it expresses. Since this, then, is our spirit, its actings are secret and hidden, and cannot be discovered without spiritual wisdom and diligence. Let us not suppose, then, that we dwell too long on this consideration. It is of such great importance to us, and yet so hidden, and we are apt to be so unaware of it; and yet our carefulness in this matter is one of the best evidences we have of our sincerity. Let us not be like a man who feels and complains of a cut finger, but not of a failing of his vital spirits that is leading to death. So under this head of our discourse there remains the consideration of the charge of the mind with regard to particular duties and sins. In considering it we will do these two things.

(1.) First, show what is required in the mind of a believer with regard to particular duties.

(2.) Secondly, declare the way the deceit of sin works to draw the mind away from attending to them. The same will be done also with regard to particular sins and avoiding them.

What the Mind Is to Attend To in Duties

1. First, for the right performance of any duty it is not enough that the thing required is performed. It must also be squared and fitted in every respect to its rule. Here lies the great duty of the mind: to attend to the rule of duties, and to take care that everything concerning them is ordered by it. Our progress in obedience is our edification, or building. Now it does very little to further a building for a man to bring wood and stones and heap them up together without order. They must be hewn and squared and fitted by line and rule if we intend to build. Nor is it of any advantage to our edification in faith and obedience to multiply duties, if we heap them on one another and do not order and arrange them according to rule. That is why God expressly rejects a multitude of duties when they are not suited in every respect to the rule. Isa 1:11: “What good to Me is your+ multitude of sacrifices?” and verse 14: “They have become a burden to Me; I am weary of bearing them.” And so all acceptable obedience is called walking according to a rule (Gal 6:16). It is canonical, that is, regular, obedience. As letters of the alphabet heaped together mean nothing unless they are arranged in their proper order, so neither do our duties mean anything without this arranging. To see that they are arranged so is the great duty of the mind, and it is to attend to it with all diligence. Eph 5:15: Walk circumspectly, exactly, accurately, that is, diligently in all things; take heed to the rule of what you+ do. We walk in duties, but we walk circumspectly in this attention of the mind.

(2.) Secondly, there are some special things the rule directs to, which the mind is to attend to in every duty, such as:

[1.] First, that the duty is full and complete in its matter. Under the law no beast that lacked any member, that had any part missing, was allowed as a sacrifice. Such beasts were rejected as well as those that were lame or blind. Duties must be complete in their parts, their matter. Part of the price may be kept back in such a way as to make the offering of all the rest unacceptable. Saul’s sparing of Agag and the fattest of the cattle made the destroying of all the rest useless. So when men will give alms, or perform other services, but not in the proportion the rule requires, and which the mind might discover by diligently attending to it, the whole duty is spoiled.

[2.] Secondly, as to its principle: that it is done in faith, and so by actually drawing strength from Christ (John 15:5), without whom “we can do nothing.” It is not enough that the person is a believer, though that is necessary for every good work (Eph 2:10). Faith must also be particularly exercised in every duty we do. For our whole obedience is the “obedience of faith” (Rom 1:5), that is, the obedience the doctrine of faith requires, and which the grace of faith bears or brings forth. So Christ is expressly said to be “our life” (Col 3:4), our spiritual life, that is, its spring, author, and cause. Now as in natural life no vital act can be performed except by the actual working of the principle of life itself, so in spiritual life no spiritually vital act, that is, no duty acceptable to God, can be performed except by the actual working of Christ, who is our life. And this is passed on to us in no other way than by faith. So the apostle says in Gal 2:20: “Christ lives in me. The life I now live in the flesh, I live by the faith of the Son of God.” Not only was Christ his life, a living principle in him, but he lived a life, that is, he performed vital actions in all duties of holiness and obedience, by the faith of the Son of God, or faith in him, drawing supplies of grace and strength from him by it. So this is what a believer ought diligently to attend to: that everything he does toward God is done in the strength of Christ. And what that consists in ought to be diligently inquired into by all who intend to walk with God.

[3.] Thirdly, in this regard to the rule, the manner of performing every duty is to be considered. Now there are two things in the manner of performing any duty that a believer, who is entrusted with spiritual light, ought to attend to.

1st. First, that it is done in the way and by the means God has prescribed for the outward manner of its performance. This is especially to be regarded in duties of the worship of God, in which the matter and the outward manner both fall equally under his command. If this is not regarded, the whole duty is spoiled. I do not speak of those who let themselves be deluded by the deceitfulness of sin into utterly disregarding the rule of the word in such things and worshiping God according to their own imaginations. I speak chiefly of those who in general profess to do nothing except what God requires, and as he requires it, and yet do not diligently attend to the rule, so as to make the authority of God the sole cause and reason both of what they do and of the manner in which they do it. And this is the reason God so often calls on his people to consider diligently and wisely, so that they may do everything as he has commanded.

2nd. Secondly, the affections of the heart and mind in duties belong to the inward manner of performing them. The prescriptions and commands of God to attend to this are countless, and the lack of it makes every duty an abomination to him. A sacrifice without a heart, without salt, without fire, what is it worth? No more are duties worth anything without spiritual affections. Here the mind is to keep the charge of God, to see that the heart he requires is offered to him. And we find also that God requires special affections to go with special duties: “he who gives with cheerfulness.” If these are not attended to, the whole is lost.

[4.] Fourthly, the mind is to attend to the ends of duties, and chiefly among them the glory of God in Christ. Sin and self will impose several other ends on our duties. There are two especially that sin will press hard on us: first, the satisfying of our accusing consciences secondly, the praise of men. For self-righteousness and display are the main ends of men who have fallen away from God, in all moral duties whatever. In their sins they try to satisfy their lusts; in their duties, their accusing consciences and their pride. The mind of a believer is to watch diligently against these, and to keep up in everything a single eye to the glory of God, as what answers to the great and general rule of all our obedience: “whatever you+ do, do it all to the glory of God.” These things and others like them, I say, which are commonly spoken of, the mind of a believer is bound to attend to diligently and constantly with regard to all the particular duties of our walking before God. Here, then, lies no small part of the deceit of sin: to draw the mind away from this watch, and bring an inattention on it, so that in these things it does not keep the watch and charge of the Lord. If sin can do this, and so strip our duties of all their excellence, which lies in these aspects of them that the mind is to attend to, it will not trouble itself or us much about the duties themselves. And it attempts this in several ways.

How Sin Draws the Mind Off from Duties

1. First, by persuading the mind to content itself with generalities, and drawing it away from attending to things in particular instances. For example, it would persuade the soul to rest satisfied in a general aim of doing things to the glory of God, without considering how every particular duty may tend that way. So Saul thought he had fulfilled his whole duty, done the will of God, and sought his glory in his war against Amalek. Yet for lack of attending to every particular duty in that service, he had dishonored God and ruined himself and his descendants. And men may persuade themselves that they have a general design for the glory of God when they have no active principle in particular duties tending that way at all. But if, instead of fixing the mind by faith on particularly advancing the glory of God in a duty, the soul contents itself with a general notion of doing so, the mind is already diverted and drawn away from its charge by the deceitfulness of sin. If a man is traveling on a journey, it is not only required of him that he turn his course that way and keep going, but also that he attend to every turning and the other things he meets on his way, or he may wander and never come to his journey’s end. So suppose that in general we aim at the glory of God, as we all profess to do. Yet if we do not attend to it distinctly in every duty that meets us on our way, we will never attain the end we aim at. And he who satisfies himself with this general purpose, without acting on it in every special duty, will not keep that purpose for long either. Sin does the same work on the mind with regard to the principle of our duties as it does with regard to their end. Their principle is that they are done in faith, in the strength of Christ. But if men content themselves that they are believers, that they have faith, and do not labor in every particular duty to exercise faith, to lead their spiritual lives in all the acts of those duties “by the faith of the Son of God,” then the mind is drawn away from its duty. It is in particular actions that we express and exercise our faith and obedience; and what we are in them, that we are, and no more.

2. Secondly, it draws the mind away from the duties already mentioned by slipping into it a secret contentment with the duty itself as performed, as to its matter. This is a fair discharge for a natural conscience. If the duty is performed, conscience and its accusations will be satisfied, even though in the manner of its performance it falls short of the rule in almost everything. So Saul, after his expedition against Amalek, cries to Samuel: “May the Lord bless you. I have carried out the Lord’s instructions.” He satisfied himself, though he had not attended as he ought to the whole will of God in that matter. So it was with those in Isa 58:3. “Why have we fasted,” they say, “and You have not seen?” They had pleased themselves in performing their duties, and expected that God also would be pleased with them. But he shows them at length where they had failed, and that so far as to make what they had done an abomination. He makes the same charge against them in 48:1, 2. This is what the deceitfulness of sin tries to bring the mind to: to rest in the performance of the duty itself. “You ought to pray, and you have prayed. You ought to give alms, and you have given alms. Then be at ease in what you have done, and go on to do the same.” If it prevails in this, the mind is released from further attending and watching over duty, and this leaves the soul on the borders of many evils. For:

3. Thirdly, from this, routine in all duties will quickly follow, which is the height of sin’s drawing the mind away from duty. For men’s minds may be drawn away from all duties in the midst of performing them most abundantly. In and under them the mind may be subject to a habitual diversion from its charge and its watch over the rule. What is done in such a frame is not done to God (Amos 5:25). None of their sacrifices were to God, though they professed that all of them were. But they did not attend to his worship in faith and to his glory, and he despised all their duties. See also Hos 10:1. This is the great reason why professing Christians thrive so little under the performance of a multitude of duties. They do not attend to them in a proper manner, since their minds have been drawn away from their circumspect watch. So they have little or no communion with God in them, which is the end they are designed for, and the only thing by which they become useful and profitable to them. In this way many duties of worship and obedience are performed by a woeful generation of hypocrites, formalists, and profane persons, without either life or light in themselves or acceptance with God, their minds having been wholly estranged from properly attending to what they do by the power and deceitfulness of sin.

What the Mind Is to Attend To as to Sin

2. Secondly, as it is with regard to duties, so it is also with regard to sins. There are various things in and about every sin that the mind of a believer, by virtue of its office and duty, is bound to attend to diligently, to preserve the soul from it. They are things God has appointed and sanctified to give effectual rebukes and checks to the whole working of the law of sin; and in the law of grace, under which we are, they are exceedingly suited and fitted for that purpose. The deceit of sin tries by all means to draw the mind away from properly considering and attending to these. We will reflect a little on a few of them.

(1.) First, the first and most general is the sovereignty of God, the great lawgiver, by whom sin is forbidden. Joseph fixed on this in his great temptation (Gen 39:9): “How could I do such a great evil and sin against God?” There was a great evil in it, a great ingratitude against man, which he also pleads and insists on (vv. 8, 9). But what fixed his heart and resolution against it was its very essence, that it was sin against God, by whom it was severely forbidden. So the apostle tells us that in our dealing in anything that is against the law, our regard is always to be to the lawgiver and his sovereignty (Jas 4:11, 12): “If you judge the law, you are not a doer of the law, but a judge of it. There is only one Lawgiver, the One who is able to save and destroy.” Consider this always: there is one lawgiver, holy, righteous, armed with sovereign power and authority, and he is able to save and to destroy. That is why sin is called rebellion, a casting off of his yoke, a despising of him, and that in his sovereignty as the great lawgiver. The mind ought always to attend to this in practice, in all the lustings, actings, and suggestions of the law of sin, especially when it is helped by some suitable or vigorous temptation: “It is God who has forbidden this thing, the great lawgiver, under whose absolute sovereignty I am, on whom I depend for my life, and by whom I am to be disposed of in my present and eternal condition.” Eve fixed on this at the beginning of her temptation: “God has said, we must not eat of this tree” (Gen 3:3). But she did not hold her ground. She did not stay with that consideration, but let her mind be diverted from it by the subtlety of Satan, and this was the entrance of her transgression. And so it is with all of us in our straying from obedience.

(2.) Secondly, the deceit of sin, of every sin, the punishment appointed for it in the law, is another thing the mind ought actually to attend to with regard to every particular evil. And the diversions from this that the minds of men have suffered, in doctrine and in practice, have been an inlet to every kind of abomination. Job professes another frame in himself in 31:23: “For destruction from God terrifies me, and because of His majesty I could not endure.” In the preceding verses he had mentioned many evils and pleads his innocence of them, though they were such that, on account of his greatness and power, he could easily have committed them without fear of danger from men. Here he gives the reason that prevailed with him to abstain from them so carefully: “destruction from God terrifies me, and because of His majesty I could not endure.” “I considered,” he says, “that God had appointed death and destruction as the punishment of sin, and that his greatness, majesty, and power were such that he could inflict it to the uttermost, in a way no creature is able to bear or to avoid.” So the apostle directs believers always to consider that “it is a fearful thing to fall into the hands of the living God” (Heb 10:31). And this is because he has said, “Vengeance is Mine; I will repay” (v. 30). He is a sin-avenging God, who will by no means acquit the guilty. So in the declaration of his gracious name, which is infinitely full of encouragements to poor sinners in Christ, he adds this at the close: “He will by no means leave the guilty unpunished” (Exod 34:7). He does this so that he may keep on the minds of those he pardons a proper sense of the punishment due from his avenging justice to every sin. So the apostle would have us bear in mind that even our “God is a consuming fire” (Heb 12:29), that is, that we should consider his holiness and avenging justice, which appoints for sin a fitting recompense of reward. And he counts men’s breaking through this consideration as what makes their sins worst of all (Rom 1:32): “Although they know God’s righteous decree that those who do such things are worthy of death, they … continue to do these things.” What hope is there for such persons? There is indeed relief against this consideration in the blood of Christ for humbled, believing souls. But this relief is not meant to draw the mind away from it, as God has appointed it to be a restraint from sin. And both these considerations, the sovereignty of God and the punishment of sin, are put together by our Savior in Matt 10:28: “Do not be afraid of those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul. Instead, fear the One who can destroy both soul and body in hell.”

(3.) Thirdly, the consideration of all the love and kindness of God, against whom every sin is committed, is another thing the mind ought diligently to attend to. And this is a prevailing consideration, if it is rightly and graciously managed in the soul. Moses presses it on the people in Deut 32:6: “Is this how you+ repay the Lord, O foolish and senseless people? Is He not your Father, who bought you? Has He not made you and established you?” Is this the return for eternal love and all its fruits, for the love and care of a Father, of a Redeemer, that we have been made partakers of? And it is the same consideration the apostle uses to this purpose in 2 Cor 7:1: “Therefore, beloved, since we have these promises, let us cleanse ourselves from everything that defiles body and spirit, perfecting holiness in the fear of God.” The receiving of the promises ought to be effectual both to stir us up to all holiness and to work and bring about an abstaining from all sin. And what promises are these? That God will “be a Father to us, and receive us” (6:17, 18), which takes in the whole of all the love of God toward us, here and to eternity. If there is any spiritual nobleness of heart in the soul, then while the mind is attentive to this consideration, no prevailing attempt can be made on it by the power of sin. Now there are two parts to this consideration.

[1.] First, what is general in it, what is common to all believers. This is used to this purpose in 1 John 3:1, 2, 3: “Behold what manner of love the Father has given to us, that we should be called children of God. The reason the world does not know us is that it did not know Him. Beloved, we are now children of God, and what we will be has not yet been revealed. We know that when He appears, we will be like Him, for we will see Him as He is. And everyone who has this hope in Him purifies himself, just as He is pure.” Consider, he says, the love of God, and the privileges we enjoy by it: “Behold what manner of love the Father has given to us, that we should be called children of God.” Adoption is a special fruit of it, and how great a privilege this is!
