Such is this love, and such are its fruits, that the world knows nothing of the blessed condition we obtain and enjoy by it: “the world does not know us.” Indeed, it is such love, and its effects are so unspeakably blessed and glorious, that we ourselves are not able to comprehend them. What use, then, ought we to make of this contemplation of the excellent, unspeakable love of God? Why, he says, “everyone who has this hope purifies himself.” Every man who has been made partaker of this love, and has from it a hope of fully enjoying its fruits, of being made like God in glory, purifies himself, that is, by abstaining from each and every sin, as is declared at length in the words that follow.

[2.] Secondly, it is to be considered with regard to the particular mercies and fruits of love that each one’s soul has been made partaker of. There is no believer who does not, besides the love and mercy he has in common with all his brethren, also have in the lot of his inheritance some enclosures, some special mercies in which he has a property of his own. He has some joy that no stranger shares in (Prov 14:10), particular applications of covenant love and mercy to his soul. Now these are all provisions God has laid in so that they may be kept in mind against an hour of temptation, and so that the consideration of them may preserve the soul from the attempts of sin. To neglect them greatly aggravates our provocations (1 Kgs 11:9). It is charged as the great evil of Solomon that he had sinned against special mercies, special intimations of love: he sinned after God had appeared to him twice. God required that he should have kept that special favor in mind and made it an argument against sin. But he neglected it, and is burdened with this severe rebuke. And indeed all special mercies, all special tokens and pledges of love, are utterly lost and wasted on us if they are not used to this end. This, then, is another thing that it is the duty of the mind to attend to greatly, and to set effectually against every attempt the law of sin makes on the soul.

(4.) Fourthly, the considerations that arise from the blood and mediation of Christ are of the same importance. So the apostle declares in 2 Cor 5:14, 15: “For Christ’s love constrains us, because we are convinced that One died for all, therefore all died. And He died for all, that those who live should no longer live for themselves, but for Him who died for them and was raised again.” There is a constraining efficacy in this consideration. It is great, forceful, and effectual, if it is properly attended to. But I must not dwell on these things in particular here. Nor,

(5.) Fifthly, I could speak of the indwelling of the Spirit, the greatest privilege we are made partakers of in this world. The proper consideration of how he is grieved by sin, how his dwelling place is defiled by it, and how his comforts are forfeited, lost, and despised by it, might also be dwelt on. But the instances gone through are sufficient for our purpose. Now here lies the duty of the mind with regard to particular sins and temptations. It is to attend diligently and carefully to these things, to dwell constantly on the consideration of them, and to have them always ready to set against all the lustings, actings, warrings, attempts, and rage of sin.

How Sin Draws the Mind Off as to Sin

With regard to this, sin puts forth and acts its deceit in a special way. It labors by all means to draw the mind away from its proper attending to these things, and so to rob the soul of this great preservative and antidote against its poison. It tries to make the soul satisfy itself with general, undigested notions about sin, so that it may have nothing in particular to turn to in its own defense against sin’s attempts and temptations. The ways it does this may also be briefly considered.

1. First, it is from the deceit of sin that the mind is spiritually slothful, and by this it becomes negligent of this duty. The chief way the mind carries out its trust in this matter is expressed by watching, which is the great warning the Lord Jesus gave his disciples with regard to all their dangers from sin and Satan (Mark 13:37): “What I say to you+, I say to everyone: Keep watch!” That is, use your+ utmost diligence and circumspection, so that you+ are not overtaken and entangled by temptations. It is called also consideration: “Consider your+ ways”; “consider your+ latter end.” God complains of the lack of this in his people (Deut 32:29). Now what is contrary to these indispensable conditions of our preservation is spiritual slothfulness, as the apostle declares in Heb 6:11, 12: “We want each of you+ to show this same diligence to the very end, in order to make your+ hope sure. Then you+ will not be slothful.” If we do not show diligence, we are slothful, and in danger of falling short of inheriting the promises. See 2 Pet 1:5, 9: “giving all diligence, add to your+ faith virtue; and to virtue, knowledge,” and so on. “For if you+ possess these qualities and continue to grow in them, they will keep you+ from being ineffective and unproductive in your+ knowledge of our Lord Jesus Christ. But whoever lacks these traits is nearsighted to the point of blindness, having forgotten that he has been cleansed from his past sins. Therefore, brothers, be diligent to make your+ calling and election sure. For if you+ practice these things you+ will never stumble, and you+ will receive a lavish reception into the eternal kingdom of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ” (vv. 9, 10). The mind is turned from all this if once it is made slothful by the deceit of sin. Now this sloth consists in three things.

(1.) First, inattention. The mind does not set itself to consider and attend to what specially concerns it. The apostle, urging the Hebrews with all earnestness to attend diligently and consider carefully, so that they may not be hardened by the deceitfulness of sin, gives this reason for their danger: they had become dull of hearing (Heb 5:11). That is, they were slothful, and did not attend to the things of their duty. A secret carelessness is apt to creep over the soul, and it does not set itself to mark diligently how things go with it and what is continually laid on it.

(2.) Secondly, an unwillingness to be stirred up to its duty (Prov 19:24): “The slothful man buries his hand in his bosom; he will not even bring it back to his mouth.” In sloth there is an unwillingness to take any notice of warnings, calls, rousings, or stirrings up by the word, the Spirit, judgments, or anything God uses to call the mind to a proper consideration of the condition of the soul. And it is a perfect evidence that the mind has been made slothful by the deceit of sin when special calls and warnings, whether in a suitable word or a pressing judgment, cannot prevail with it to pull its hand out of its bosom, that is, to set about the special duties it is called to.

(3.) Thirdly, weak and ineffectual attempts to recover itself to its duty (Prov 26:14): “As a door turns on its hinges, so the slothful man turns on his bed.” In the turning of a door on its hinges there is some motion, but no progress. It moves back and forth, but it is still in the place and position it was in. So it is with the spiritually slothful man on his bed, or in his complacency. He makes some motions or faint efforts toward carrying out his duty, but he does not go on. Where he was one day, there he is the next; indeed, where he was one year, there he is the next. His efforts are faint, cold, and fleeting. He gains no ground by them, but is always beginning his work and never finishing it.

(4.) Fourthly, losing heart at the sight of difficulties and discouragements (Prov 22:13): “The slothful man says, ‘There is a lion outside! I will be slain in the streets!’” Every difficulty deters him from duty. He thinks it impossible for him to attain the accuracy, exactness, and perfection he is to press after in this matter. So he contents himself with his old coldness and negligence rather than run the risk of a universal circumspection. Now if the deceit of sin has once drawn the mind away into this frame, it lays it open to every temptation and invasion of sin. The bride in the Song of Songs seems to have been overtaken by this disorder (5:1, 2, 3). It puts her on various excuses for why she cannot attend to the call of Christ and apply herself to her duty of walking with him.

2. Secondly, it draws the mind away from its watch and duty with regard to sin by surprises. It joins with some pressing temptation and surprises the mind into thoughts of quite another nature than those it ought to dwell on in its own defense. So it seems to have been with Peter. His carnal fear joined with the temptation in which Satan sought to sift him, and filled his mind with so many thoughts about his own imminent danger that he could not take into consideration the love and warning of Christ, or the evil his temptation led him to, or anything he ought to have dwelt on for his preservation. And so, on looking back over his folly in neglecting those thoughts of God and of the love of Christ, which with the help of the Holy Spirit might have kept him from his scandalous fall, he “wept bitterly.” And this indeed is the common way the deceit of sin works with regard to particular evils. It lays hold on the mind suddenly with thoughts about the present sin, possesses it, and takes it up. Either the mind does not recover itself at all to the considerations mentioned, or, if any thoughts of them are suggested, the mind is so taken up and filled beforehand that they make no impression on the soul or do not stay in it. This is doubtless how David was overtaken at the entrance of his great sin. Sin and temptation so possessed and filled his mind with the present object of his lust that he utterly forgot, as it were, the considerations he had made use of before, when he kept himself so diligently from his iniquity. So here lies the great wisdom of the soul: rejecting the very first stirrings of sin. For by negotiating with them the mind may be drawn away from attending to its preservatives, and so the whole soul rushes into evil.

3. Thirdly, it draws the mind away by the frequency and long continuance of its solicitations, at last, as it were, conquering it. And this does not happen without an open neglect on the soul’s part, in failing to stir itself up to give sin an effectual rebuke in the strength and by the grace of Christ, which would have prevented its prevailing power. But more will be said of this later. This is the first way the law of sin acts its deceit against the soul. It draws the mind away from attending to its charge and office, both with regard to duty and with regard to sin. And so far as this is done, the person is said to be drawn away, or drawn off. He is tempted; each one is tempted when he is drawn away in this way by his own lust, or by the deceit of sin dwelling in him. And the whole effect of this working of the deceitfulness of sin may be brought under these three heads.

1. First, the slackening of a universally watchful frame of spirit toward every duty and against all the actings of sin, even the most hidden and secret.

2. Secondly, the omission of special attending to the duties that have a special regard to the weakening and ruin of the whole law of sin, and to heading off its deceitfulness.

3. Thirdly, spiritual sloth with regard to diligently attending to all the special concerns of duties and sins. When these three things, with the branches of them mentioned, are brought about in or on the soul, more or less, then so far as they are, a man is drawn away by his own lust, or by the deceit of sin.

There is no need to add here any directions for preventing this evil. They have been laid down sufficiently as we went through the consideration both of the duty of the mind and of the deceit of sin.

Chapter XI

The working of sin by deceit to entangle the affections. The ways it is done. Means of preventing it.

The second thing the apostle’s words ascribe to the deceitful working of sin is its enticing. A man is drawn away and enticed. This seems to concern the affections in particular, as drawing away concerns the mind. The mind is drawn away from duty, and the affections are enticed to sin. When this prevails, a man is said to be enticed, or entangled as with a bait, as the word means. For there is an allusion in it to the bait by which a fish is caught on the hook that holds it to its destruction. Concerning this effect of the deceit of sin, we will briefly show two things.

1. First, what it is to be enticed, or entangled with the bait of sin, to have the affections tainted with an inclination to it, and when they are so.

2. Secondly, what course sin takes, and what way it goes on in, to entice, ensnare, or entangle the soul in this way.

For the first: first, the affections are certainly entangled when they stir up frequent imaginations about the object set before them, toward which this deceit of sin leads and entices. When sin prevails and the affections have gone fully after it, it fills the imagination with it, continually possessing it with images, likenesses, and appearances of it. Such persons “devise iniquity” and plot evil on their beds, and they carry it out when they are able, when “the power is in their hands” (Mic 2:1). In particular, Peter tells us that they “have eyes full of an adulteress, and they cannot cease from sin” (2 Pet 2:14). That is, their imaginations are possessed with a continual representation of the object of their lusts. And it is so in part where the affections are in part entangled with sin and begin to turn aside to it. John tells us that the things in the world are “the lust of the flesh, the lust of the eyes, and the pride of life” (1 John 2:16). “The lust of the eyes” is what is conveyed to the soul by the eyes. Now what is meant is not the bodily sense of seeing, but the fixing of the imagination on such things through that sense. It is called the eyes because by the imagination things are constantly represented to the mind and soul, as outward objects are represented to the inward sense by the eyes. And often the outward sight of the eyes is the occasion of these imaginations. So Achan declares how sin prevailed with him (Josh 7:21). First he saw the bar of gold and the cloak from Babylon, and then he coveted them. He turned over their pleasures and their profit in his imagination, and then fixed his heart on getting them. Now the heart may have a settled, fixed detestation of sin. Yet if a man finds that the imagination of his mind is frequently solicited by it and occupied with it, he may know that his affections are secretly enticed and entangled.

(2.) Secondly, this entanglement is heightened when the imagination can prevail with the mind to lodge vain thoughts in it with secret delight and pleasure. Casuists call this cogitatio morosa cum delectation, a lingering thought with delight, which toward forbidden objects is in all cases actually sinful. And yet this may happen when the consent of the will to sin has not been obtained, when the soul would not for the world do the thing about which thoughts nevertheless begin to lodge in the mind. The prophet complains of this lodging of vain thoughts in the heart as something greatly sinful and to be abhorred (Jer 4:14). All these thoughts are messengers that carry sin back and forth between the imagination and the affections, and they keep increasing it, inflaming the imagination and entangling the affections more and more. Achan thinks about the bar of gold; this makes him like it and love it. By loving it his thoughts are infected, and they return to imagining its worth and fine appearance; and so little by little the soul is inflamed to sin. And here, if the will gives up its sovereignty, sin is actually conceived.

(3.) Thirdly, inclinations or readiness to listen to excuses that lessen sin, or to the reliefs offered against sin once it is committed, show that the affections are entangled with it. We have shown, and will show further still, that it is a great part of the deceit of sin to offer the mind thoughts that lessen and extenuate sin. “Is it not a little one?” or, there is mercy provided; or, it will be left off and given up in due time: this is its language in a deceived heart. Now when there is a readiness in the soul to listen to and welcome such secret suggestions arising from this deceit, with regard to any sin or any course that cannot be approved, it is evidence that the affections are enticed. When the soul is willing, as it were, to be tempted, to be courted by sin, to listen to its flirtations and solicitations, it has lost some of its marriage affection for Christ and is entangled. This is “gazing at wine while it is red, when it sparkles in the cup and goes down smoothly” (Prov 23:31): a pleasing contemplation of the invitations of sin, whose end the wise man gives us in verse 32. When the deceit of sin has prevailed this far on any person, then he is enticed or entangled. The will has not yet come to the actual conception of this or that sin by its consent, but the whole soul is close to inclining to it. I could give many other instances as tokens and evidences of this entanglement. These may be enough to show what we mean by it.

Our next inquiry is how, or by what means, the deceit of sin goes about enticing and entangling the affections in this way. Two or three of its baits are plain here.

(1.) First, it makes use of the way it has already prevailed on the mind, in drawing it away from its watch and circumspection. The wise man says in Prov 1:17: “It is in vain to spread the net where any bird can see it!” or, as in the original, before the eyes of everything that has a wing. If the bird has eyes open to see the snare, and a wing to carry it away, it will not be caught. So the deceit of sin would spread its snares and nets in vain to entangle the soul, as long as the eyes of the mind are intent on what it is doing, and so stir up the wings of the soul’s will and affections to carry it away and escape. But if the eyes are put out or diverted, the wings are of very little use for escape. That is why this is one of the ways used by those who catch birds or fowl in their nets: they have false lights, or appearances of things, to divert the sight of their prey, and when that is done they seize the moment to cast their nets over them. So the deceit of sin does. As has been shown, it first draws away and diverts the mind by false reasonings and pretenses, and then it casts its net over the affections to entangle them.

(2.) Secondly, taking advantage of such seasons, it sets sin before the soul as desirable, as exceedingly satisfying to the corrupt part of our affections. It gilds over the object with a thousand pretenses, which it presents to corrupt lustings. This is the laying of a bait, to which the apostle plainly alludes in this verse. A bait is something desirable and suitable set before the hungry creature to satisfy it, and it is made desirable and suitable by every kind of trick. This is how sin is presented to the soul with the help of the imagination; that is, sinful and inordinate objects, which the affections cling to, are presented this way. The apostle tells us that there are “pleasures of sin” (Heb 11:25), and unless they are despised, as they were by Moses, there is no escaping sin itself. So those who live in sin are said to “live in pleasure” (Jas 5:5). Now this pleasure of sin consists in its suitableness to give satisfaction to the flesh, to lust, to corrupt affections. That is the reason for the warning in Rom 13:14: “make no provision for the flesh to fulfill its lusts.” That is, do not let your+ minds, thoughts, or affections fix on sinful objects suited to satisfy the lusts of the flesh, so as to nourish and cherish those lusts by them. He speaks to the same purpose again in Gal 5:16: “do not gratify the lusts of the flesh.” Do not bring in the pleasures of sin to satisfy them. When men are under the power of sin, they are said to be “fulfilling the cravings of the flesh and indulging its desires and thoughts” (Eph 2:3). So the deceit of sin tries to entangle the affections by setting before them, with the help of the imagination, the suitableness that is in sin to satisfy their corrupt lusts, which are now set at some liberty by the inattention of the mind. It presents its wine as sparkling in the cup, the beauty of the adulteress, the riches of the world to sensual and covetous persons, and something of the same kind, in some degree, to believers themselves. So when sin would entangle the soul, I say, it prevails with the imagination to solicit the heart by representing this false, painted beauty, or the pretended satisfaction of sin. And then, if Satan comes in to its help with some special temptation, it often inflames all the affections and throws the whole soul into disorder.

(3.) Thirdly, it hides the danger that goes with sin. It covers it as the hook is covered with the bait, or as the net is spread over with food for the bird to be caught. It is indeed not possible for sin to deprive the soul utterly of the knowledge of its danger. It cannot rid the soul of its notion or persuasion that “the wages of sin is death,” and that it is “God’s righteous decree that those who do such things are worthy of death.” But this it will do: it will so take up and possess the mind and affections with the baits and desirableness of sin that it diverts them from an actual and practical contemplation of its danger. What Satan did in and by his first temptation, sin has done ever since. At first Eve guards herself by calling to mind the danger of sin: if we eat it or touch it, we will die (Gen 3:3). But as soon as Satan had filled her mind with the beauty of the fruit and its usefulness to make one wise, how quickly she laid aside her practical, prevailing consideration of the danger of eating it and of the curse due to it! Or else she relieves herself with a vain hope and pretense that it would not come about, because the serpent told her so. So David was beguiled by the deceit of sin in his great transgression. Since his lust was pleased and satisfied, the consideration of the guilt and danger of his transgression was taken away. That is why he is said to have “despised the Lord” (2 Sam 12:9): he did not consider the evil that was in his heart, and the danger that went with it in the threatening, or denunciation, of the law. Now when sin presses on the soul to this purpose, it will use a thousand wiles to hide from it the terror of the Lord, the end of transgressions, and especially of the particular folly it is soliciting the mind toward. Hopes of pardon will be used to hide it; future repentance will hide it; the present urgency of lust will hide it; occasions and opportunities will hide it; surprises will hide it; the lessening of sin will hide it; weighing duties against it will hide it; fixing the imagination on present objects will hide it; desperate resolutions to risk the uttermost for the enjoyment of lust in its pleasures and profits will hide it. It has a thousand wiles that cannot be counted.

(4.) Fourthly, having prevailed this far, gilding over the pleasures of sin and hiding its end and what it deserves, it goes on to raise perverse reasonings in the mind, to fix it on the sin set before it, so that the sin may be conceived and brought forth, since the affections have already been prevailed on. We will speak of this under the next head of its progress.

Here we may stop a little, as before, to give a few directions for heading off this woeful work of the deceitfulness of sin. If we would not be enticed or entangled, if we would not be disposed to the conception of sin, if we would be turned out of the road and way that goes down to death, let us take heed to our affections. They are of such great concern in the whole course of our obedience that in the Scripture they are commonly called by the name of the heart, as the chief thing God requires in our walking before him. And this is not to be attended to lightly. The wise man says in Prov 4:23: “Keep your heart with all diligence,” or, as in the original, above or before all keepings: “Before every watch, keep your heart.” You+ have many keepings that you+ watch over. You+ watch to keep your+ lives, to keep your+ estates, to keep your+ reputations, to keep up your+ families. But, he says, above all these keepings prefer this one; attend to the keeping of the heart, of your+ affections, so that they are not entangled with sin. There is no safety without it. Save everything else and lose the heart, and all is lost, lost for all eternity. You+ will say, then, “What shall we do? How shall we observe this duty?”

1. First, keep your+ affections with regard to their object in general. The apostle gives this advice in this very case in Col 3. His advice at the beginning of that chapter is meant to direct us to the mortification of sin, which he expressly takes up in verse 5: “Put to death, therefore, your+ members that are on the earth.” Head off the working and deceit of sin that wars in your+ members. To prepare us and enable us for this, he gives us that great direction in verse 2: “Set your+ affections on things above, not on earthly things.” Fix your+ affections on heavenly things; this will enable you+ to mortify sin. Fill them with the things that are above; let them be occupied with them, and so let those things have the chief place in them. Things above are blessed and suitable objects, fit for our affections and answering to them: God himself in his beauty and glory; the Lord Jesus Christ, who is “altogether lovely, outstanding among ten thousand”; grace and glory; the mysteries revealed in the gospel; the blessedness promised by it. It is our duty that our affections should be filled, taken up, and possessed with these things, and it is our happiness when they are. If they were, what access could sin have to our souls with its painted pleasures, its sugared poisons, its venomous baits? How we would loathe all its proposals, and say to them, “Be gone!” as to an abominable thing! For what are the vain, passing pleasures of sin compared with the exceeding recompense of reward that is set before us? The apostle presses this argument in 2 Cor 4:18.

(2.) Secondly, as to the object of your+ affections in a special way: let it be the cross of Christ, which has an exceeding efficacy for defeating the whole work of indwelling sin. Gal 6:14: “But as for me, may I never boast, except in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, through which the world has been crucified to me, and I to the world.” The cross of Christ was what he boasted and rejoiced in. His heart was set on it, and these were its effects: it crucified the world to him; it made the world a dead and undesirable thing. The baits and pleasures of sin are all taken out of the world and the things in the world, that is, “the lust of the flesh, the lust of the eyes, and the pride of life.” These are the things in the world, and from them sin takes all its baits, by which it entices and entangles our souls. If the heart is filled with the cross of Christ, it casts death and undesirableness on them all. It leaves no seeming beauty, no apparent pleasure or attractiveness in them. Again, he says, “it crucifies me to the world.” It makes my heart, my affections, my desires dead to all these things. It roots up corrupt lusts and affections, and leaves no principle to go out and make provision for the flesh, to gratify its lusts. So labor to fill your+ hearts with the cross of Christ. Consider the sorrows he underwent, the curse he bore, the blood he shed, the cries he uttered, the love that was in all this toward your+ souls, and the mystery of the grace of God in it. Meditate on the vileness, the ill desert, and the punishment of sin as represented in the cross, the blood, and the death of Christ. Is Christ crucified for sin, and will our hearts not be crucified with him to sin? Shall we welcome that thing, or listen to its flirtations, which wounded, pierced, and slew our dear Lord Jesus? God forbid! Fill your+ affections with the cross of Christ, so that there may be no room for sin. The world once put him out of the house into a stable when he came to save us; let him now turn the world out of doors, now that he has come to sanctify us.

2. Secondly, look to the vigor of the affections toward heavenly things. If they are not constantly attended to, roused, directed, and warned, they are apt to decay, and sin lies in wait to take every advantage against them. We have many complaints in the Scripture of those who lost their “first love” by letting their affections decay. This should make us watchful over our own hearts, so that we too are not overtaken by the same backsliding frame. So be watchful over them; examine them often and strictly and call them to account; supply them with proper considerations to rouse and stir them up to duty.

Chapter XII

The conception of sin through its deceit. What it consists in. The consent of the will to sin. Its nature. The ways and means by which it is obtained. Other advantages used by the deceit of sin. Ignorance, errors.

The third result of the deceit of sin in its advancing work is the conception of actual sin. When it has drawn the mind away from its duty and entangled the affections, it goes on to conceive sin, with a view to bringing it forth. “Then, when lust has conceived, it brings forth sin.” Now the conception of sin, with a view to committing it, can be nothing but the consent of the will. For as sin cannot be committed without the consent of the will, so where the will has consented to it, there is nothing in the soul to hinder its actual accomplishment. God does indeed by various ways and means frustrate the bringing forth of these adulterous conceptions. He makes them melt away in the womb, or in one way or another miscarry, so that not the least part of the sin that is willed or conceived is committed. Yet once the will has given its consent, there is nothing left in the soul itself to check it. Often, when a cloud is full of rain and ready to fall, a wind comes and drives it away. And when the will is ready to bring forth its sin, God turns it aside by one wind or another. But the cloud was as full of rain as if it had fallen, and the soul was as full of sin as if the sin had been committed.

This conceiving of lust, or of sin, then, is its prevailing in obtaining the consent of the will to its solicitations. By this the soul is deflowered of its chastity toward God in Christ, as the apostle suggests in 2 Cor 11:2, 3. To clear up this matter we must observe:

1. First, that the will is the principle, the nearest seat and cause, of obedience and disobedience. Moral actions are good or evil to us, or in us, so far as they share in the consent of the will. He spoke the truth long ago who said, “Omne peccatum est adeoò voluntarium, ut non sit peccatum nisi sit voluntarium.” “Every sin is so voluntary that if it is not voluntary, it is not sin.” This is most true of actual sins. The very essence of their iniquity arises from the acts of the will in them and concerning them; I mean with regard to the persons who commit them. Otherwise, in itself, the essence of sin is its departure from the law of God.

2. Secondly, there is a twofold consent of the will to sin.

(1.) First, one that is full, absolute, complete, and given on deliberation. It is a prevailing consent: the mind’s conviction of sin has been conquered, and there is no principle of grace in the will to weaken it. With this consent the soul goes into sin like a ship before the wind with all its sails spread, without any check or stop. It rushes into sin like the horse into battle. By it men, as the apostle says, “have given themselves over” to sin “with a craving for more” (Eph 4:19). This is how Ahab’s will was in the murder of Naboth. He did it on deliberation, by plotting, with full consent. Doing it gave him such satisfaction that it cured his sickness, the disorder of his mind. This is the consent of the will that is acted in the finishing and completing of sin in unregenerate persons. It is not required for the simple bringing forth of sin, which is what we are speaking of.

(2.) Secondly, there is a consent of the will that is accompanied by a secret resistance and a willing of the contrary. This is how Peter’s will was in denying his Master. His will was in it, or he would not have done it. It was a voluntary action, something he chose to do at that time. The sin would not have been brought forth if it had not been conceived in this way. Yet at that very time there was present in his will a contrary principle of love to Christ, indeed of faith in him, which did not fail utterly. Its efficacy was intercepted, and its workings actually suspended, through the violent pressure of the temptation he was under. But it was still in his will, and it weakened his consent to sin even though he consented. It was not done with the self-pleasing that such full acts of the will produce.

3. Thirdly, though there may be a predominant consent in the will, which may be enough for the conception of particular sins, there cannot be an absolute, total, full consent of the will of a believer to any sin. For:

(1.) First, there is in his will a principle fixed on good, on all good (Rom 7:21): “he wants to do good.” The principle of grace in the will inclines him to all good. And in general this prevails against the principle of sin, so that the will takes its name from it. In the will of every believer grace has the rule and dominion, not sin. Now a consent to sin in the will that is contrary to the inclination and general prevailing principle in that same will is not, and cannot be, total, absolute, and complete.

(2.) Secondly, there is not only a general, ruling, prevailing principle in the will against sin, but there is also a secret resistance in the will against its own act in consenting to sin. It is true that at times the soul does not feel this resistance, because the present consent carries away the prevailing act of the will and takes away the sense of the lusting of the spirit, the resistance of the principle of grace in the will. But the general rule holds in all things at all times (Gal 5:17): “the spirit lusts against the flesh.” It does so actually, though not always to the same degree or with the same success. And the prevailing of the contrary principle in this or that particular act does not disprove it. It is the same on the other side. There is no acting of grace in the will that sin does not lust against. Though that lusting is not felt in the soul, because the contrary acting of grace prevails, it is still enough to keep those actings from being perfect in their kind. So it is with this resistance of grace against the acting of sin in the soul. Though it is not felt in its workings, it is enough to keep that act from being full and complete. And much spiritual wisdom lies in discerning rightly between the spiritual resistance of the principle of grace in the will against sin, and the rebukes that conscience gives the soul for sin upon conviction.

4. Fourthly, observe that repeated acts of the consent of the will to sin may produce a disposition and inclination in it to similar acts, which may bring the will to a proneness and readiness to consent to sin at easy solicitations. This is a dangerous condition of soul, and one to be greatly watched against.

5. Fifthly, this consent of the will, which we have now described, may be considered in two ways.

(1.) First, as it is occupied with the circumstances, causes, means, and inducements to sin. Secondly, as it concerns this or that actual sin. In the first sense there is a virtual consent of the will to sin in every failure to watch against sin, in every neglect of duty that makes way for it, in every listening to any temptation that leads toward it. In a word, it is in all the diversions of the mind from its duty and all the entanglements of the affections by sin mentioned above. For where there is no act of the will, either formally or virtually, there is no sin. But this is not what we are now speaking of. We are speaking in particular of the consent of the will to this or that actual sin, so far as either the sin is committed, or it is prevented by other ways and means that are not now under consideration. And in this consists the conceiving of sin.

With these things supposed, what we are to consider next is the way the deceit of sin goes about obtaining the consent of the will, and so conceiving actual sin in the soul. To this purpose, observe:

1. First, that the will is a rational appetite: rational as it is guided by the mind, and an appetite as it is stirred by the affections. So in its working or actings it has regard to both and is influenced by both.

2. Secondly, it chooses nothing and consents to nothing except sub ration boni, as it has an appearance of good, some present good. It cannot consent to anything under the notion or perception of its being evil in any way. Good is its natural and necessary object. So whatever is put before it for its consent must be put before it with an appearance of being good, either in itself, or good to the soul at present, or good in the circumstances it is in. So:

3. Thirdly, we may see from this the reason why the conception of sin is placed here as a consequence of the mind’s being drawn away and the affections’ being entangled. Both of these have an influence on the consent of the will, and so on the conception of this or that actual sin. So our way here is made somewhat plain. We have seen at length how the mind is drawn away by the deceit of sin and how the affections are entangled; what remains is only the proper effect of these things. To uncover it, we must give some instances of the special deceits, the corrupt and false reasonings mentioned earlier, and then show how they prevail on the will to consent to sin.

(1.) First, the will is deceived by the corrupt reasoning that grace is exalted in pardon, and that mercy is provided for sinners. As has been shown, this first deceives the mind, and that opens the way to the will’s consent by removing the sight of the evil the will has an aversion to. In carnal hearts this prevails so far as to make them think that their freedom consists in being slaves of corruption (2 Pet 2:19). And its poison often taints and corrupts the minds of believers themselves; that is why we are so warned against it in the Scripture. So to what has been said before about the use and abuse of the doctrine of the grace of the gospel, we will add a few more considerations, and fix on one place of Scripture that will give light on it. There is a twofold mystery of grace: of walking with God, and of coming to God. The great design of sin is to alter the doctrine and mystery of grace with regard to these things by applying to the one the considerations proper to the other. By this each part is hindered, and the influence of the doctrine of grace on them, which would further them, is defeated. See 1 John 2:1, 2: “I am writing these things to you+ so that you+ will not sin. But if anyone does sin, we have an advocate before the Father, Jesus Christ, the Righteous One. He Himself is the propitiation for our sins.” Here the whole design and use of the gospel is briefly expressed. “These things,” he says, “I am writing to you+.” What were these things? Those mentioned in 1:2: “this is the life that was revealed; we have seen it and testified to it, and we proclaim to you+ the eternal life that was with the Father and was revealed to us,” that is, the things concerning the person and mediation of Christ; and in 1:7, that pardon, forgiveness, and expiation for sin are to be obtained by the blood of Christ. But to what end and purpose does he write these things to them? What do they teach? What do they tend to? A universal abstaining from sin. “I am writing to you+,” he says, “so that you+ will not sin.” This is the proper, only, genuine end of the doctrine of the gospel. But to abstain from all sin is not our condition in this world (1 John 1:8): “If we say we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us.” What, then, is to be done in this case? Supposing sin, supposing that we have sinned, is there no relief provided for our souls and consciences in the gospel? Yes, he says: “if anyone does sin, we have an advocate before the Father, Jesus Christ, the Righteous One. He Himself is the propitiation for our sins.” There is full relief in the propitiation and intercession of Christ for us. This is the order and method of the doctrine of the gospel and of applying it to our own souls: first, to keep us from sin; and then, to help us against sin. But here the deceit of sin comes in and puts this “new wine into old wineskins,” so that the wineskins burst and the wine is lost as far as our benefit from it goes. It changes this method and order of applying gospel truths. It takes up the last first, and that shuts out the use of the first entirely. “If anyone sins, pardon is provided” is all the gospel sin would willingly let stay on the minds of men. When we would come to God by believing, sin would be pressing the first part, being free from sin, when the gospel chiefly sets forth the second, the pardon of sin, for our encouragement. When we have come to God and should walk with him, sin will have only the second part set forth, that there is pardon of sin, when the gospel chiefly sets forth the first, keeping ourselves from sin, since the grace of God has appeared to us, bringing salvation, to that end and purpose. Now the mind, entangled with this deceit, drawn away from its watch by it, and diverted from the true ends of the gospel, deceives the will in several ways to obtain its consent.

[1.] First, by a sudden surprise in a case of temptation. Temptation is the representation of something as a present good, a particular good, when it is a real evil, a general evil. Now when a temptation armed with opportunity and provocation befalls the soul, the principle of grace in the will rises up to reject and detest it. But suddenly the mind, deceived by sin, breaks in on the will with a corrupt and false reasoning from gospel grace and mercy. This first staggers the will’s opposition, then weakens it, and then makes the will tip the scale by its consent on the side of the temptation, which presents evil as a present good. And so sin is conceived in the sight of God, though it is never committed. In this way the seed of God is sacrificed to Molech, and the weapons of Christ are abused in the service of the devil.

[2.] Secondly, it does it imperceptibly. It slips in the poison of this corrupt reasoning little by little, until it has greatly prevailed. The whole effect of the doctrine of the gospel in holiness and obedience consists in the soul’s being cast into its frame and mold (Rom 6:17). In the same way the whole of apostasy from the gospel is chiefly the casting of the soul into the mold of this false reasoning: that sin may be indulged on account of grace and pardon. By this the soul is gratified in sloth and negligence, and drawn away from its care about particular duties and avoiding particular sins. The mystery of the law of grace is to look for salvation as if we had never performed any duty, since after we have done everything we are unprofitable servants, resting on sovereign mercy through the blood of Christ; and to attend to duties with all diligence as if we looked for no mercy, that is, with no less care, though with more liberty and freedom. This reasoning imperceptibly works the soul away from that mystery. The deceitfulness of sin tries by every means to work the soul away from it, and by this it corrupts the will when its consent is required to particular sins.

(2.) Secondly, the deceived mind deceives the will to obtain its consent to sin by setting before it the advantages that may come and arise from it; this is one means by which the mind itself is also drawn away. It makes what is absolutely evil a present seeming good. So it was with Eve (Gen 3). Laying aside all consideration of the law, covenant, and threatenings of God, she at once reflects on the advantages, pleasures, and benefits she would obtain by her sin, and counts them up to solicit the consent of her will. The tree “is,” she says, “good for food and pleasing to the eyes, and … desirable for obtaining wisdom.” What should she do, then, but eat it? Her will consented, and she did so. The pleas for obedience are laid out of the way, and only the pleasures of sin are taken into consideration. So Ahab says (1 Kgs 21): “Naboth’s vineyard is next to my palace, and I may use it as a vegetable garden”; therefore I must have it. A deceived mind imposed these considerations on his will until it made him obstinate in pursuing his covetousness through perjury and murder, to the utter ruin of himself and his family. In this way the guilt and tendency of sin are hidden under the cover of advantages and pleasures, and so sin is conceived, or resolved on, in the soul.

2. Secondly, as the mind, when it is drawn away, so also the affections, when they are enticed and entangled, greatly further the conception of sin in the soul by the consent of the will. They do it in two ways.

(1.) First, by some hasty impulse and surprise. When the affections themselves are stirred up, provoked, and drawn out by some violent provocation or suitable temptation, they throw the whole soul, as it were, into an uproar, and draw the will into consenting to what they are provoked to and entangled with. Such was the case of David in the matter of Nabal. A violent provocation, from the extremely unworthy behavior of that foolish churl, stirs him up to wrath and revenge (1 Sam 25:13). He resolves on it, to destroy a whole family, the innocent with the guilty (vv. 33, 34). Self-revenge and murder were for that time conceived, resolved on, and consented to, until God graciously drew him back. His entangled, provoked affections surprised his will into consenting to the conception of many bloody sins. The case was the same with Asa in his anger, when he struck the prophet, and with Peter in his fear, when he denied his Master. Let the soul that would beware of conceiving sin beware of entangled affections. For sin may be suddenly conceived; the prevailing consent of the will may be suddenly obtained, and this gives the soul a fixed guilt, though the sin itself is never actually brought forth.

(2.) Secondly, enticed affections obtain the consent of the will by frequent solicitations, by which they gain ground on it imperceptibly and enthrone themselves. Take as an instance the sons of Jacob (Gen 37:4). They hate their brother because their father loved him. Their affections being enticed, many new occasions arise to entangle them further, such as his dreams and the like. This lay rankling in their hearts and never stopped soliciting their wills until they resolved on his death. The unlawfulness and unnaturalness of the act, the grief of their aged father, the guilt of their own souls, are all laid aside. The hatred and envy they had conceived against him did not stop until they had got the consent of their wills to his ruin. The wise man describes excellently this gradual progress of the prevailing of corrupt affections in soliciting the soul to sin (Prov 23:31, 32, 33, 34, 35). And this is the common way sin proceeds in the destruction of souls who seem to have made some good commitments in the ways of God. When it has entangled them with one temptation and brought the will to some liking of it, that at once becomes another temptation, either to neglect some duty or to refuse more light. And commonly the thing by which men fall away from God entirely is not the thing they were first entangled with. This may be enough, briefly, for the third advancing act of the deceit of sin. It obtains the will’s consent to its conception, and by this means multitudes of sins are conceived in the heart that defile the soul very little less, and make it contract very little less guilt, than if they were actually committed.

To what has been said about the deceitfulness of indwelling sin in general, which greatly shows its power and efficacy, I will add, to close this discourse, one or two particular ways in which it acts deceitfully. These consist in advantages it makes use of, and in means of defending itself against the search made after it by the word and Spirit for its ruin. We will name here only one head of each sort.

1. First, it takes great advantage of the darkness of the mind to work out its designs and intentions. The shadows of a mind that is totally dark, that is, utterly without saving grace, are the proper workshop of sin. That is why its effects are called the “works of darkness” (Eph 5:11; Rom 13:12), as springing from there. Sin works and brings forth with the help of darkness. The working of lust under the cover of a dark mind is, as it were, the upper region of hell, for it lies next door to hell in filth, horror, and disorder. Now there is a partial darkness still remaining in believers; they “know in part” (1 Cor 13:12). Though there is a principle of saving light in all of them, and the morning star has risen in their hearts, yet not all the shadows of darkness are utterly driven out of them in this life. And there are two parts, as it were, or two chief effects, of the remaining darkness in believers. First, ignorance, or not knowing the will of God, either juris or facti: of the rule and law in general, or of how the particular act before the mind relates to the law. Secondly, error and mistakes, positively taking for truth what is falsehood, and for light what is darkness. Now the law of sin takes great advantage of both of these to exert its power in the soul.

(1.) First, if there is any remaining ignorance of anything of the will of God, sin will be sure to make use of it and exploit it to the uttermost. Though Abimelech was not a believer, he was a person who had a moral integrity in his ways and actions. He declares that he had it, in a solemn appeal to God, the searcher of all hearts, even in the matter in which he went wrong (Gen 20:5). But he did not know that fornication was a sin, or so great a sin that it was unfitting for a morally upright man to defile himself with it. So lust hurries him into that intention of evil with regard to Sarah, as we have it related there. God complains that his people “are destroyed for lack of knowledge” (Hos 4:6). Being ignorant of the mind and will of God, they rushed into evil at every command of the law of sin. Whether it is a duty to be performed or a sin to be committed, if there is darkness or ignorance of mind about it, sin will not lose its advantage. Many a man who does not know the duty that lies on him for the instruction of his family, and throws the whole weight of it on the public teaching, is brought by the deceitfulness of sin into a habitual sloth and negligence of duty. As much ignorance of the will of God and of duty as there is, so much advantage is given to the law of sin. And from this we may see what the true knowledge is that is acceptable to God. How exactly many a poor soul walks with God, though he is low in notional knowledge! It seems such souls know so much that sin has little advantage against them on that account. Others, meanwhile, high in their notions, give advantage to their lusts even by their ignorance, though they do not know it.

(2.) Secondly, error is a worse part or effect of the mind’s darkness, and it gives great advantage to the law of sin. There is indeed ignorance in every error, but there is not error in all ignorance, and so they may be distinguished. I need to illustrate this with only one consideration: that of men who, being zealous for some error, seek to suppress and persecute the truth. Indwelling sin desires no greater advantage. How it will pour out wrath, revilings, and harsh speeches every day, every hour, and breathe revenge, murder, and desolation, perhaps under the name of zeal! On this account we may see poor creatures pleasing themselves every day, as if they were boasting of their excellence, when they are foaming out their own shame. Under their real darkness and pretended zeal, sin sits securely, and fills pulpits, houses, prayers, and streets as full as they can hold with the bitter fruits of envy, malice, wrath, hatred, evil suspicions, and false speaking. The common outcome with such poor creatures is that the holy, blessed, meek Spirit of God withdraws from them, and leaves them visibly and openly to the evil, perverse, wrathful, worldly spirit that the law of sin has cherished and heightened in them. Sin dwells nowhere more securely than in such a frame. So, I say, it lays hold of particular advantages to practice on with its deceitfulness, and in them also to exert its power in the soul. This single instance, its exploiting the darkness of the mind to its own ends, is sufficient evidence of it.

2. Secondly, it uses means of defending itself against the pursuit made after it in the heart by the word and Spirit of grace. I will name one of its wiles of this kind also, by way of instance, and that is the lightening of its own guilt. It pleads for itself that it is not so bad, so filthy, so fatal as is claimed. It goes on in this course of extenuation in two ways. First, absolutely. It will have many secret pleas that the evil it tends toward is not so destructive as conscience is persuaded it is; it may be ventured on without ruin. It will urge these considerations strongly when it is at work by way of surprise, when the soul has no leisure or freedom to weigh its suggestions in the balance of the sanctuary. And not seldom the will is deceived by this, and sin gains the advantage of slipping out from under the sword of the Spirit. “It is not such a sin that it cannot be let alone, or allowed to die of itself, which it will probably do before long. There is no need for the violence that is to be used in mortification. It is time enough to deal later with a matter of no greater importance.” And there are other pleas like those mentioned before. Secondly, comparatively, and this is a large field for its deceit and subtlety to lurk in. “Though it is indeed an evil to be given up, and the soul is to be made watchful against it, yet it is not of that size and degree we may see in the lives of others, even saints of God, much less of the kind some saints of old have fallen into.” By these and similar pretenses, I say, it seeks to escape and keep its home in the soul when it is pursued to destruction. And how small a portion of its deceitfulness is it that we have declared!

Chapter XIII

Several ways in which the bringing forth of conceived sin is obstructed.

Before we go on to the remaining evidences of the power and efficacy of the law of sin, we will take occasion from what has been said to turn aside to one consideration. It arises from the Scripture that was made the basis and foundation of our discourse on the general deceitfulness of sin, namely Jas 1:14, 15. The apostle tells us that “lust, when it has conceived, brings forth sin.” He seems to imply that whatever sin is conceived is also brought forth. Now we have placed the conception of sin in the consent of the will to it, and we rightly reckon that the bringing forth of sin consists in its actual commission. On that account we know that the one does not necessarily follow the other. There is a world of sin conceived in the womb of men’s wills and hearts that is never brought forth. Our present business, then, will be to inquire how that comes about. I answer, then:

1. First, that conceived sin is not always brought forth is no thanks to sin, nor to its law. What it conceives, it would bring forth. That it does not is, for the most part, only a small lessening of its guilt. A settled will to sin actually is actual sin. Nothing is lacking on sin’s part to make every conceived sin actually accomplished. The obstacle and hindrance lie elsewhere.

2. Secondly, two things are necessary in the person who has conceived sin if it is to bring it forth. First, power. Secondly, continuance in the will to sin until the sin is carried out and committed. Where these two are present, actual sin will unavoidably follow. It is evident, therefore, that whatever hinders conceived sin from being brought forth must affect either the power or the will of the sinner. This must be from God. And he has two ways of doing it. First, by his providence, by which he obstructs the power to sin. Secondly, by his grace, by which he turns aside or changes the will to sin. I do not name these ways of God’s dealings so separately as though the one were always without the other. There is much grace in his providential dealings, and much of the wisdom of providence is seen in the dispensations of grace. But I place them in this distinction because each appears most eminent in its own field: providence in outward acts that concern the power of the person; grace, common or special, in an inward efficacy that concerns his will. We will begin with the first.

God’s Providence Opposed to the Sinner’s Power

1. First, when sin is conceived, the Lord obstructs its production by his providence. He does it by taking away, or cutting short, the power that is absolutely necessary for bringing it forth or accomplishing it. For example:

(1.) First, life is the foundation of all power, the principle of all action. When life ceases, all power ceases with it. Even God himself, to show the everlasting stability of his own power, takes the title of the living God. Now he often forestalls the power of carrying out sin actually by cutting short and taking away the lives of those who have conceived it. So he dealt with the army of Sennacherib. Sennacherib had purposed, and accordingly threatened, that the Lord would not deliver Jerusalem from his hand (2 Kgs 18:35). God threatens to cut short his power, so that he should not carry out his intention (ch. 19:28). He does this accordingly by taking away the lives of his soldiers (v. 35), without whom it was impossible for Sennacherib’s conceived sin to be brought forth. Moses sets out this providential dealing in obstructing conceived sin excellently in the case of Pharaoh (Exod 15:9, 10): “The enemy declared, ‘I will pursue, I will overtake. I will divide the spoils; my lust will be satisfied on them. I will draw my sword; my hand will destroy them.’ But You blew with Your breath, and the sea covered them. They sank like lead in the mighty waters.” Sin’s conception is fully expressed, and as full a prevention is joined to it. In the same way he dealt with the companies of fifty and their captains who came to seize Elijah (2 Kgs 1:10, 11). Fire came down from heaven and consumed them when they were ready to take him. Many other instances of the same kind could be recorded. We have one that concerns all mankind in the great providential change that set a limit to the lives of men. Men who lived hundreds of years had a long time to bring forth the sins they had conceived. As a result the earth was filled with violence, injustice, and plunder, and all “flesh had corrupted their ways” (Gen 6:12, 13). To prevent a similar flood of sin, God shortens the course of men’s pilgrimage on the earth and reduces their lives to a much shorter measure. Besides this general law, God daily cuts off in this way persons who had conceived much mischief and violence in their hearts, and prevents them from carrying it out. “Men of bloodshed and deceit will not live out half their days.” They still have much work to do, if only they were given time to carry out the bloody and sinful purposes of their minds. The psalmist tells us (Ps 146:4), “When his spirit departs, … on that very day his plans perish.” The man had many schemes about sin, but now they are all cut off. So also Eccl 8:12, 13: “Although a sinner does evil a hundred times and still lives long, yet I also know that it will go well with those who fear God, who are reverent in His presence. Yet because the wicked do not fear God, it will not go well with them, and their days will not lengthen like a shadow.” However long a wicked man lives, he dies by God’s judgment, and will not remain to do the evil he had conceived.

But since we have granted that even believers themselves may conceive sin through its power and deceitfulness, it may be asked whether God ever forestalls its production and accomplishment in them in this way. Does he cut off and take away their lives, so that they will not be able to carry it out? I answer:

[1.] First, God does not in judgment cut off and take away the life of any of his own for this end and purpose: to prevent the carrying out, or bringing forth, of some particular sin that the person had conceived, and that he would have committed if his life had not been taken away. For:

1st. First, this is directly contrary to the declared end of the patience of God toward them (2 Pet 3:9). This is the very end of the long-suffering of God toward believers: that before they depart from here they may come to the sense, acknowledgment, and repentance of every known sin. This is the constant and unchangeable rule of God’s patience in the covenant of grace. It is so far from being an encouragement to sin in them that it is a motive to watchfulness against every sin, of the same nature as all gospel grace and as mercy in the blood of Christ. Now the dealing we are speaking of would directly contradict it.

2nd. Secondly, this also follows from the first. Conceived sin contains the whole nature of sin, as our Savior declares at length (Matt 5). And to be cut off under its guilt, in order to prevent its further progress, implies continuing in the purpose of it without repentance. So those who are cut off in this way in judgment cannot but perish forever. But God does not deal so with his own; he does not cast off the people whom he foreknew. That is why David prays for the patience of God just mentioned, that it might not be so with him (Ps 39:13): “Spare me, that I may recover strength before I depart and am no more.” But yet:

[2.] Secondly, there are some cases in which God may, and does, take away the lives of his own to prevent the guilt they would otherwise be involved in. For example:

1st. First, in the coming of some great temptation and trial upon the world. God knows that this or that one of his own would not be able to withstand it and hold out against it, but would dishonor him and defile himself. So he may, and no doubt often does, take them out of the world, to take them out of its way (Isa 57:1): “the righteous are taken away from the evil to come.” This means not only the evil of punishment and judgment, but the evil of temptations and trials, which often proves much the worse of the two. In the same way a captain in war will call a soldier off from his watch and guard when he knows that, through some weakness, the soldier is not able to bear the pressure and force of the enemy coming upon him.

2nd. Secondly, in the case of their having entered on any way not acceptable to him, through ignorance, or through not knowing his mind and will. This seems to have been the case with Josiah. And no doubt the Lord often deals with his own in this way. When any of his own are engaged in ways that do not please him, through the darkness and ignorance of their minds, he calls them off from their station and employment, so that they may not go on to further evil or mischief. He takes them to himself, where they will err and mistake no more. But in ordinary cases God has other ways of turning his own from sin than by killing them, as we will see afterward.

(2.) Secondly, God in his providence hinders the bringing forth of conceived sin by taking away, and cutting short, the power of those who had conceived it. Their lives continue, but they will not have the power without which it is impossible for them to carry out what they had intended, or to bring forth what they had conceived. We have several instances of this as well. Such was the case with the builders of Babel (Gen 11). Whatever it was in particular that they aimed at, it was in pursuit of a design of apostasy from God. One thing needed to accomplish their aim was the oneness of their language. So God says (v. 6), “If they have begun to do this as one people speaking the same language, then nothing they devise will be beyond them.” In the ordinary course of things they will accomplish their wicked design. What course does God now take to forestall their conceived sin? Does he bring a flood on them to destroy them, as on the old world some time before? Does he send his angel to cut them off, as with the army of Sennacherib afterward? Does he take away their lives by any means? No. Their lives are continued, but he confuses their language, so that they cannot go on with their work (v. 7). He takes away the thing in which their power lay. In the same way he dealt with the men of Sodom (Gen 19:11). They were engaged in and set on the pursuit of their filthy lusts. God strikes them with blindness, so that they could not find the door where they intended to use violence to get what they wanted. Their lives were continued, and their will to sin, but their power is cut short and curtailed. His dealing with Jeroboam was of the same nature (1 Kgs 13:4). He “stretched out his hand” to seize the prophet, and it withered and became useless. And this is a notable way in which God’s providence acts effectually in the world to stop that flood of sin which would overflow all the earth if every womb of it were opened. He cuts men short of the moral power by which they would accomplish it. Many a wretch who has conceived mischief against the church of God has by this means been stripped of the power by which he thought to accomplish it. Some have their bodies struck with diseases, so that they can no longer serve their lusts or go along with them in committing folly. Some are deprived of the instruments with which they would work. For a long time there has been sin and mischief enough conceived to root out the generation of the righteous from the face of the earth, if men had strength and ability to match their will, and if God did not cut off and shorten their power and the days of their prevailing. Ps 64:6: “They devise injustice and say, ‘We have perfected a secret plan.’ For the inner man and the heart are deep.” Everything is in readiness; the design is well laid; their counsels are deep and secret. What will now hinder them from doing whatever they have imagined? Vv. 7, 8: “But God will shoot them with arrows; suddenly they will be wounded. They will be made to stumble, their own tongues turned against them.” God meets them and brings them down, so that they will not be able to accomplish their design. This way of God’s preventing sin seems to be found, at least ordinarily, only among the men of the world. God deals with them so every day, and leaves them to waste away in their sins. They go all their days big with the iniquity they have conceived, and are greatly burdened that they cannot be delivered of it. The prophet tells us that they accomplish the iniquity they had conceived “because the power is in their hands” (Mic 2:1). If they have power for it, they will accomplish it. Ezek 22:6: “Every prince of Israel within you has used his power to shed blood.” This is the measure of their sinning: their power. Many of them do no more evil, commit no more sin, only because they cannot. Their whole restraint lies in being cut short in power in one way or another. Their bodies will not serve them for the uncleanness they have planned, nor their hands for their revenge and plunder, nor their instruments for persecution. But they go burdened with conceived sin, and are disquieted and tortured by it all their days. That is why they become, to themselves as well as to others, “like the storm-tossed sea, for it cannot be still” (Isa 57:20).

It may also be that in some cases, under some violent temptations or in mistakes, God forestalls the accomplishment of conceived sin in his own in this way. There seems to be an instance of it in his dealing with Jehoshaphat. Against the mind of God he had planned to join in alliance with Ahab, and to send his ships with him to Tarshish. But God breaks his ships by a wind, so that he could not accomplish what he had planned. But when God deals with his own in this way, there is a difference from the same dealing with others. For:

[1.] First, it is so only in cases of extraordinary temptation. When through the violence of temptation and the craft of Satan they are hurried out from under the rule of the law of grace, God in one way or another takes away their power, or may do so, so that they will not be able to carry out what they had planned. But with wicked men this is an ordinary way of dealing. This hook of God is in them through the whole course of their lives. They struggle with it, like a wild bull in a net (Isa 51:20). God’s net is upon them, and they are filled with fury that they cannot do all the wickedness they would.

[2.] Secondly, God does not do it to leave his own wrestling with sin and trying other ways of accomplishing it when the way they were engaged in has failed. Rather, by their disappointment he awakens them to consider their condition and what they are doing. And so he consumes sin in the womb by the ways that will be dwelt on afterward. For some men, being deprived of power to commit a sin they had conceived and contrived has been sanctified to change their hearts from all toying with that sin or any other.

(3.) Thirdly, God in his providence hinders the bringing forth of conceived sin by setting an outward hindering power against sinners. He leaves them their lives, and leaves them power to do what they intend, but he raises up an opposing power to coerce, forbid, and restrain them. We have an instance of this in 1 Sam 14:45. Saul had sworn that Jonathan should be put to death, and as far as appears he went on resolutely to kill him. God stirs up the spirit of the people; they set themselves against the wrath and fury of Saul, and Jonathan is delivered. So also 2 Chr 26:7, 8, 19. When King Uzziah would have offered incense in his own person contrary to the law, eighty of the priests resisted him and drove him out of the temple. Under this head belong all the help God stirs up to deliver his people from the fury of persecutors. He raises up “deliverers on Mount Zion to rule over the mountains of Esau.” So in Rev 12:16: the dragon, and those acting under him and moved by his spirit, were striving furiously to destroy the church. God stirs up the earth to help her, that is, men of the world who are not joined with the others in Satan’s design. By their opposition he hinders the others from carrying out the rage they had planned. God’s dealing with his own people in Hos 2:6, 7 seems to be of this nature. They were pursuing their iniquities, going after their lovers. God leaves them for a while to act in the folly of their spirits, but he sets a hedge and a wall before them, so that they will not be able to fulfill their designs and lusts.

(4.) Fourthly, God forestalls the accomplishment of conceived sin by removing, or taking away, the objects on whom, or concerning whom, the conceived sin was to be committed. Acts 12:11 gives us a notable instance of this outcome of providence. When the day was coming on which Herod intended to kill Peter, who was shut up in prison, God sends and takes Peter away from their rage and their lying in wait. So too our Savior himself was taken away from the murderous rage of the Jews before his hour had come (John 8:59; ch. 10:39). Both early and later times are full of stories of this kind. Prison doors have been opened, and poor creatures appointed to die have often been rescued from the jaws of death. In the world itself, among its own men, adulterers and adulteresses, the sin of the one is often hindered and stifled by the taking away of the other. So wings were given to the woman to carry her into the wilderness, and to frustrate the world in carrying out its rage (Rev 12:14).

(5.) Fifthly, God does this by some notable diversions of the thoughts of men who had conceived sin (Gen 37:24). The brothers of Joseph threw him into a pit, intending to starve him there. While they were, it seems, pleasing themselves with what they had done, God arranges for a company of merchants to come by. With that new object he turns their thoughts from killing their brother to selling him (vv. 26, 27). And we know how far in this they served the infinitely wise counsel of God. So also when Saul was pursuing David, and was just about to prevail against him to his destruction, God stirs up the Philistines to invade the land. This both turned Saul’s thoughts aside and drew the course of his actions another way (1 Sam 23:27).

These are some of the ways in which God is pleased to hinder the bringing forth of conceived sin by setting himself and his providence against the power of the sinning creature. And in passing we may take a brief look at the great advantages to faith and to the church of God that may be found in this matter. For example:

[1.] First, this may give us a little insight into the providence of God, ever to be adored, which by these and similar ways, in great variety, obstructs the breaking out of sin in the world. It is he who builds those dams and shuts those floodgates of corrupted nature, so that it does not break out in a deluge of filthy abominations to overwhelm the creation with confusion and disorder. As it was of old, so it is today: “every inclination of the thoughts of man’s heart is altogether evil all the time.” That all the earth is not filled in every place with violence, as it was of old, is solely from the mighty hand of God working effectually to obstruct sin. It is from this alone that the highways, streets, and fields are not all filled with violence, blood, plunder, uncleanness, and every villainy the heart of man can conceive. O the infinite beauty of divine wisdom and providence in the government of the world! For preserving it daily requires no less power and wisdom than its first making did.

[2.] Secondly, if we look to our own concerns, they will in a special way press us to adore the wisdom and efficacy of God’s providence in stopping the progress of conceived sin. That we are at peace in our houses, at rest in our beds, and have any quiet in what we enjoy, comes from this alone. Whose person would not be defiled or destroyed? Whose home would not be ruined? Whose blood, almost, would not be shed, if wicked men had power to carry out all the sin they conceive? It may be that the ruin of some of us has been conceived a thousand times. We are indebted to this providence that obstructs sin for our lives, our families, our estates, our liberties, and whatever is or may be dear to us. For may we not sometimes say with the psalmist (Ps 57:4), “My soul is among the lions; I lie among those who are set on fire—with men whose teeth are spears and arrows, whose tongues are sharp swords.” And how is deliverance from such persons brought about? Ps 58:6: “God shatters their teeth in their mouths, the fangs of the young lions.” He keeps this fire from burning, or quenches it when it is ready to break out into a flame. He breaks their spears and arrows, so that sometimes we are not even wounded by them. Some he cuts off and destroys; some he cuts short in their power; some he deprives of the instruments by which alone they can work; some he keeps from the opportunities they desire, or turns aside by other objects for their lusts; and often he makes them spend their lusts among themselves, one upon another. We may say, therefore, with the psalmist (Ps 104:24), “How many are Your works, O Lord! In wisdom You have made them all; the earth is full of Your creatures”; and with the prophet (Hos 14:9), “Whoever is wise, let him understand these things; whoever is discerning, let him know them. For the ways of the Lord are right, and the righteous walk in them, but the rebellious stumble in them.”

[3.] Thirdly, if these and similar ways are the ways in which God forestalls the bringing forth of conceived sin in wicked men, we may learn from this how miserable their condition is, and in what constant torment they spend their days, for the most part. They are “like the storm-tossed sea,” says the Lord, “for it cannot be still.” As they try to make sure that others have no peace, so it is certain that they themselves have none. The principle of sin is not impaired or weakened in them; the will to sin is not taken away. They have a womb of sin that is able to conceive monsters every moment. Indeed, for the most part they are forging and framing folly all day long. They are contriving how to satisfy one lust or another. They are devouring by malice and revenge, or corrupting by uncleanness, or trampling by ambition, or swallowing up by covetousness, all who stand before them. Many of their follies and mischiefs they bring to the very point of birth, and are in pain to be delivered. But God fills them with disappointment every day, and shuts up the womb of sin. Some are filled with hatred of God’s people all their days, and never once have an opportunity to act on it. So David describes them (Ps 59:6): “They return in the evening, snarling like dogs and prowling around the city.” Verse 7: “See what they spew from their mouths—sharp words from their lips.” And yet they are not able to accomplish their designs. What torture do such poor creatures live in! Envy, malice, wrath, and revenge devour their hearts for lack of an outlet. And when God has used the other acts of his wise providence, cutting short their power or setting a greater power against them, and nothing else will do, he cuts them off in their sins, and to the grave they go full of purposes of iniquity. Others are no less driven and pulled about by the power of other lusts that they are not able to satisfy. This is the heavy labor they are occupied with all their days. If they accomplish their designs, they are more wicked and hellish than before; and if they do not, they are filled with vexation and discontent. This is the portion of those who do not know the Lord, nor the power of his grace. Do not envy their condition. Whatever their outward glittering show, their hearts are full of anxiety, trouble, and sorrow.

[4.] Fourthly, do we sometimes see the floodgates of men’s lusts and rage opened against the church and its cause? Does success go with them, and is power on their side for a season? Let not the saints of God lose heart. He has unspeakably varied and effectual ways of stifling what they have conceived, to give them dry breasts and a miscarrying womb. He can stop their fury when he pleases. “Surely,” says the psalmist, “the wrath of man shall praise You; the remainder of wrath You will restrain” (Ps 76:10). When so much of their wrath has been let out as will exalt his praise, he can, when he pleases, set up a power greater than the combined strength of all sinning creatures, and restrain the remainder of the wrath they had conceived. “He cuts off the spirits of princes; He is feared by the kings of the earth” (v. 12). Some he will cut off and destroy, some he will terrify and frighten, and he will prevent the rage of them all.
