He can knock them on the head, or break out their teeth, or chain up their wrath; and who can oppose him?

[5.] Fifthly, those who have received benefit by any of the ways mentioned may know to whom they owe their preservation, and not look on it as a common thing. When you+ have conceived sin, has God weakened your+ power for sin, or denied you+ opportunity, or taken away the object of your+ lusts, or turned your+ thoughts aside by new providences? Know assuredly that you+ have received mercy by it. God does not always deal out these providences in subservience to the covenant of grace, yet there is always mercy in them, and always a call in them to consider their Author. If God had not dealt with you+ in this way, perhaps today you+ would have been a terror to yourselves+, a shame to your+ relatives, and under the punishment due to some notorious sins you+ had conceived. Besides, there is commonly an added guilt in sin brought forth, beyond what there is in its mere conception. Perhaps others would have been ruined by it here, or drawn into partnership in the sin by it, and so been ruined eternally by it. All this is prevented by these providences, and eternity will bear witness that there is a singular mercy in them. Do not look on such things, then, as common accidents. The hand of God is in them all, and it is a merciful hand, if it is not despised. If it is despised, God still does good to others by it: the world is the better for it, and you+ are not as wicked as you+ would be.

[6.] Sixthly, we may also see from this the great use of magistracy in the world, that great appointment of God. Among other things it serves this holy providence in a special way, in obstructing the bringing forth of conceived sin; it does so by the terror of him who bears the sword. God fixes on the hearts of evil men what he expresses in Rom 13:4: “If you do wrong, be afraid, for he does not carry the sword in vain. He is God’s servant, an agent of retribution to the wrongdoer.” God fixes this on the hearts of men, and by its dread and terror closes the womb of sin, so that it does not bring forth. When there was “no king in Israel,” no one to rebuke, and no one whom evil men feared, woeful work and havoc was made among the children of men in the world, as we may see in the last chapters of the book of Judges. The greatest mercies and blessings we partake of in this world, next to those of the gospel and the covenant of grace, come to us through this channel and conduit. Indeed, what we have been speaking of is the proper work of magistracy: to serve the providence of God in obstructing the bringing forth of conceived sin.

These, then, are some of the ways in which God in his providence prevents the bringing forth of sin by setting obstacles against the power of the sinner. By them sin is not consumed, but shut up in the womb. Men are not burdened for it, but with it. They are not weighed down in heart and conscience with its guilt, but troubled with its power, which they are not able to exercise and satisfy.

God’s Working on the Will of the Sinner

The way that still remains to be considered, by which God forestalls the production of conceived sin, is his working on the will of sinners, so making sin waste away in the womb.

In general there are two ways in which God prevents the bringing forth of conceived sin by working on the will of the sinner. They are, first, by restraining grace; secondly, by renewing grace. He does it sometimes in the one way, sometimes in the other. The first is common to regenerate and unregenerate persons; the second belongs only to believers. And by both of them God works in varied ways as to particulars. We will begin with the first.

1. First, God does this in the way of restraining grace by some arrow of particular conviction fixed in the heart and conscience of the sinner, concerning the particular sin he had conceived. This staggers and changes his mind about the particular thing intended. It makes his hands hang down, and the weapons of lust fall out of them. By this conceived sin proves abortive. How God does this work, by what immediate touches, strokes, blows, and rebukes of his Spirit, by what reasonings, arguments, and stirrings of men’s own consciences, is not for us to find out fully. It is done, as was said, in unspeakable variety, and the works of God are past finding out. But after we have shown the general way of God’s proceeding, we will dwell on what light may be given to it from instances in Scripture.

This, then, was how God dealt in the case of Esau and Jacob. Esau had long conceived his brother’s death. He comforted himself with the thought of it and with resolutions about it (Gen 27:41), as is the manner of profligate sinners. At his first opportunity he comes out to carry out the rage he intended, and Jacob concludes that he would “attack the mothers and children” (Gen 32:11). An opportunity is presented to this wicked and profane man to bring forth the sin that had lain in his heart for twenty years now. He has full power in his hand to carry out his purpose. In the middle of this state of things, God comes in on his heart with some secret and effectual working of his Spirit and power. He turns him from his purpose and makes his conceived sin melt away, so that he falls on the neck of the man he had thought to kill and embraces him.

God’s dealing with Laban the Aramean, concerning the same Jacob, was of the same nature, though the way of it was unusual (Gen 31:24). By a dream, a vision in the night, God keeps him from so much as speaking harshly to Jacob. It was with Laban as in Mic 2:1: he had devised evil on his bed, and when he intended to carry it out in the morning, God stepped in with a dream and hid sin from him, as it is put in Job 33:15, 16, 17. To the same purpose is what the psalmist says about the people of God (Ps 106:46): “He made them objects of compassion to all who held them captive.” Men usually deal harshly with those they have taken captive in war. It was the way of old to rule captives with force and cruelty. Here God turns and changes their captors’ hearts, not in general to himself, but in this particular of regard for his people. And in this way, in general, God prevents the bringing forth of a world of sin every day. He sharpens arrows of conviction against the spirits of men concerning the particular thing they are engaged in. Their hearts are not changed as to sin, but their minds are altered as to this or that sin. Perhaps they break the vessel they had fashioned and go to work on some other. Now, so that we may see a little into the ways in which God accomplishes this work, we must lay down the following considerations first.

(1.) First, the general means in which the material of restraining grace consists, by which God prevents the bringing forth of sin in this way, lies in certain arguments and reasonings presented to the mind of the sinner. By them he is led to abandon his purpose, and to change and alter his mind about the sin he had conceived. Reasons against it are presented to him, and they prevail on him to give up his design and abandon his purpose. This is the general way restraining grace works: by arguments and reasonings rising up against committing conceived sin.

(2.) Secondly, no arguments or reasonings as such, considered in their content, are sufficient to stop or hinder any purpose of sinning, or to make conceived sin prove abortive, if the sinner has power and opportunity to bring it forth. They are not restraining grace in themselves and on their own account. If they were, the administration and imparting of grace, as grace, would be left to every man who is able to give advice against sin. Nothing is or can be called grace, even common grace that may perish, except with respect to its particular relation to God. God, by the power of his Spirit making arguments and reasons effectual and prevailing, turns into grace (I mean grace of this kind) what was in itself and in its own nature mere reason. And that efficacy of the Spirit which the Lord puts forth in these persuasions and motives is what we call restraining grace. With these things laid down, we will now consider some of the arguments we find he has used for this end and purpose.

[1.] First, God stops many men in their ways, once sin is conceived, by an argument taken from the difficulty, if not the impossibility, of doing what they aim at. They have a mind to it, but God sets a hedge and a wall before them, so that they judge what they intend so hard and difficult to accomplish that it is better for them to let it alone and give it up. So Herod would have put John the Baptist to death at the first provocation, but he “was afraid of the people, because they regarded John as a prophet” (Matt 14:5). He had conceived John’s murder and was ready to carry it out. God raised this consideration in his heart: “If I kill him, the people will riot. He has a great following among them, and a revolt will arise that may cost me my life or my kingdom.” He “was afraid of the people,” and did not dare to carry out the wickedness he had conceived, because of the difficulty he foresaw he would be entangled in. And God made the argument effectual for that season. Otherwise, we know that men will venture the utmost hazards to satisfy their lusts, as Herod also did afterward. The Pharisees were in exactly the same state and condition (Matt 21:26). They would gladly have denounced the ministry of John, but did not dare to for fear of the people. And in v. 46 of the same chapter they were deterred by the same argument from killing our Savior, who had greatly provoked them by a parable that set out their deserved and approaching destruction. They did not dare to do it for fear of a riot among the people, since the people looked on him as a prophet. In this way God overawes the hearts of countless persons in the world every day, and makes them give up attempting to bring forth the sins they had conceived. They would be sure to meet difficulties; indeed, it is likely that if they attempted it, they would find it impossible to accomplish. We owe much of our quiet in this world to the efficacy the Holy Spirit gives this consideration in the hearts of men. Adulteries, robberies, and murders are forestalled and stifled by it. Men would engage in them daily, if they did not judge it impossible to fulfill what they aim at.

[2.] Secondly, God does it by an argument ab incommodo, from the harms, evils, and troubles that will come upon men in the pursuit of sin. If they follow it, this or that harm will follow: this trouble, this evil, temporal or eternal. This argument, as the Spirit of God handles it, is the great engine in his hand by which he throws up banks and sets bounds to the lusts of men, so that they do not break out to the confusion of all the order and beauty that still remains in the works of his hands. Paul gives us the general meaning of this argument in Rom 2:14, 15: “Indeed, when Gentiles, who do not have the law, do by nature what the law requires, they are a law to themselves, even though they do not have the law. So they show that the work of the law is written on their hearts, their consciences also bearing witness, and their thoughts either accusing or defending them.” If any men in the world might be thought to be given up to pursue and fulfill all the sins their lusts can conceive, it is those who “do not have the law,” those to whom the written law of God does not announce the evil that goes with sin. But though they do not have it, says the apostle, they show the work of it. They do many things it requires, and refrain or abstain from many things it forbids, and so show its work and efficacy. But how is it that they do so? Why, their thoughts accuse or defend them. It comes from the considerations and arguments they have within themselves about sin and its consequences. These prevail on them to abstain from many things their hearts would carry them into. For conscience is a man’s judging of himself beforehand, with respect to the future judgment of God. So Felix was staggered in his pursuit of sin when he trembled at Paul’s preaching of righteousness and the judgment to come (Acts 24:25). So Job tells us that the consideration of punishment from God has a strong influence on the minds of men to keep them from sin (ch. 31:1, 2, 3). I have declared before how the Lord uses that consideration even toward his own, when they have broken the cords of his love and thrown off the rule of his grace for a season.

[3.] Thirdly, God does this same work by making effectual an argument ab inutili, from the unprofitableness of the thing men are engaged in. By this the brothers of Joseph were held back from killing him (Gen 37:26, 27). “What profit will we gain,” they say, “if we kill our brother and cover up his blood?” We will get nothing by it; it will bring us no advantage or satisfaction. The heads of this way in which God obstructs conceived sin, or the springs of these kinds of arguments, are so many and various that it is impossible to dwell on them one by one. There is nothing present or to come, nothing belonging to this life or another, nothing desirable or undesirable, nothing good or evil, from which an argument may not be taken at one time or another to obstruct sin.

[4.] Fourthly, God accomplishes this work by arguments taken ab honesto [from the honorable], from what is good and honorable, what is fitting, praiseworthy, and acceptable to himself. This is the great road on which he walks with the saints under their temptations, or when they conceive sin. He effectually brings back to their minds the consideration of all those springs and motives of obedience that are revealed and set before us in the gospel, some at one time, some at another. He reminds them of his own love, mercy, and kindness, his eternal love, with the fruits of it that they themselves have shared in. He reminds them of the blood of his Son, his cross, his sufferings, his tremendous undertaking in the work of mediation, and how his heart, love, honor, and name are concerned in their obedience. He reminds them of the love of the Spirit, with all his comforts that they have shared in, and the privileges entrusted to them by him. He reminds them of the gospel, its glory and beauty as revealed to their souls. He reminds them of the excellence and beauty of obedience, of their doing the duty they owe to God, and of the peace, quietness, and calm of mind they have enjoyed in it. On the other side, he reminds them that by sin they are a provocation to the eyes of his glory, saying in their hearts, “Do not do this detestable thing that I hate.” He reminds them that they wound the Lord Christ and put him to shame; that they grieve the Holy Spirit, by whom they are sealed for the day of redemption; that they defile his dwelling place. He reminds them of the reproach, dishonor, and scandal they bring on the gospel and the profession of it. He reminds them of the terrors, darkness, wounds, and lack of peace they may bring on their own souls. From these and similar considerations God puts a stop to the progress of the law of sin in the heart, so that it does not go on to bring forth the evil it has conceived. I could give instances recorded in Scripture of arguments of all these several kinds, but that would be too long a work for us, who are now engaged in a design of another nature. One or two examples may be mentioned, though. Joseph resists his first temptation on one of these grounds (Gen 39:9): “So how could I do such a great evil and sin against God?” The evil of sinning against God, his God: that consideration alone keeps him from the least inclination to his temptation. “It is sin against God, to whom I owe all obedience, the God of my life and of all my mercies; I will not do it.” The argument by which Abigail prevailed on David to hold him back from avenging himself and from murder was of the same nature (1 Sam 25:31), and he acknowledges that it was from the Lord (v. 32). I will add no more, for all the motives to duty we have in Scripture, made effectual by grace, are instances of this way of God’s proceeding.

Sometimes, I confess, God works secretly on the hearts of men with his own finger to stop the progress of sin, without the use and means of such arguments as those we have dwelt on. So he tells Abimelech (Gen 20:6), “I have kept you from sinning against Me.” Now this could not have been done by any of the arguments we have dwelt on, because Abimelech did not know that what he intended was sin. That is why he pleads that he did it “in the integrity of his heart and the innocence of his hands” (v. 5). God turned his will and thoughts around, so that he did not accomplish his intention; but by what ways or means is not revealed. Nor is it evident what course God took in changing Esau’s heart when he came out against his brother to destroy him (Gen 33:4). Did he stir up in him a fresh spring of natural affection? Did he make him consider what grief he would bring by this means on his aged father, who loved him so tenderly? Or, having now grown great and wealthy, did Esau more and more despise the matter in dispute between him and his brother, and so utterly disregard it? It is not known. Perhaps God did it by an immediate, powerful act of his Spirit on his heart, without any of these or similar considerations actually coming between. Now the things mentioned are, at other times, feeble and weak in themselves. Yet when they are handled by the Spirit of God for such an end and purpose, they certainly become effectual, and are the material of his preventing grace.

2. Secondly, God prevents the bringing forth of conceived sin by real, spiritual, saving grace, either in the first conversion of sinners or in the further supplies of it that follow. This is one part of the mystery of his grace and love. He sometimes meets men in their highest resolutions for sin with the highest efficacy of his grace. By this he shows the power of his own grace. And he gives the soul a further experience of the law of sin when the soul takes its leave of that law by being changed in the middle of its resolutions to serve its lusts. By this he melts down the lusts of men and makes them wither at the root, so that they no longer strive to bring forth what they have conceived, but are filled with shame and sorrow that they conceived it. We have an example and instance of this proceeding of God in Paul, for the use and instruction of all generations. His heart was full of wickedness, blasphemy, and persecution. What he had conceived had come to rage and madness, and to a full purpose of exercising it all to the utmost; so the history relates it (Acts 9). And so he himself declares his state to have been (Acts 26:9, 10, 11, 12; 1 Tim 1:13). In the middle of all this violent pursuit of sin, a voice from heaven shuts up the womb and dries up its breasts, and he cries, “What should I do, Lord?” (Acts 9:6). The same person seems to imply that this is God’s way of proceeding with others too: that he meets them with his converting grace at the height of their sin and folly (1 Tim 1:16). For he himself, he says, was an example of God’s dealing with others; as God dealt with him, so he would also do with some other such sinners. “But for this very reason I was shown mercy, so that in me, the worst of sinners, Christ Jesus might display His perfect patience as an example to those who would believe in Him for eternal life.” And we have not a few examples of it in our own days. Several persons who deliberately went to this or that place to mock and scoff at the preaching of the word have been met in the very place where they planned to serve their lusts and Satan, and have been cast down at the feet of God. This way of God’s dealing with sinners is set out at length in Job 33:15, 16, 17, 18. Dionysius the Areopagite is another instance of this work of God’s grace and love. Paul is dragged either by him or before him to plead for his life as a proclaimer of foreign gods, which at Athens was death by the law. In the middle of this frame of spirit God meets Dionysius with converting grace. Sin withers in the womb, and he joins himself to Paul and his doctrine (Acts 17:18–34). We have a similar dealing toward Israel in Hos 11:7, 8, 9, 10. But there is no need to dwell on more instances of this observation. God is pleased to leave no generation unconvinced of this truth, if only they attend to their own experiences and to the examples of this work of his mercy among them. Every day one or another is taken in the full purpose of his heart to go on in sin, in this or that sin, and is stopped in his course by the power of converting grace.

(2.) Secondly, God does it by the same grace in its renewed supplies, that is, by special assisting grace. This is his common way of dealing with believers in this case. It was declared before that they too, through the deceitfulness of sin, may be carried on to conceive this or that sin. God puts a stop to their progress, or rather to the prevailing power of the law of sin in them, by giving them the special help needed for their preservation and deliverance. As David says of himself (Ps 73:2), “my feet had almost stumbled; my steps had nearly slipped.” He was at the very brink of unbelieving, despairing thoughts and conclusions about God’s providence in governing the world, and he was recovered from them, as he declares afterward. So it is with many a believer. He is often at the very brink, at the very door, of some folly or iniquity, when God steps in with the efficacy of actually assisting grace and recovers such believers to an obedient frame of heart again. And this is a special work of Christ, in which he shows and exercises his faithfulness toward his own. Heb 2:18: “He is able to help those who are being tempted.” It is not an absolute power that is meant, but a power clothed with mercy, a power put forth from a sense of what poor believers suffer under their temptations. And how does he exercise this merciful ability toward us? Heb 4:16: he gives out, and we find in him, “grace to help us in our time of need”; timely help and assistance for our deliverance when we are ready to be overpowered by sin and temptation. When “lust has conceived” and is ready to “bring forth,” when the soul lies at the brink of some iniquity, he gives timely help, relief, deliverance, and safety. A great part of Christ’s care and faithfulness toward his poor saints lies here. He will not allow them to be harried by the power of sin, nor to be carried out into ways that will dishonor the gospel, or fill them with shame and reproach, and so make them useless in the world. Instead he steps in with the saving relief and assistance of his grace, stops the course of sin, and makes them “more than conquerors” in him. And this assistance lies under the promise of 1 Cor 10:13: “No temptation has seized you+ except what is common to man. And God is faithful; He will not let you+ be tempted beyond what you+ can bear. But when you+ are tempted, He will also provide a way of escape, so that you+ can stand up under it.” Temptation will try us; it is for our good, and the Lord brings about many holy ends by it. But when we are tried to the utmost of our ability, so that one more assault would overpower us, a “way of escape” is provided. This may be done in several ways, as I have declared elsewhere, and the way we are now speaking of is one of the most eminent: supplies of grace to enable the soul to hold up, resist, and conquer. And once God begins to deal with a soul in this way of love, he will not stop adding one supply after another until the whole work of his grace and faithfulness is accomplished. We have an example of this in Isa 57:17, 18. Poor sinners there are so far held captive to the power of their lusts that God’s first and second dealings with them do not effectually deliver them. But he will not give up. He is pursuing a design of love toward them, and so he does not stop until they are recovered. These are the general heads of the second way in which God hinders the bringing forth of conceived sin, that is, by working on the will of the sinner. He does it either by common convictions of sin or by special grace, so that sinners let go of their own accord the purpose and will to sin that they had risen up to. And this is no small part of his providing for his own glory and for the honor of his gospel in the world. Those who profess it would stain all its beauty if they were left to themselves to bring forth all the evil conceived in their hearts.

The Way of Afflictions

Besides these general ways, there is one still more special, which works on both the power and the will of the sinner at once. This is the way of afflictions, and one word about it will close this discourse. Afflictions, I say, work in both these ways with regard to conceived sin. They work providentially on the power of the person. When a man has conceived a sin and is fully set on pursuing it, God often sends a sickness and weakens his strength, or a loss that cuts him short in his plenty. So he takes the man off from the pursuit of his lusts, though perhaps his heart is not weaned from them. His power is weakened, and he cannot do the evil he would. In this sense affliction belongs to the first way in which God forestalls the production of sin. Great afflictions sometimes work, not immediately and directly from their own nature, but from the gracious purpose and intention of him who sends them. Into his dealing out of them he puts something of grace and power, of love and kindness, which effectually draws the heart and mind away from sin. Ps 119:67: “Before I was afflicted, I went astray; but now I keep Your word.” And in this way, because renewing and assisting grace predominates in them, afflictions belong to the second means of preventing sin.

These are some of the ways in which it pleases God to put a stop to the progress of sin, both in believers and in unbelievers, that we will give as instances at present. And if we tried to search out his ways further, to perfection, we would still have to conclude that it is only a little portion that we know of him.

Chapter XIV

The power of sin further demonstrated by the effects it has had in the lives of professing Christians: first, in actual sins; secondly, in habitual declines.

We must now go on to other evidences of the sad truth we are demonstrating. But since the main part of our work has been gone through, I will be briefer in handling the arguments that remain.

The next thing we may fix on, then, is the demonstration this law of sin has given in all ages of its power and efficacy, by the woeful fruits it has brought forth even in believers themselves. These are of two sorts. First, the great actual outbreaks of sin in their lives. Secondly, their habitual declines from the frames, state, and condition of obedience and communion with God that they had attained. By the rule of James opened up before, both are to be laid to the account of this law of sin, and belong to the fourth head of its progress. Both are convincing evidences of its power and efficacy.

1. First, consider the fearful outbreaks of actual sin there have been in the lives of believers, and we will find our position proved. If I were to go through this consideration at length, I would have to recount all the sad and scandalous failings of the saints left on record in Holy Scripture. But their particulars are known to all, so I will not need to mention them, nor the many circumstances that made them worse. Only a few things may be noted that will help make our present consideration of them useful. For example:

(1.) First, most of them are in the lives of men who were not of the lowest rank or ordinary sort of believers. They were men who had a special eminence on account of their walking with God in their generation. Such were Noah, Lot, David, Hezekiah, and others. They were not men of ordinary size, but taller than their brothers from the shoulders up in what they professed, and indeed in real holiness. And surely that must be of mighty efficacy which could hurry such giants in the ways of God into such abominable sins as they fell into. No ordinary engine could ever have turned them out of the course of their obedience. It was a poison that no athletic constitution of spiritual health and no antidote could withstand.

(2.) Secondly, these very men did not fall into their great sins at the beginning of their profession of faith. They did not fall when they had had only a little experience of the goodness of God, of the sweetness and pleasantness of obedience, and of the power and craft of sin, its drivings, solicitations, and surprises. They fell after a long course of walking with God and of acquaintance with all these things, together with countless motives to watchfulness. Noah, by the length of men’s lives in those days of the world, had walked uprightly with God some hundreds of years before he was surprised as he was (Gen 9). Righteous Lot seems to have been toward the end of his days before he defiled himself with the abominations recorded. David, in a short life, had as much experience of grace and of sin, and as much close spiritual communion with God, as any of the sons of men ever had, before he was thrown to the ground by this law of sin. So it was with Hezekiah in his degree, which was by no means a low one. Now to set upon such persons, so well acquainted with its power and deceit, so armed and provided against it, who had been conquerors over it for so many years, and to prevail against them, shows a power and efficacy too mighty for anything to withstand but the Spirit of the Almighty. Who can expect to have a greater stock of inherent grace than those men had? Who can expect more experience of God and of the excellence of his ways, of the sweetness of his love, and of communion with him, than they had? Who has either better equipment to oppose sin with, or more obligation to do so, than they? And yet we see how fearfully they were prevailed against.

(3.) Thirdly, as if God had permitted their falls on purpose, so that we might learn to be wary of this powerful enemy, they all fell when they had newly received great and astonishing mercies from the hand of God. These ought to have been strong obligations to diligence and watchfulness in close obedience. Noah had only just come out of that world of waters in which he saw the ungodly world perishing for their sins, and himself preserved by that astonishing miracle which all ages must wonder at. While the world’s desolation was an hourly reminder to him of his remarkable preservation by the immediate care and hand of God, he falls into drunkenness. Lot had just seen what no one can think of without trembling. He saw, as one puts it, “hell coming out of heaven” upon unclean sinners: the greatest evidence God ever gave in his providence of the judgment to come, except the cross of Christ. He saw himself and his children delivered by the special care and miraculous hand of God. And yet while these remarkable mercies were fresh upon him, he fell into drunkenness and incest. David was delivered from all his troubles and had the necks of his enemies all around given to him; and he uses his peace from a world of trials and troubles to contrive murder and adultery. It was immediately after Hezekiah’s great and miraculous deliverance that he falls into his carnal pride and boasting. Their falls at such seasons, I say, seem to have been permitted on purpose, to instruct us all in the truth we have in hand. No persons, at any season, with whatever equipment of grace, can promise themselves security from its prevailing power, except by keeping close constantly to him who has supplies to give out that are beyond its reach and efficacy. I think this should make us look about us. Are we better than Noah, who had this testimony from God, that he was “blameless in his generation, and walked with God?” Are we better than Lot, whose righteous soul was tormented by the evil deeds of ungodly men, and who is commended for it by the Holy Spirit? Are we more holy, wise, and watchful than David, who obtained this testimony, that he was “a man after My own heart?” Or better than Hezekiah, who appealed to God himself that he had served him uprightly with a perfect heart? And yet we see what prevailing power this law of sin worked in them and over them. There is no end of similar examples. They are all set up as buoys to show us the sands, the shoals, and the rocks on which they suffered their shipwreck. It was to their hazard, danger, and loss; indeed, it would have been to their ruin, if God had not been pleased in his faithfulness graciously to prevent it. And this is the first part of this evidence of the power of sin, from its effects.

2. Secondly, sin shows its power in the habitual declines found in many believers: declines from zeal and holiness, from the frames, state, and condition of obedience and communion with God they had attained. Promises of growth and progress are many and precious; the means are excellent and effectual; the benefits are great and unspeakable. Yet it often happens that, instead of this, decays and declines are found in professing Christians, and indeed in many of the saints of God. Now this must come principally and chiefly from the strength and efficacy of indwelling sin, and is therefore a great evidence of it. So I will first prove the observation itself to be true: that some of the saints themselves often decline from the growth and progress in faith, grace, and holiness that might rightly be expected of them. Then I will show that the cause of this evil lies in what we are treating of. That indwelling sin is the cause of total apostasy in professing Christians who are unsound will be declared afterward. But the work we have in hand is a greater one. To prevail on true believers to a sinful decline and a gradual apostasy requires more strength and efficacy to be put forth than to prevail on professing Christians who are unsound to total apostasy. The wind that will blow a dead tree with no root to the ground will hardly shake or bend a living, well-rooted tree. But this it will do. Scripture mentions the first ways of David, and commends them above his later ones (2 Chr 17:3). The last ways even of David were tainted by the power of indwelling sin. Although we are told only of the actual outbreak of sin, yet the uncleanness, and the pride that was working in him when he numbered the people, were certainly rooted in a decline from his first frame. Those rushes did not grow without mire. David would not have done so in his younger days, when he followed God in the wilderness of temptations and trials, full of faith, love, humility, brokenness of heart, zeal, and tender affection for all the ordinances of God, all of which were eminent in him. But his strength is impaired by the efficacy and deceitfulness of sin; his locks are cut, and he becomes a prey to vile lusts and temptations. We have a notable instance in most of the churches that our Savior rouses to consider their condition in the Revelation. We may single out one of them. There were many good things in the church of Ephesus (ch. 2:2, 3), for which it is greatly commended. Yet it is charged with a decay, a decline, a gradual falling off and apostasy (vv. 4, 5): “You have abandoned your first love. Therefore, keep in mind how far you have fallen. Repent and perform the deeds you did at first.” By the testimony of Christ himself there was a decay, both inward, in the frame of heart as to faith and love, and outward, as to obedience and works, compared with what they had before. The same might be shown about the rest of those churches, except only one or two of them. Five of them are charged with decays and declines. That is why Scripture mentions with great commendation the “devotion of your youth” and “your love as a bride” (Jer 2:2, 3); our “first faith” (1 Tim 5:12); the “assurance we had at first” (Heb 3:14). And warnings are given that we “do not lose what we have worked for” (2 John 8). But why need we look back or search for instances to confirm the truth of this observation? An habitual decline from first engagements to God, from first attainments of communion with God, from first strictness in the duties of obedience, is ordinary and common among professing Christians. Suppose we took a general view, for this purpose, of the professing Christians in these nations, among whom the lot of the best of us will be found to fall, in part or in whole, in something or in everything. We might be abundantly convinced of the truth of this observation. Is their zeal for God as warm, living, vigorous, effectual, and earnest as it was when they first gave themselves to God? Or rather, has not a common, shallow, selfish frame of spirit come upon most professing Christians in its place? Iniquity has abounded, and their love has grown cold. Was it not once a burden to their spirits to hear the name and ways and worship of God blasphemed and profaned? Could they not have said with the psalmist (Ps 119:136), “My eyes shed streams of tears because Your law is not obeyed”? Were their souls not anxious about the cause of Christ in the world, like Eli’s about the ark? Did they not contend earnestly for the “truth once delivered to the saints,” and every part of it, especially where the grace of God and the glory of the gospel were especially concerned? Did they not labor to judge and condemn the world by a holy and separate way of life? And does the general run of professing Christians now remain in this frame? Have they grown in it and made progress? Or has not a coldness and indifference about this grown upon the spirits of many? Indeed, do not many despise all these things, and look on their own former zeal as folly? May we not see many who were once esteemed in the ways of the professed faith becoming daily a scorn and reproach to the men of the world through their misconduct, and that justly? Is it not with them as it was of old with the daughters of Zion (Isa 3:24), when God judged them for their sins and wantonness? Have not the world and self utterly ruined their profession of faith? And do they not disregard the things in which they once declared a singular concern? Indeed, have not some come, partly on one pretense and partly on another, to an open enmity toward and hatred of the ways of God? These ways please them no more, but are evil in their eyes. But not to mention such open apostates any further, whose hypocrisy the Lord Jesus Christ will shortly judge, how is it with the best? Have not almost all men grown cold and slack in these things? Are they not less concerned in them than before? Have they not grown weary and selfish in their religion, so that, as long as things are fairly well at home, they hardly care how they go out in the world? At least, do they not prefer their ease, reputation, safety, and worldly advantages to these things? Christ abhors that frame, and declares that those in whom it prevails are not his. Some, indeed, seem to keep a good zeal for truth. But where they make the fairest appearance, there they will be found most abominable. They cry out against errors, not for truth’s sake, but for the sake of parties and interests. Let a man be of their party and promote their interest, and however corrupt he is in his judgment, he is embraced, and perhaps admired. This is not zeal for God, but for a man’s self. It is not “zeal for Your house has consumed me,” but “Master, forbid them, because they do not follow with us.” It would no doubt be better for men never to claim any zeal at all than to put such wrathful selfishness in its place.

(2.) Secondly, is men’s delight in the ordinances and worship of God the same as in former days? Do they find the same sweetness and relish in them as they did of old? How precious the word was to them once! What joy and delight they had in attending on it! How they would have run and gone to share in it, wherever it was dispensed in its power and purity, in the evidence and demonstration of the Spirit! Did they not call the Sabbath their delight, and was not its approach a real joy to their souls? Did they not long for the fellowship and communion of the saints? And could they not undergo many dangers to obtain it? And does this frame still remain on them? Are there not decays and declines to be found among them? May it not be said, “Their hair is streaked with gray, but they do not know it?” Indeed, are not men ready to say with those of old, “What a weariness it is!” (Mal 1:13). “It is a burden and a weariness to be tied to observing all these ordinances. Why must we be so very strict in observing the Sabbath? Why must we hear so often? Why this distinction in hearing?” Without their noticing it, a great disrespect, and even a contempt, of the pleasant and excellent ways of Christ and his gospel has fallen on many professing Christians.

(3.) Thirdly, may not the same conviction be carried further by an inquiry into the whole course of obedience, and into the performance of the duties men have been engaged in? Does the same conscientious tenderness about sinning remain in many as in days of old? The same exact performance of private duties? The same love for the brothers? The same readiness for the cross? The same humility of mind and spirit? The same self-denial? The steam of men’s lusts, with which the air is tainted, will not allow us to say so.

We need go no further, then, than this wretched generation in which we live to prove the truth of the observation laid down as the foundation of the instance we are dwelling on. The Lord give repentance before it is too late!

Now all these declines, all these decays found in some professing Christians, proceed from this root and cause. They are all the product of indwelling sin, and all prove its exceeding power and efficacy. To prove this I will not need to go further than the general rule we have already considered from James: that lust, or indwelling sin, is the cause of all actual sin and all habitual declines in believers. This is what the apostle means to teach and declare in that place. So I will handle these two things. First, I will show that this proves a great efficacy and power in sin. Secondly, I will declare the ways and means by which it brings forth, or brings about, this cursed effect. All of it serves our general aim: to call on and warn believers to avoid it and to oppose it.

1. First, it appears to be a work of great power and efficacy from the provision that is made against it, over which it prevails. In the covenant of grace plentiful provision is made, not only to prevent declines and decays in believers, but also to carry them on continually toward perfection. For example:

(1.) First, the word itself, and all the ordinances of the gospel, are appointed and given to us for this end (Eph 4:11, 12, 13, 14, 15). The end for which gospel officers are given to the church is also the end for which all the ordinances they administer are given. For they are given “for works of ministry,” that is, for administering the ordinances of the gospel. Now what are these ends? They are all for preventing decays and declines in the saints, and all for carrying them on to perfection, as is said in v. 12. In general it is “for the perfecting of the saints,” carrying on the work of grace in them and the work of holiness and obedience by them; or it is “to build up the body of Christ,” building them up in an increase of faith and love, every true member of the mystical body. But how far are the word and ordinances appointed to carry them on and to build them up? Do they have fixed bounds to their work? Do they carry them so far and then leave them? No, says the apostle (verse 13). The dispensation of the word of the gospel and its ordinances is designed for our help, assistance, and furtherance until the whole work of faith and obedience is completed. It is appointed to perfect and complete the faith, knowledge, and growth in grace and holiness allotted to us in this world. But what if opposition and temptations lie in the way, with Satan and his instruments working with great subtlety and deceit? Why, verse 14: these ordinances are designed to safeguard and deliver us from all their attempts and assaults. So, being preserved in the use of them, “speaking the truth in love, we will in all things grow up into Christ Himself, who is the head.” This, in general, is the use of all gospel ordinances, the chief and main end for which God gave and appointed them: to preserve believers from all decays of faith and obedience, and to carry them on continually toward perfection. These are means the good vinedresser, God, uses to make the vine thrive and bear fruit. I could also show that this is the special end of each of them separately. Briefly, the word is milk and solid food for nourishing and strengthening believers of all sorts and all degrees. It has both seed and water in it, and fertilizer too, to make them fruitful. The ordinance of the Supper is appointed for the very purpose of strengthening our faith in remembering the death of the Lord, and for exercising love toward one another. The communion of saints is for building one another up in faith, love, and obedience.

(2.) Secondly, there is something that adds weight to this consideration. God does not allow us to be unmindful of this help he has given us, but is continually calling on us to use the means appointed for reaching the end proposed. He shows them to us, as the angel showed the spring of water to Hagar. Commands, exhortations, promises, and threats are multiplied for this purpose; see them summed up in Heb 2:1. He is continually saying to us, “Why will you+ die? Why will you+ wither and decay? Come to the pastures provided for you+, and your+ souls will live.” If we see a lamb run from the fold into the wilderness, we do not wonder if it is torn apart by wild beasts. If we see a sheep leaving its green pastures and streams of water to stay on dry, barren heaths, we think it no wonder, and inquire no further, if we see it lean and ready to perish. But if we find lambs wounded in the fold, we wonder at the boldness and rage of the beasts of prey that dared to attack them there. If we see sheep wasting away in full pastures, we judge them to be diseased and unsound. It is indeed no wonder that poor creatures who forsake their own mercies, and run away from the pasture and fold of Christ in his ordinances, are torn apart by various lusts and waste away with hunger and famine. But to see men living under and enjoying all the means of spiritual thriving, and yet decaying, not fat and flourishing but rather wasting and withering daily: this shows some secret, powerful disease whose poisonous and harmful qualities hinder the power and efficacy of the means they enjoy. This is indwelling sin. It is so wonderfully powerful, so effectually poisonous, that it can bring leanness on the souls of men in the middle of all the precious means of growth and flourishing. It may well make us tremble to see men living under the means of the gospel and using them, preaching, praying, the administration of the sacraments, and yet growing colder in zeal for God every day, more selfish and worldly, and even habitually declining from the degrees of holiness they had attained.

(3.) Thirdly, together with the dispensation of the outward means of spiritual growth or progress, supplies of grace are also continually given to the saints from their Head, Christ Jesus. He is the Head of all the saints. And he is a living Head, so living that he tells us that “because he lives, we also will live” (John 14:19). He imparts spiritual life to all who are his. In him is the fountain of our life, which is therefore said to be “hidden with Christ in God” (Col 3:3). And he gives this life to his saints by quickening them by his Spirit (Rom 8:11). And he continues it to them by the supplies of living grace that he imparts to them. From these two, his quickening of us and his continually giving out supplies of life to us, he is said to live in us (Gal 2:20): “I no longer live, but Christ lives in me.” The spiritual life I have is not my own. It was not drawn out of myself, nor is it maintained by myself, but it is purely and solely the work of Christ. So it is not “I who live, but he lives in me,” the whole of my life being from him alone. Nor does this living Head impart only a bare life to believers, so that they should merely live and no more, a poor, weak, dying life, as it were. He gives out enough to afford them a strong, vigorous, thriving, flourishing life (John 10:10). He comes not only that his sheep may have life, but that “they may have it more abundantly”; that is, in a plentiful way, so that they may flourish and be fat and fruitful. So it is with the whole body of Christ and every member of it (Eph 4:15, 16): “we will in all things grow up into Christ Himself, who is the head. From Him the whole body, fitted and held together by what every joint supplies, according to the effectual working of each part, makes the body grow and builds itself up in love.” The end of all the grace and supplies of life given from this living and blessed Head is the increase of the whole body and of every member of it, and its building itself up in love. His treasures of grace are unsearchable; his stores are inexhaustible. His life, the fountain of ours, is full and eternal; his heart is bountiful and large; his hand is open and generous. So there is no doubt that he gives out supplies of grace abundantly to all his saints for their increase in holiness. How is it, then, that they do not all flourish and thrive accordingly? It is here as you may often see it in a natural body. Though the seat and source of the blood and spirits in the head and heart are excellently good and sound, there may yet be a withering limb in the body; something intercepts the influences of life to it. So although the heart and head perform their office in giving supplies no less to that limb than to any other, all the effect produced is merely to keep it from perishing utterly; it grows weak and decays every day. The withering and decaying of any member of Christ’s mystical body is not for lack of his imparting grace for an abundant life. It is from the powerful interception of its efficacy by the interposing and opposition of indwelling sin. That is why, where lust grows strong, a great deal of grace will only keep the soul alive, and will not give it any eminence in fruitfulness at all. Christ often gives very much grace where not many of its effects appear. Grace spends its strength and power in withstanding the continual assaults of violent corruptions and lusts, so that it cannot put forth its own power toward further fruitfulness. Think of a powerful medicine, fit both to check harmful and noxious disorders and to comfort, refresh, and strengthen nature. If the infection is strong and greatly prevailing, the medicine spends its whole strength and power in subduing and correcting it, and contributes much less to the relief of nature than it would if it did not meet such opposition. So it is with the eye salve and the healing grace we have abundantly from the wings of the Sun of Righteousness. It is often forced to put forth its power to oppose and contend against prevailing lusts and corruptions, and to subdue them in any measure. That is why the soul does not receive the strengthening for duties and fruitfulness that it might otherwise receive from it. How sound, healthy, and flourishing, how fruitful and exemplary in holiness, many a soul might be by and with the grace continually imparted to it from Christ! Yet now, because of the power of indwelling sin, that soul is only not dead, but weak, withering, and useless. And this, if anything, is a notable evidence of the efficacy of indwelling sin: that it is able to put such a stop and check on the mighty and effectual power of grace. Despite the blessed and continual supplies we receive from our Head, many believers decline and decay, and that habitually, from what they had attained; their last ways do not match their first. This is what makes the vineyard on the very fertile hill bring forth so many wild grapes. This is what makes so many trees barren in fertile fields.

(4.) Fourthly, there are the continual supplies of grace that are constantly given to believers according to the terms of the covenant, which keep them from ever thirsting again in total lack. But besides these, there is also a readiness in the Lord Christ to give special help to the souls of his own, as their occasions require. The apostle tells us that he is “a merciful High Priest” and “able” (that is, ready, prepared and willing) “to help those who are being tempted” (Heb 2:18). On that account we are invited to come “with confidence” to the throne of grace, “so that we may receive mercy and find grace to help us in our time of need”; that is, grace that is sufficient, timely, and fitted to any special trial or temptation we may be tested by. Our merciful High Priest is ready to give out this special, timely grace, over and above the constant giving of supplies of the Spirit we mentioned before. Besides the never-failing springs of ordinary covenant grace, he also has special refreshing showers for times of drought. This is exceedingly to the advantage of the saints, for their preservation and growth in grace; and very many more things of the same nature might be added. But now, I say, in spite of all these, and the rest of similar importance, such is the power and efficacy of indwelling sin, so great is its deceitfulness and restlessness, so many are its wiles and temptations, that it often happens that many of those for whose growth and progress all this provision is made, will nevertheless, as was shown, go back and decline, even in their course of walking with God. Samson’s strength proved itself fully when he broke the seven fresh bowstrings and the new ropes he was bound with, as if they were burning tow and thread. A harmful infection in the body so stubborn that no use of the most effective remedies can prevail against it ought to be taken seriously. Such is this indwelling sin, if it is not watched over. It breaks all the cords made to bind it; it blunts the instruments appointed to root it up; it resists all healing medicines, however effective; and it is therefore assuredly of exceeding efficacy. Besides, believers have countless obligations on them, from the love and the command of God, to grow in grace and to press on toward perfection, just as they have abundant means provided for them to do so. Doing so is a matter of the greatest advantage, profit, sweetness, and contentment to them in the world. It is the burden and trouble of their souls that they do not do so, that they are not more holy, more zealous, more useful and fruitful; they desire it above life itself. They know it is their duty to watch against this enemy, to fight against it, and to pray against it, and they do so. They desire its destruction more than the enjoyment of all this world and all it can offer. And yet, in spite of all this, such is the subtlety and fraud, the violence and fury, the urgency and importunity of this adversary, that it often prevails to bring them into the woeful condition mentioned. So it is sometimes with believers as it is with men in some places at sea. They have a good, fair wind, perhaps all night long. They work their rigging and attend diligently to their business, and perhaps take great satisfaction in considering how they are getting on in their voyage. In the morning, or after a time, they come to measure what headway they have made and what progress they have had. They find that, instead of getting one step forward, they are much farther back than they were. They have fallen into a swift tide or current against them, which has frustrated all their labors and made the wind in their sails almost useless. By the wind they have held up somewhat against the stream, but they have made no progress. So it is with believers. They have a good wind of supplies of the Spirit from above. They attend to duties diligently, pray constantly, hear attentively, and leave out nothing that may carry them on their voyage toward eternity. But after a while, when they come to consider seriously, by examining their hearts and ways, what progress they have made, they find that all their help and duties have not been able to hold them up against some strong tide or current of indwelling sin. It is true that these have kept them from being driven and wrecked on rocks and shoals; they have preserved them from gross, scandalous sins. Yet they have lost ground in their spiritual frame, or gone backward, and are entangled in many woeful decays. This is a notable evidence of the life of sin that we are treating of. Now the purpose of uncovering this power of sin is that we may be careful to forestall and prevent it in its working. And of all the effects it produces, none is more dangerous or destructive than the one we have dwelt on last: that it prevails on many professing Christians to a habitual decline from their former ways and attainments, in spite of all the sweetness and excellence their souls have found in them. So, as was said, I will next consider by what ways and means, and with what help, it usually prevails in this way, so that we may be better instructed to watch against it.

Chapter XV

Decays in degrees of grace caused by indwelling sin. The ways it prevails for this purpose.

The ways and means by which indwelling sin prevails on believers to bring them to habitual declines and decays in degrees of grace and holiness now come under consideration, and they are many.

1. First, at the first conversion and calling of sinners to God and Christ, many fresh springs usually break out in their souls, and refreshing showers come on them, which carry them up to a high level of faith, love, holiness, fruitfulness, and obedience. It is like a river in a flood, when many smaller streams run into it: it swells over its banks and rolls on with more than its ordinary fullness. Now if these springs are not kept open, if they do not prevail for these showers to continue, they must decay and go backward. We will name one or two of them.

(1.) First, they have a fresh, vigorous sense of pardoning mercy. As this is in the soul, so will its love and delight in God be, and so will its obedience be. As the sense of gospel pardon is, I say, so will the life of gospel love be. Luke 7:47: “Therefore I tell you,” says our Savior about the poor woman, “because her many sins have been forgiven, she has loved much. But he who has been forgiven little loves little.” Her great love was an evidence of great forgiveness, and of her great sense of it. For our Savior is not giving a reason for her forgiveness, as though it were for her love, but a reason for her love: that it came from her forgiveness. In the preceding parable, from verse 38 onward, he had convinced the Pharisees he was dealing with that the one who was forgiven most would love most (verse 43). From this he gives an account of the great love of the woman, springing from the sense she had of the great forgiveness she had so freely received. So sinners at their first conversion are very aware of great forgiveness; “of whom I am the worst” lies closest to their heart. This greatly subdues their hearts and spirits to all that is in God, and quickens them to all obedience: that such poor cursed sinners as they were should be so freely delivered and pardoned! The love of God and of Christ in their forgiveness powerfully conquers and constrains them to make it their business to live for God.

(2.) Secondly, the fresh taste they have had of spiritual things keeps up such a savor and relish of them in their souls that the worldly satisfactions by which men are drawn away from close walking with God become tasteless and undesirable to them. Having tasted the wine of the gospel, they desire no other, for they say, “this is best.” So it was with the apostles when the choice of departing from Christ was offered them, after many who professed falsely had fallen away: “Do you+ want to leave too?” (John 6:67). They answer through Peter, “Lord, to whom would we go? You have the words of eternal life” (verse 68). They had such a fresh savor and relish of the doctrine of the gospel and the grace of Christ on their souls that they could not entertain any thought of turning away from it. Think of a man who has been kept long in a dungeon. If he is suddenly brought out into the light of the sun, he finds so much pleasure and satisfaction in it, in the beauties of the old creation, that he thinks he can never tire of it, and that he will never on any account be content to be in darkness again. So it is with souls when they are first brought into the “marvelous light” of Christ, to behold the beauties of the new creation. They see a new glory in him that has quite tarnished the desirableness of all earthly pastimes. And they see a new guilt and filth in sin that gives them an utter abhorrence of its old delights and pleasures; and so with other things.

Now while these and similar springs are kept open in the souls of converted sinners, they constrain them to a vigorous, active holiness. They can never do enough for God. So their zeal as saints often does not let them escape without some blots on their prudence as men, as might be shown in many of the martyrs of old.

This, then, is the first way, or at least one way, in which indwelling sin prepares men for decays and declines in grace and obedience: it tries to stop up or taint these springs. And there are several ways in which it brings this about.

[1.] First, it works by sloth and negligence. It prevails on the soul to neglect stirring up continual thoughts of, or about, the things that so powerfully influence it to strict and fruitful obedience. If care is not taken, if diligence and watchfulness are not used, and all the means God has appointed are not observed to keep a fresh and living sense of these things on the soul, they will dry up and decay. As a result, the obedience that should spring from them will do so too. Isaac dug wells, but the Philistines stopped them up, and his flocks had no benefit from them. Let the heart fall out of the habit of gracious, soul-moving thoughts of the love of God, the cross of Christ, the greatness and excellence of gospel mercy, and the beauty of holiness, even a little, and these things will quickly be as much estranged from a man as he can be from them. He who shuts his eyes in the sun for a while can see nothing at all when he opens them again. And as much faith as a man loses toward these things, so much power will they lose toward him. Because of his unbelief they can do little or nothing on him, though once they were so exceedingly effectual toward him. So it was with the bride in the Song of Songs (ch. 5:2). Christ calls to her (v. 1) with a wonderfully loving and gracious invitation to communion with himself. She had once been enraptured at the first hearing of that “joyful sound.” But now, under the power of sloth and carnal ease, she returns a sorry, excusing answer to his call, which ended in her own notable loss and sorrow. Indwelling sin, I say, prevails on the souls of men by spiritual sloth, so that they fail to heed the promptings of God’s Spirit in their former perceptions of divine love, and neglect stirring up continual thoughts of faith about it. When it does, a decay grows on the whole soul without their noticing it. So God often complains that his people had “forgotten him”; that is, they grew unmindful of his love and grace, and this was the beginning of their apostasy.

[2.] Secondly, it works by unsettling the soul, so that it has formal, weary, powerless thoughts of the things that should prevail on it to diligence in thankful obedience. The apostle warns us that in dealing with God we should “worship God acceptably with reverence and awe,” because of his purity, holiness, and majesty (Heb 12:28, 29). And this is what the Lord himself spoke at the destruction of Nadab and Abihu: “I will be sanctified in those who come near Me” (Lev 10:3). He will be dealt with in an awe-filled, holy, reverent manner. So we are to deal with all the things of God in which, or by which, we have communion with him. The soul is to have a great reverence for God in them. When men begin to treat them with light and ordinary thoughts, and do not use them and make the most of them for the ends for which they are appointed, these things lose all their beauty and glory and power toward them. When we have anything to do in which faith and love toward God are to be exercised, we must do it with all our hearts, with all our minds, strength, and souls, not lightly and perfunctorily, which God abhors. He requires not only that we keep his love and grace in remembrance, but that, as far as lies in us, we do it according to their worth and excellence. It was the sin of Hezekiah that he “did not repay the favor shown to him” (2 Chr 32:25). So while we consider gospel truths, the utmost effort of the soul ought to be that we may be transformed into the same image or likeness (2 Cor 3:18); that is, that they may have their full power and effect on us. Otherwise James tells us what our “beholding the glory of the Lord as in a mirror,” which the apostle mentions there, comes to; that is, our reading or hearing the mind of God in Christ revealed in the gospel (ch. 1:23, 24). “It is like a man who looks at his face in a mirror, and after observing himself goes away and immediately forgets what he looks like.” It makes no impression on him; it forms no idea or image of his likeness in his imagination, because he does it only lightly, with a passing look. So it is with men who will indeed think of gospel truths, but in a light way, without striving with all their hearts, minds, and strength to have them grafted into their souls, and all their effects produced in them. Now this is how sinners deal with these things in their first commitments to God. They never think of pardoning mercy without laboring to move their whole souls with it, and stirring themselves up to fitting affections and to returns of constant obedience. They never think of the excellence of Christ, and of spiritual things now newly revealed to them in a saving light, without pressing with all their might after a further and fuller enjoyment of them. This keeps them humble and holy; this makes them thankful and fruitful. But if the utmost diligence and care are not used to make use of this wisdom and grow in it, and to keep up this frame, indwelling sin, working by the futility of men’s minds, will bring them without their noticing it to content themselves with careless and rare thoughts of these things, without a diligent, earnest effort to give these things their due effect on the soul. As men decay in this, so they will assuredly decay and decline in the power of holiness and close walking with God. When the springs are stopped up or tainted, the streams will not run so swiftly, or at least not so sweetly, as before. By this means some, under an unbroken profession of faith, wither almost to nothing without noticing it. They talk of religion and spiritual things as much as they ever did in their lives, and perform duties as constantly as they ever did. Yet they have poor, lean, starving souls as to any real and effectual communion with God. By the power and subtlety of indwelling sin they have grown formal, and learned to deal with spiritual things in a superficial way, and by it they have lost all the life, vigor, savor, and efficacy these things had toward them. Be always serious in spiritual things, if you+ ever intend to be made better by them.

[3.] Thirdly, indwelling sin often prevails to stop up these springs of gospel obedience by false and foolish opinions that corrupt the simplicity of the gospel. False opinions are works of the flesh. For the most part they come from the futility and darkness of men’s minds, with a mixture, more or less, of corrupt affections. The apostle was watchful over his Corinthians in this matter. He was afraid that their minds might “by any means be corrupted from the simplicity that is in Christ” (2 Cor 11:2, 3), which he knew would be followed by a decay and decline in faith, love, and obedience. And so things often turn out in this case. We have seen some who received a sweet taste of the love of God in Christ, of the excellence of pardoning mercy, and walked humbly with God for many years in the faith and understanding of the truth. Yet by the corruption of their minds from the simplicity that is in Christ through false and foolish opinions, they have despised all their own experiences and rejected all the efficacy of truth for furthering their obedience. That is why John warns the elect lady and her children to take care that they are not led astray, “so that they do not lose what they have worked for” (2 John 8); that is, so that they do not themselves throw away all their former obedience as lost and of no value. We have countless instances of this in the days we live in. How many are there who not many years ago set an unspeakable value on the pardon of sin in the blood of Christ, who delighted in the gospel’s revelations of spiritual things and walked in obedience to God because of them? And now, having been deceived and turned aside from the truth as it is in Jesus, they despise these springs of their own former obedience. And as this is done grossly and openly in some, so there are more secret and more plausible suggestions of corrupt opinions that taint the springs and fountains of gospel obedience. They gain ground on men through the futility of their minds, which is a principal part of indwelling sin. Such are all opinions that tend to lessen special grace in its freedom and efficacy, and to exalt the wills or efforts of men in their spiritual power and ability. They are all works of the flesh. However much some may claim that they are useful for promoting holiness, they will be found to taint the springs of true evangelical obedience, to turn the heart from God without its noticing, and to bring the whole soul into a spiritual decay.

This is one way in which indwelling sin produces this destructive effect. It draws men away from the power, purity, and fruitfulness that went with their first conversion and commitments to God, and brings them into habitual decline, at least in the degrees of their holiness and grace. There is nothing we ought to watch against more, if we intend to deal effectually with this powerful and subtle enemy. It is no small part of the wisdom of faith to observe whether gospel truths still have the same savor to the soul and the same efficacy on it as they once had, and whether an effort is kept up to make use of them continually, as at the first. A commandment that is always practiced is always new, as John says of the commandment of love. And to the man who really makes use of gospel truths, they will always be new and fresh, though he hears them a thousand times, because they move him to newness of practice. But to another man, who grows accustomed to them, they are burdensome and ordinary; he even loathes the manna he is so used to.

2. Secondly, indwelling sin does this by taking men off their guard against the return of Satan. When our Lord Christ first comes to take possession of any soul for himself, he binds that strong man and plunders his goods. He strips him of all his power, dominion, and claim. Satan, dispossessed in this way and frustrated in his hopes and expectations, leaves the soul, finding it newly mortified to his baits. So he left our Savior after his first fruitless attempts. But it is said that “he left Him until an opportune time” (Luke 4:13). He intended to return again when he saw his advantage. So it is with believers too. Having been cast out of his hold on them, he leaves them for a time, or at least does so comparatively. Freed from his assaults and perplexing temptations, they go on vigorously in the course of their obedience, and so flourish in the ways of God. But this does not last. Satan returns again, and if the soul does not stand continually on guard against him, he will quickly gain such advantages as will put a notable interruption on its fruitfulness and obedience. That is why some, after spending some time, perhaps some years, in cheerful, exemplary walking with God, have spent all their later days, upon Satan’s return, wrestling with the perplexing temptations he has entangled them in. Others have plainly fallen under the power of his assaults. It is like a man who has lived usefully among his neighbors for a while, doing good and sharing as he was able, giving to the poor and helping all around him. At length he falls into the hands of vexatious, quarrelsome, oppressive men, and is forced to spend all his time and income defending himself against them at law, and so becomes useless in the place where he lives. So it is with many a believer. He has walked in a fruitful course of obedience, to the glory of God and the building up of the church of Christ. Then, being attacked afresh by the return of Satan in one way or another, he has enough to do for the rest of his life to keep himself alive, while in the meantime he decays woefully and goes backward in many graces. Now this too, though Satan has a hand in it, is from indwelling sin. I mean that the success Satan gains in his undertaking comes from it. Indwelling sin encourages him, makes way for his return, and gives his temptations an entrance. You+ know how it is with those from whom he is cast out only by gospel conviction of sin. After he has wandered and waited a while, he says, “I will return to the house I left.” And what is the outcome? Carnal lusts have prevailed over the man’s convictions of sin and made his soul fit to receive returning devils. It is the same as to the degree of prevailing power Satan gains against believers, through advantages given him by sin, which leaves the soul exposed to his temptations.

Now the ways and means by which indwelling sin gives Satan an advantage for his return are all those that dispose believers toward a decline, which will be mentioned afterward. Satan is a diligent, watchful, and crafty adversary. He will neglect no opportunity and no advantage that is offered him. So wherever our spiritual strength is impaired by sin, or whichever way our lusts press, Satan joins in with that weakness and presses toward that ruin. All the actings of the law of sin, then, serve this end of Satan. So at present I will mention only one or two of them that seem principally to invite Satan to attempt a return.

(1.) First, it entangles the soul in the things of the world, all of which are so many suppliers for Satan. When Pharaoh had let the people go, he heard after a while that they were “entangled in the wilderness,” and he supposes that he will therefore now overtake them and destroy them. This stirs him up to pursue them. Satan finds those he has been cast out from entangled in the things of the world, through which he is sure to find easy access to them, and he is encouraged to make a fresh attempt on them, as the spider is encouraged to come down on the strongest fly that is entangled in its web. For by his temptations he comes only to drive them into what they are already inclined to by their own lusts, adding poison to their lusts and paint to their objects. And by this advantage he often gets so far into the souls of men that they are never properly free of him again as long as they live. As men’s distractions from the world increase, so do their entanglements from Satan. When they have more to do in the world than they can well manage, they will have more to deal with from Satan than they can well withstand. When men are made spiritually faint by dealing in and with the world, Satan attacks them as Amalek attacked the faint and weak of the people who came out of Egypt.

(2.) Secondly, it produces this effect by making the soul negligent and taking it off its guard. We have shown at length before that one main part of the effectual deceitfulness of indwelling sin is to make the soul heedless, and to turn it away from the diligent, watchful attention to its duty that is required. Now there is nothing for which diligence and watchfulness are more strictly commanded than the returning assaults of Satan (1 Pet 5:8): “Be sober-minded and watchful.” And why so? Because of “your+ adversary the devil.” Unless you+ are exceedingly watchful, he will surprise you+ at one time or another. And all the commands of our blessed Savior to watch are always with reference to Satan and his temptations. Now when the soul is made careless and heedless, forgetting what an enemy it has to deal with, or is lifted up with the successes it has newly gained against him, then is Satan’s time to attempt to re-enter his old dwelling. If he cannot do that, he still makes their lives comfortless to themselves and unfruitful to others, weakening their root and withering their fruit by his poisoning temptations. He comes down on our duties of obedience as the birds came down on Abraham’s sacrifice. If we do not watch, as Abraham did, to drive them away [for by resistance he is overcome and put to flight], he will devour them.

(3.) Thirdly, indwelling sin takes advantage of the evil examples of professing Christians among whom men live to put forth its efficacy and deceit, so as to draw them away from their early zeal and holiness, from their first faith, love, and works. When men first engage in the ways of God, they have a reverent esteem for those they believe received that mercy before them. They love and honor these, as is their duty. But after a while they find many of them walking unevenly and crookedly in many things, and not unlike the men of the world. Here sin does not miss its advantage. Without their noticing it, it prevails on men to go along with them. “This way, this course of walking, does well enough for others; why may it not do so for us too?” Such is the inward thought of many, and it works effectually in them. And so, through the craft of sin, the generation of professing Christians corrupt one another. Think of a stream rising from a clear spring or fountain. While it runs in its own channel and keeps its water unmixed, it preserves its purity and cleanness. But when in its course it meets other streams that are cloudy and foul, though they run the same way, it becomes muddy and discolored too. So it is in this case. Believers come out from the spring of the new birth with some purity and cleanness, and for a while they keep it in the course of their private walking with God. But then, at times, they come into company with others whose profession of faith flows and runs the same way as theirs, toward heaven, but who are muddied and stained with sin and the world. Then they are often corrupted with them and by them, and so decline from their first purity, faith, and holiness. Now in case this has been the case with anyone who reads this discourse, I will add a few cautions that are necessary to preserve men from this infection.

[1.] First, in the body of professing Christians there is a great number of hypocrites. We cannot say of this or that man that he is one, yet it is most certain that there are some.
