Our Savior has told us that it will be so to the end of the world. Not all who have oil in their lamps have it in their vessels. Let men take care how they give themselves up to conforming to the professing Christians they meet. Otherwise, instead of saints and the best of men, they may sometimes set up hypocrites, who are the worst, as their example. And when they think they are like those who bear the image of God, they are conforming themselves to those who bear the image of Satan.

[2.] Secondly, you+ do not know what the present temptations of those whose ways you+ observe may be. Perhaps they are under some special desertion from God, and so are withering for a season until he sends them some refreshing showers from above. Perhaps they are entangled with some special corruptions that are their burden, which you+ do not know of. For anyone to fall willingly into the kind of frame others are thrown into by the power of their temptations, or to think that what he sees is enough for others, whose diseases he does not know, will be enough for him, is folly and presumption. Suppose a man knows this or that person to be a living man of healthy constitution. If he sees him crawling about his affairs, feeble and weak, sometimes falling, sometimes standing, and making little progress in anything, will he think it enough for himself to do the same? Will he not ask whether the person he sees has lately fallen into some disease or sickness that has weakened him and brought him into that condition? He certainly will. Take care, Christians: many of the professing Christians you+ associate with are sick and wounded. The wounds of some of them “are foul and festering because of their folly.” If you+ have any spiritual health, do not think their weak and uneven walking will be accepted from you+; much less think it will be well for you+ to become sick and wounded too.

[3.] Thirdly, remember that of many of the best Christians only the worst is known and seen. Many who keep up precious communion with God still often do not make so glorious an appearance, because of their natural temperaments of openness or quick temper, as others who perhaps fall short of them in grace and the power of godliness. As to their outward conduct, it may seem that they are scarcely saved, when as to their faith and love they may be eminent. Like the king’s daughter, they may be “all glorious within,” though their gown is not always “embroidered with gold.” Take care, then, that you+ are not infected with their worst, when perhaps you+ are not able to imitate them in their best. But to return.

(4.) Fourthly, sin does this work by cherishing some secret particular lust in the heart. The soul fights against it faintly. It does fight against it out of sincerity; it cannot help doing so. But it does not make thorough work of mortifying it vigorously by the strength and power of grace. Now where it is so with the soul, a habitual decline in holiness will assuredly follow. David shows us how in his first days he kept his heart close to God (Ps 18:23): “I have been blameless before Him and kept myself from my iniquity.” His great care was that no one lust should prevail in him or over him, which might be called his iniquity in a special way. Paul steered the same course (1 Cor 9:27). He was in danger of being lifted up by his spiritual revelations and enjoyments. This makes him “discipline his body and make it his slave,” so that no carnal reasonings or vain imaginations might take hold in him. But where indwelling sin has provoked, irritated, and strengthened a special lust, it assuredly proves a principal means of a general decline. For as an infirmity and weakness in any one vital part will make the whole body consumptive, so the weakness in any one grace that a perplexing lust brings with it will make the soul consumptive. It weakens spiritual strength in every way. It weakens confidence in God, in faith and prayer. The knees will be feeble and the hands will hang down in dealing with God where a galling and unmortified lust lies in the heart. It will take such hold of the soul that it will not be able to look up (Ps 40:12). It darkens the mind with countless foolish imaginations, which it stirs up to make provision for itself. It galls the conscience with the spots and stains it brings on the soul in and by its actings. It contends in the will for rule and dominion. An active, stirring corruption wants the commanding power in the soul, and it is again and again ready to take the throne. It disturbs the thoughts, and sometimes it will even frighten the soul away from dealing with it by meditation, for fear that the corrupt affections will be entangled by it, and grace will lose ground instead of prevailing. It often breaks out into scandalous sins, as it did in David and Hezekiah, and loads the sinner with sorrow and discouragement. By these and similar means it becomes like a moth in a garment to the soul, eating up and devouring its strongest threads. So although the whole still hangs loosely together, it is easily torn to pieces. Though the soul that is in this condition keeps up a fair profession of faith for a season, the man’s strength is secretly devoured, and every temptation tears and rips his conscience at will. It is with such men as with some who have been of a sound, strong, athletic constitution for many years. Some secret, wasting fever seizes them. For a time they take no notice of it. Or if they do, they think they will do well enough with it, and shake it off easily when they have a little leisure to attend to it. For the present they think, like Samson with his locks cut, that they will do as at other times. Sometimes, perhaps, they complain that they are not well, and do not know what is wrong with them; and perhaps they rise up violently in opposition to their disease. But after struggling in vain for a while, as the vigor of their spirits and their strength fail them, they are forced to yield to the power of a consumption. And now all they can do is barely enough to keep them alive. So it is with men brought into spiritual decays by some secret, perplexing corruption. Perhaps they have had a vigorous principle of obedience and holiness. Indwelling sin, watching for its opportunities, has kindled and inflamed some particular lust in them by some temptation or other. For a while, perhaps, they take little notice of it. Sometimes they complain, but think they will do as in former times. Then, weakened in their spiritual strength without noticing it, they have work enough to do keeping alive what remains and is ready to die (Hos 5:13). I will not add anything here about preventing and forestalling this advantage of indwelling sin, since I have treated of it specially and separately elsewhere.

(5.) Fifthly, it works by negligence in private communion with God in prayer and meditation. I have shown before how indwelling sin puts forth its deceitfulness in turning the soul from watchfulness in and toward these duties. If it prevails here, it will not fail to bring on a habitual decline in the whole course of obedience. All neglect of private duties springs from a weariness of God, as he complains in Isa 43:22: “But you have not called on Me, O Jacob, because you have grown weary of Me, O Israel.” Neglect of calling on God comes from weariness. And where there is weariness, there will be withdrawing from what we are weary of. Now since God alone is the fountain and spring of spiritual life, if there is weariness of him and withdrawing from him, it is impossible that a decay in that life should not follow. Indeed, what men are in these duties (I mean as to faith and love in them), that they are, and no more. Here lies the root of their obedience, and if this fails, all fruit will quickly fail. You may sometimes see a tree flourishing with fine and pleasant leaves and fruit. After a while the leaves begin to decay, the fruit to wither, and the whole tree to droop. Search, and you will find that the root has received a wound, or has perished in some way, and does not do its duty. It is by the root that the tree should draw moisture and richness from the earth to supply the trunk and branches with sap and juice for growth and fruit. So though the branches flourish for a while with what they had received, they must decay once their nourishment is cut off. So it is here. These duties of private communion with God are the means of receiving supplies of spiritual strength from him, of sap and richness from Christ the vine and olive. While they do so, the life and course of obedience flourishes and is fruitful, and all outward duties are cheerfully and regularly performed. But suppose there is a wound, a defect, a failing in what should first take in the spiritual vital moisture that should be conveyed to the whole. Then the rest may keep their station and appearance for a while, but after a while profession of faith will wither, fruits will decay, and the whole will be ready to die. That is why our Savior lets us know (Matt 6:6) that what a man is in secret, in these private duties, that he is in the eyes of God, and no more. One reason among others is that in these duties unmixed grace acts more vigorously than in any other duties whatever. In all or most particular duties there is the influence they may have from carnal motives, which are many, and whose ways of creeping in are subtle and imperceptible. Besides this, there is an alloy of gifts, which sometimes even devours the pure gold of grace that should be chief and principal in them. In these private duties there is immediate dealing between God and what is of himself in the soul. If sin once prevails by its deceits and treacheries to take the soul off from diligent attention to communion with God and from constancy in these duties, it will not fail to bring about a decline in the whole of a man’s obedience. It has made its entrance, and will assuredly make good its progress.

(6.) Sixthly, growing in notions of truth without matching practice is another thing indwelling sin uses to bring the souls of believers into decay. The apostle tells us that “knowledge puffs up” (1 Cor 8:1). If it is alone, not put to use in practice, it swells men beyond a due proportion. As with a man who has dropsy, we are not to expect that he has strength to match his size. It is like trees that are continually shooting up tall, which keeps them from bearing fruit. Men reach the point where they can take in and receive evangelical truths in a new and more glorious light, or a clearer revelation than before, or new manifestations of truth they did not know before, and they please themselves in doing so. When they do this without diligent efforts to have the power of those truths and notions on their hearts, and to have their souls conformed to them, they generally learn to treat in the same way all the truths they knew before, which were once set into their hearts with more efficacy and power. This has proved, if not the ruin, yet the great impairing, of many in these days of light in which we live. By this means many have withered from humble, close walking into an empty, barren, talking profession of faith. In a short time almost everything has become alike to them. Whether things were true or false, as long as they could debate them and dispute about them, all was well. This is food for sin; it hatches sin and increases it, and is increased by it. It is a notable way for the futility in the mind to exert itself without a rebuke from conscience. While men are talking, writing, and studying about religion, and hearing preaching, perhaps with great delight, like those in Ezek 33:32, conscience will be very well pacified, unless it is thoroughly awake and watchful, and furnished with spiritual wisdom and care. It will enter no rebukes or pleas against the way the soul is in. Yet all this may be nothing but the acting of the natural vanity that lies in the mind, and is a principal part of the sin we are treating of. And generally it is so when men content themselves, as was said, with the notions of truth, without laboring for an experience of their power in their hearts and bringing forth their fruit in their lives. On this a decay must follow.

(7.) Seventhly, growth in carnal wisdom is another help to sin in producing this sad effect. “Your wisdom and knowledge,” says the prophet, “led you astray” (Isa 47:10). As much as carnal wisdom increases, so much does faith decay. Its own work is to teach a man to trust to himself and in himself; faith’s work is to trust wholly in another. So carnal wisdom labors to destroy the whole work of faith by making the soul return to a deceiving fullness of its own. We have woeful examples of the prevailing power of this principle of decline in the days we live in. How many a poor, humble, brokenhearted creature, who followed God in simplicity and integrity of spirit, have we seen come to be dipped in a worldly, carnal frame, and utterly wither in their profession of faith! It came about through observing the ways and walk of others, and through giving in to the temptations to craft and cunning that their opportunities in the world gave them. Many are so stained by it that they cannot be recognized as the men they were.

(8.) Eighthly, some great sin lying long in the heart and conscience unrepented of, or not repented of as it ought to be and as the matter requires, furthers indwelling sin in this work. The great turn in the life of David, which gave his first ways the better reputation, lay in his harboring his great sin in his conscience without fitting repentance. It was otherwise with Peter, as we know, and he had a different outcome. A great sin will certainly give a great turn to the life of a professing Christian. If it is well healed in the blood of Christ, with the humbling the gospel requires, it often proves a means of more watchfulness, fruitfulness, humility, and contentment than the soul ever had before. If it is neglected, it certainly hardens the heart, weakens spiritual strength, and enfeebles the soul, discouraging it from all communion with God, and it is a notable principle of a general decay. So David complains (Ps 38:5): “My wounds are foul and festering because of my folly.” His present disease was not so much from his sin as from his folly; not so much from the wounds he had received as from his neglect to seek their cure in time. It is like a broken bone. If it is well set, it leaves the place stronger than before; if not, it makes the man a cripple all his days. We only name these things briefly. Several other advantages of the same kind that sin uses to produce this effect might also be given as instances, but these may be enough for our present purpose. Whatever it uses, sin itself is always the principle, and this is no small demonstration of its efficacy and power.

Chapter XVI

The strength of indwelling sin shown from its power and effects in unregenerate persons.

It is the power and efficacy of indwelling sin, as it remains in various degrees in believers, that we are treating of. Now I have shown elsewhere that its nature and all its natural properties still remain in them. So although we cannot prove directly what the strength of sin is in believers from what its power is in those in whom it is only checked and not weakened at all, still, by observing that power, we may warn believers of the real power of the deadly enemy they have to deal with.

Suppose the plague is raging violently in one city, destroying multitudes. And suppose there is in another city an infection of the same kind, which does not rise to that height and fury there because of the check it meets from a better air and from the remedies used. A man may still show the inhabitants of the second city the force and danger of the infection that has got in among them, by the effects it has produced and is producing among others who do not have the benefit of the preventives and preservatives they enjoy. This will both teach them to value the means of their preservation and make them more watchful against the power of the infection that is among them. So it is in this case. Believers may be taught what the power and efficacy of the plague of sin in them and among them is, by the effects the same plague produces in and among others, who do not have the checks on its poison and the preservatives from death with which the Lord Jesus has furnished believers.

Having fixed, then, on the demonstration of the power of sin from the effects it produces, and having given a twofold instance of this in believers themselves, I will now give further evidence of the same truth, or pursue the same evidence of it, by showing something of the power it exercises in those who are unregenerate, and so do not have the remedies against it that believers are furnished with.

I will not handle the whole power of sin in unregenerate persons. That is a very large field, and not the business I have in hand. I will only point out to believers, as I said, what they have to deal with, by a few instances of its effects in the unregenerate.

1. First, then, it appears in the violence it does to the nature of man, compelling men to sins wholly contrary to all the principles of the rational nature God has given them. Every creature of God has had a law of operation implanted in it at its creation. That law is the rule of everything that proceeds from it, of all it does of its own accord. So fire rises upward, weighty and heavy bodies fall downward, and water flows, each according to the principles of its nature, which give it the law of its operation. Whatever hinders them in their operation is force and violence, as what hinders a stone from falling or fire from rising. What forces them to move contrary to the law of their nature, as a stone to go upward or fire to go downward, is the greatest violence of its kind, and its degrees are endless. Now everyone would acknowledge that whatever could take a great millstone and fling it upward into the air would be a thing of wonderful force, power, and efficacy.

Man also has his law of operation and working created together with him. This may be considered in two ways: first, as it is common to him and other creatures; or, secondly, as it is distinctive to him, with reference to the special end for which he was made. Some things in this law of nature, I say, are common to man and other creatures: to nourish their young; to live quietly with those of the same kind and race; to seek and pursue what is good for them in the state and condition in which they were created. All brute living creatures have these things in the law of their nature, as man also has.

But besides these things, man was created in a special way to give glory to God by rational and moral obedience, and so to obtain a reward in the enjoyment of him. So there are many things in the law of his creation that are distinctive to him: to love God above all, to seek the enjoyment of him as his chief good and last end, to inquire after his mind and will, to yield obedience, and the like. All of these are part of the law of his nature.

Now these things are not distinguished as though a man might perform the actions of the law of his nature that are common to him and other creatures merely from the principles of his nature, as they do. The law of his dependence on God, and of doing everything in obedience to him, extends over all of them as well. He can never be considered a mere creature, but a creature made for the glory of God by rational, moral obedience: rational, because he chooses it and performs it with reason; and moral, because it is regulated by a law that reason attends to.

For example, man shares with other creatures the care for the nourishing of his children, the young and helpless ones who receive their being through him. A love and care for them is implanted in him, created together with the principles of his nature; so it is with other living creatures. Now let other creatures answer this instinct and inclination, and not be hardened against their young like the foolish ostrich, to whom God has not given this natural wisdom (Job 39:16, 17), and they fully answer the law of their creation. It is not so with man. It is not enough for him to answer the instinct and secret impulse and inclination of his nature and kind, as in nourishing his children. He must also do it in subjection to God, obeying him in it and doing it for his glory; for the law of moral obedience extends over his whole being and all his operations. Indeed, the whole of a man lies, as it were, in these things: in the things implanted in his nature as a creature, common to him and all other living creatures, backed by the command or will of God, since he is a creature capable of yielding moral obedience and of doing everything for God’s glory.

Whatever, then, can drive and compel a man to transgress this law of his nature must be reckoned of great force and efficacy. It is not only like throwing millstones upward, or driving beasts from caring for their young, or taking from cattle of one kind their herding together in quietness. Beyond that, it makes a man cast off, as far as lies in him, his fundamental dependence on God as a creature made to obey him.

Now this is often done by indwelling sin in unregenerate persons. Let us take a few instances.

(1.) First, there is nothing more deeply set into the principles of the nature of all living creatures, and so of man himself, than love for their young and care for their preservation and nourishment. Many brute creatures will die for them; some feed them with their own flesh and blood. All deprive themselves of the food that nature directs them to as their best, in order to give it to them, and they act on their behalf to the utmost of their power.

Now such is the efficacy, power, and force of indwelling sin in man, an infection the nature of other creatures knows nothing of, that in many it prevails to stop up this fountain. It beats back the stream of natural affections, roots up the principles of the law of nature, and drives men to neglect and destroy the fruit of their own bodies. Paul tells us that the Gentiles of old were ἄστοργοι, “without natural affection” (Rom 1:31). What he has in view is the barbarous custom among the Romans, who often, to spare themselves the trouble of bringing up their children and to be free to satisfy their lusts, destroyed their own children from the womb. So far did the strength of sin prevail to blot out the law of nature and to beat back its force and power.

Examples of this kind are common in all nations; among ourselves, of women murdering their own children through the deceitful reasoning of sin. In this sin turns back the strong current of nature, darkens all the light of God in the soul, and overrules all natural principles, even though they are backed by the power of the command and will of God. Yet this evil has been made fearfully worse through the efficacy of sin. Men have not only killed their children, but cruelly sacrificed them to satisfy their lusts. The apostle counts idolatry, and so consequently all superstition, among the works of the flesh (Gal 5:20); that is, among the fruit and product of indwelling sin. It is from this that men have done the horrid and unspeakable violence to the law of nature just mentioned. So the psalmist tells us (Ps 106:37, 38). The same is mentioned again in Jer 7:31 and in various other places. I have described elsewhere the whole manner of that abomination. For the present it may be enough to point out that they took their children and burned them to ashes in a slow fire. The wicked priests who assisted at the sacrifice gave them this relief: they made a noise and clamor, so that the vile wretches might not hear the woeful moans and cries of the poor, dying, tormented infants. I suppose that in this case we need no further evidence. Naturalists can give no rational account of the secret force of that little fish which, they say, will stop a ship in full sail in the middle of the sea; they can only wonder at it. And we must acknowledge that it is beyond our power to give an account of the secret force and unsearchable deceit in this inbred traitor, sin. It can not only stop the course of nature when all its sails that carry it forward are filled, as they are in the affection for children. It can also drive it backward with such violence and force as to make men deal with their own children as a good man could not be hired by any reward to deal with his dog. And it may not be to the disadvantage of the best to know and consider that they carry about with them, and in them, the thing that has produced these effects in others.

The same may be said of all the other sins against the first dictates of the law of nature with which mankind is or has been stained and disgraced: murder of parents and children, of wives and husbands, sodomy, incest, and similar enormities. In all of them sin prevails in men against the whole law of their being and dependence on God.

Why should I count up the murders of Cain and Abel, or the treason of Judas, with the circumstances that made them worse? Or recall the filth and villainy of Nero, in whom sin seemed to intend an instance of how far it could debase the nature of man? In a word, all the studied, premeditated perjuries; all the planned, bloody revenges; all the filth and uncleanness; all the enmity against God and his ways that is in the world, is fruit growing from this root alone.

2. Secondly, sin shows its efficacy in keeping men from believing under the dispensation of the gospel. This evidence must be cleared up a little further.

(1.) First, under the dispensation of the gospel there are only a few who believe. So its preachers complain (Isa 53:1): “Who has believed our message?” The apostle interprets this of the small number of believers (John 12:38). Our Savior Christ himself tells us that “many are called”—the word is preached to many—but “few are chosen.” And so the church complains of its numbers (Mic 7:1). There are few who enter by the narrow gate, and daily experience confirms this woeful observation. How many villages, parishes, and even towns may we go to where the gospel has perhaps been preached for many years, and perhaps hardly meet one true believer in them, one who shows forth the death of Christ in his way of life! In the best places, those most eminent for their profession of faith, are not such persons like the berries left when an olive tree is beaten, “two or three berries atop the tree, four or five on its highest branches”?

(2.) Secondly, in the preaching of the gospel everything that separately prevails on men to do whatever else they do in their lives is set before them together, as motives to believing. Whatever anyone does with deliberation, he does either because it is reasonable and good for him to do it, or because it is profitable and advantageous, or pleasant, or lastly necessary for avoiding evil. Whatever men do with deliberation, I say, whether it is good or evil, whether in the works of this life or in things that lead to another, they do it from one or another of the reasons or motives mentioned. And, God knows, the motives men are prevailed on by are often very poor and paltry of their kind. How often will men be led, for a very little pleasure or a very little profit, to do what will embitter their lives and damn their souls! And what effort they will make to avoid what they take to be evil or grievous to them! Any one of these is enough to oil the wheels of men’s utmost efforts and set them to work in earnest.

But now all these things meet in the offer of the gospel and the command to believe; and each of them in such a way that the whole world can offer nothing like it.

It is the most reasonable thing that can be put to a man’s understanding. Through his own fault he has lost the way in which he was first placed, the way of bringing glory to God and saving his own soul, the ends for which he was made. It is most reasonable that he should accept and embrace that other blessed, easy, safe, excellent way of attaining those ends, which God in infinite grace, love, mercy, wisdom, and righteousness has found out and offers to him. And,

[2.] Secondly, it is the most profitable thing a man can possibly be invited to, if there is any profit, benefit, or advantage in the forgiveness of sins, in the love and favor of God, in a blessed immortality, in eternal glory. And,

[3.] Thirdly, it is most pleasant as well. Surely it is a pleasant thing to be brought out of darkness into light, out of a dungeon to a throne, from captivity and slavery to Satan and cursed lusts into the glorious liberty of the children of God, with a thousand heavenly sweetnesses not to be mentioned now. And,

[4.] Fourthly, it is surely necessary. It is necessary not only from the command of God, who has supreme authority over us, but also indispensably so for avoiding the eternal ruin of body and soul (Mark 16:16). It is constantly offered on these terms: believe, or you perish under the weight of the wrath of the great God, and that forever.

But in spite of the fact that all these considerations are preached to men, and pressed on them in the name of the great God, from day to day and from one year to another, yet, as was observed before, there are very few who set their hearts on them so as to embrace what they lead to. Tell men ten thousand times that this is wisdom, and indeed riches; that all their profit lies in it; that they will assuredly and eternally perish, perhaps within a few hours, if they do not receive the gospel. Assure them that it is their one real stake and concern. Let them know that God himself says all this to them. Still it is all one: they do not regard it, they do not set their hearts on it, but as good as say plainly, “We will have nothing to do with these things.” They will rather perish in their lusts than accept mercy.

(3.) Thirdly, it is indwelling sin that both disables men from believing and hinders them from it, and that alone. Blindness of mind, stubbornness of will, and sensuality of the affections all join to keep poor perishing souls at a distance from Christ. Sin makes men blind, so that they cannot see his excellencies; obstinate, so that they will not lay hold of his righteousness; senseless, so that they take no notice of their own eternal concerns.

Now certainly whatever can prevail on men who are wise, sober, and prudent in other things to neglect and despise the love of God, the blood of Christ, and the eternal welfare of their own souls, on weak and worthless pretexts, must be acknowledged to have an astonishing force and efficacy going with it.

Whose heart, if he has once heard of the ways of God, can help bleeding to see poor souls eternally perishing under a thousand gracious invitations to accept mercy and pardon in the blood of Christ? And can we help being astonished at the power of the principle that makes them run headlong to their own destruction? Yet all this comes upon them from the power and deceit of the sin that dwells in them.

3. It is evident in their total apostasies. Many men who are not really converted are much worked on by the word. The apostle tells us that they “completely escape from those who live in error” (2 Pet 2:18). They separate themselves from idolatry and false worship, acknowledging and professing the truth. They also “escape the corruption of the world” (v. 20); that is, “the corruption in the world caused by lust,” as he puts it in ch. 1:4, the filthy, corrupt, and unclean ways that the men of the world walk and live in as they pursue their lusts. These they escape by amending their lives and ordering their conduct according to the conviction of sin they have from the word. For he tells us that all this is brought about “through the knowledge of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ”; that is, by the preaching of the gospel. They are worked on so far as to forsake all ways of false worship, to profess the truth, to reform their lives, and to walk according to the conviction of sin that is on them.

By this means they gain the reputation of professing Christians. “They have a reputation for being alive” (Rev 3:1), and they share in some or all of the privileges of the gospel that the apostle lists in Heb 6:4, 5.

It is not my present business to show how far, or in what respects, a man may be effectually worked on by the word and yet not be really won over to embrace Christ; or what the utmost bounds and limits of a common work of grace on unregenerate men may be. It is confessed on all sides that it may be carried so far that it is very difficult to tell its effects and products apart from those of the grace that is special and saving.

But in spite of all this, we see many of these men daily fall away from God, utterly and wickedly: some into debauchery and uncleanness, some into worldliness and covetousness, some into persecuting the saints, all to the perdition of their own souls. The apostle declares in the place mentioned how this comes about. They are, he says, entangled again. To entice and entangle is the very work of indwelling sin, as I have shown before from Jas 1:14, 15. It alone entangles the soul. As the apostle says in v. 18, they are enticed from their whole profession of faith into cursed apostasy through the lusts of the flesh.

Through its deceit and power it prevails on them to abandon their profession of faith utterly, and their whole engagement to God. And this proves the greatness of its strength and efficacy in several ways.

(1.) It does so in that it checks and overrules the exceeding greatness of power put forth in the word in their conviction of sin and reformation. We see by experience that men are not easily worked on by the word. Most men can live under its ministry all the days of their lives and remain as senseless and dull as the seats they sit on, or the flint in the rock. Mighty difficulties and prejudices must be conquered, and great blows must be given to the conscience, before this can be brought about. It is like stopping a river in its course and turning its streams another way; or keeping a stone from falling downward; or turning back the wild donkey when she is furiously set on going her way, as the prophet speaks (Jer 2:24). It takes mighty power and strength to turn men from their corrupt ways, sins, and pleasures; to make them pray, fast, hear, and do many things contrary to the principle of the flesh that secretly predominates in them, and to do them willingly and gladly; to make them profess Christ and the gospel, perhaps under some trials and reproaches; to give them light to see into various mysteries, and gifts for carrying out various duties; to make dead, blind, senseless men walk and talk and do all the outward offices and duties of living and healthy men; along with similar accompaniments of conviction of sin and reformation. These are the effects and products of mighty power and strength. Indeed, the power the Holy Spirit puts forth by the word is inexpressible: in staggering and convicting sinners, in awakening their consciences, enlightening their minds, changing their affections, awing their hearts, reforming their lives, and compelling them to duties.

But now to all of these indwelling sin gives a check and overrules them. It prevails against this whole work of the Spirit by the word, with all the advantages of providential dealings, in afflictions and mercies, that go with it. Once sin is enraged, all these things become no more than the fresh bowstrings and ropes Samson was bound with before his head was shaved. Only cry out to it, “The Philistines are upon you! Here is a subtle, a suitable temptation; now show your strength and efficacy,” and all these things become “like a strand of yarn seared by a flame.” Conscience is stifled, reputation in the church of God despised, light overthrown, the impressions of the word thrown off, convictions of sin swallowed down, heaven and hell despised. Sin makes its way through everything, and turns the soul utterly from the good and right ways of God. Sometimes it does this subtly, by imperceptible degrees. It takes away all the force of former impressions from the Spirit by the word; it stains conscience by degrees, hardens the heart, and makes the affections fleshly by its various workings. So the poor “backslider in heart” hardly knows what he is doing until he has come to the very bottom of all impiety, profaneness, and enmity against God. Sometimes, joining with some vigorous temptation, it suddenly and all at once plunges the soul into a course of estrangement from God and from professing his ways.

(2.) It takes them away from the hopes of heaven they had attained through their convictions of sin, obedience, and temporary faith or believing. There is a general hope of heaven, or at least of escaping hell, of an untroubled immortality, in the most dull and stupid souls in the world, who are persuaded, by tradition or by instruction from the word, that there is another state of things to come after this life. But in unconvicted, unenlightened persons it is a dull, senseless, unmoving thing. It has no other hold on them, and no power in them, except to keep them free from the trouble and perplexity of contrary thoughts and fears. It is otherwise with those who are worked on by the word as we have declared before. Their hope of heaven and a blessed immortality is often accompanied with great joys and exultations, and is a relief to them under and against the worst of their fears and trials. It is such that they would not part with it for all the world; and on every occasion they retreat to it in their minds for comfort and relief.

Now by the power of sin they are prevailed on to give up all this. Let heaven go if it will, a blessed immortality with the enjoyment of God himself; sin must be served, and provision made to fulfill its lusts.

Suppose a man in the things of this world had such a hope of a large inheritance, of a kingdom, that he was satisfied it would not fail him, but that in the end he would surely enjoy it, and lead a happy and glorious life in possession of it for many days. And suppose someone went to him and said, “It is true, the kingdom you look for is a wide and honorable dominion, full of all good and desirable things, and you may attain it. But come, throw away all hopes and expectations of it, and join me in the service and slavery of this or that oppressive tyrant.” You+ will readily grant that he must have some strange, bewitching power about him if he could prevail on a man in his right mind to follow his advice.

Yet so it is, and much more so, in the case we have in hand. Sin itself cannot deny that the kingdom of heaven the soul hopes for and expects is glorious and excellent. Nor does it set about convincing the man that his thoughts of it are vain and will deceive him. It simply prevails on him to throw away his hopes and despise the kingdom he was expecting, on no other motive than that he may serve some worldly, cruel, or filthy and sensual lust. Certainly here lies a secret efficacy whose depths cannot be fathomed.

(3.) The apostle shows the power of the entanglements of sin in and on apostates in this: that it turns them away from “the way of righteousness after they have known it” (2 Pet 2:21). At the last day it will be found “evil and bitter” that men live all their days in the service of sin, self, and the world, refusing to make any trial of the ways of God they are invited to. They have no experience of the excellence, beauty, pleasantness, and safety of those ways; yet they have had evidence brought to them from God himself that the ways are such, and so refusing them will, I say, be bitterness in the end. But the condition of those is far worse still who, as the apostle says, have “known the way of righteousness” and are turned aside from the holy commandment by the power of indwelling sin. To leave God for the devil after a man has made some trial of him and his service; heaven for hell after a man has had some cheering, refreshing thoughts of it; the fellowship of the saints for an alehouse or a brothel after a man has been admitted to their communion and tasted its pleasantness; to leave walking in pure, clear, straight paths in order to wallow in mire, sewage, and filth: this will be cause for lamentation. Yet sin prevails on apostates to do this. It does so against all their light, conviction of sin, experiences, professions of faith, and engagements, and whatever else may be strong on them to keep them in the known ways of righteousness.

(4.) It proves its strength in them by prevailing on them to a total renunciation of God as revealed in Christ, and of the power of all gospel truth, in the sin against the Holy Spirit. I do not now determine precisely what the sin against the Holy Spirit is, or what it consists in. There are different views of it. All agree in this: that by it an end is put to all dealings between God and man in a way of grace. It is a sin that leads to death. And the hardness and blindness of many men’s hearts bring them to this; by these they are at length set beyond the reach of mercy. They choose to have nothing more to do with God, and God swears that they will never enter his rest. So “sin brings forth death.” By it a man is brought to renounce the end for which he was made, to reject willfully the means by which he might come to the enjoyment of God, to provoke God to his face, and so to perish in his rebellion.

I have not mentioned these things as though I hoped to set out by them the full power of indwelling sin in unregenerate men. I only intended to give a glimpse of it by a few instances. Anyone who wants a fuller view of it need only open his eyes and take a little look at the wickedness that reigns, indeed rages, all over the world. Let him consider the prevailing flood of the things Paul names as “the acts of the flesh” (Gal 5:19, 20, 21) among the sons of men, in all places, nations, cities, towns, and parishes. Then let him add to this just one consideration. As to the outward acting of these abominations, the world, which is full of their steam, filth, and blood, is a pleasant garden, a paradise, compared with the heart of man. In that heart they are all conceived, and hourly millions more of viler abominations, which are stifled in the womb by some of the ways dwelt on before, and so can never be brought forth into the light. Let a man take this course, I say, using the law as his light and rule, and if he has any spiritual discernment he may quickly be satisfied in this matter.

And I showed at the beginning of this discourse how this consideration fully confirms the truth proposed.

Chapter XVII

The strength of sin shown from its resistance to the power of the law.

The strength of any person, or of a fortified city, may well be measured by the opposition it is able to withstand without being overcome. Suppose we hear of a city that has endured a long siege by a powerful enemy and yet has not been taken or conquered, whose walls have endured heavy bombardment and are not demolished. Though we have never seen the place, we conclude that it is strong, if not impregnable.

This consideration will also show the power and strength of indwelling sin. It is able to hold out, and not only to live, but also to secure its reign and dominion against very strong opposition made to it.

I will give only one instance: the opposition made to it by the law. That opposition is often great and terrible, and always fruitless. Sin bears all the law’s assaults and is not overcome. There are several things in which the law sets itself against sin and its power. For example:

1. First, it uncovers it. Sin in the soul is like a secret, wasting disease in the body: its being unknown and unnoticed is one great means of its prevailing. Or it is like traitors in a civil state, who carry on their design vigorously while they lie hidden. Most men in the world know nothing of this sickness, indeed this death, of their souls. Though they have been taught something of the doctrine of it, they know nothing of its power. They do not know it in such a way as to deal with it as their deadly enemy. Whatever a man is told, he cannot be said to know that he has a wasting fever if he loves his life and does not set himself to stop its progress.

This, then, the law does: it uncovers this enemy. It convinces the soul that there is such a traitor harbored in its bosom. Rom 7:7: “I would not have known sin except through the law. For I would not have known lust if the law had not said, ‘Do not covet.’” “I would not have known it”; that is, fully, clearly, distinctly. Conscience will raise something of a commotion about it, but a man cannot know it clearly and distinctly from that. Conscience gives a man such a sight of it as the blind man in the Gospel had at the first touch on his eyes: “I can see the people, but they look like trees walking around”; dimly and confusedly. But when the law comes, it gives the soul a distinct sight of this indwelling sin. Again, “I would not have known it”; that is, its depths, its root, the habitual inclination of my nature to sin, which is called lust here, as it is by James (ch. 1:14). I would not have known it, or not known it to be sin, except through the law.

This, then, the law does: it drags this traitor out of its secret lurking places, the inmost recesses of the soul. When the law comes, a man is no longer ignorant of his enemy. If he will now perish by it, it is openly and knowingly. He cannot help saying that the law warned him of it and uncovered it to him. Indeed, it raised a crowd against it in the soul, of various affections, as an officer does who discovers a thief or robber and calls out for help to arrest him.

2. Secondly, the law not only uncovers sin, but shows it to be a very bad lodger, dangerous, indeed destructive, to the soul. Rom 7:13: “Did that which is good” (that is, the law), “then, become death to me? Certainly not! But in order that sin might be exposed as sin, it produced death in me through what was good, so that through the commandment sin might become utterly sinful.” There are many things in this verse that do not concern us at present. All I aim at is the exposing of sin by the law. It is “exposed as sin”; and it is shown in its own colors, and appears “utterly sinful.” The law makes the soul know the filth and guilt of this indwelling sin, how great they are; how vile it is, what an abomination, what enmity against God, how hated by him. Whatever thoughts the soul had of it before, it will never again look on it as a small matter; and by this it is greatly taken aback.

It is like a man who finds himself somewhat ill and sends for a skilled physician. When the physician comes, the man asks his judgment of the illness. The physician considers his condition and tells him, “Alas, I am sorry for you. The case is far otherwise with you than you imagine. Your disease is mortal. It has gone so far, pressing on your spirits and infecting the whole mass of your blood, that I fear you will live only a very few hours unless the most effectual remedies are used.”

So it is in this case. A man may have some trouble in his mind and conscience about indwelling sin. He finds that all is not well with him as it should be, more from the effects of sin and its continual outbreaks than from its nature, which he hopes to wrestle with. But now when the law comes, it lets the soul know that its disease is deadly and mortal, that it is utterly sinful, as being the root and cause of all his estrangement from God. In this way, too, the law proceeds against it.

3. Thirdly, the law judges the person, or lets the sinner know plainly what he is to expect on account of this sin. This is the law’s own work; its uncovering power is only preparation for its judging. The law is itself when it is on the throne. Here it does not mince matters with sinners, as we are used to doing with one another, but tells him plainly, “You are that man!” “You are the one in whom this utterly sinful sin dwells, and you must answer for its guilt.” And this, I think, if anything, should rouse a man to set himself against it, and indeed to destroy it utterly. The law lets him know that on account of this sin he is liable to the curse and wrath of the great God against him. Indeed, it pronounces the sentence of everlasting condemnation on him on that account. “Remain in this state and perish” is its language. It does not leave the soul without this warning in this world, and so it will leave it without excuse in the world to come.

4. Fourthly, the law follows up its sentence in such a way that it disquiets and frightens the soul, and does not allow it to enjoy the least rest or quiet while it harbors its sinful lodger. Whenever the soul has given in to sin’s commands and made provision for it, the law immediately flies at it with the wrath and terror of the Lord and makes it quake and tremble. It will have no rest, but is like a poor beast with a deadly arrow sticking in its side, which makes it restless wherever it is and whatever it does.

5. Fifthly, the law does not stop there; it also slays the soul (Rom 7:9). That is, by convicting him of the nature, power, and ill desert of this indwelling sin, it strips the person in whom sin dwells of all the life of self-righteousness and hope that he formerly kept himself going with. It leaves him a poor, dead, helpless, hopeless creature. And all this it does in pursuing its opposition to this sin. May we not now expect that sin’s power will be quelled and its strength broken, that it will die away under these blows of the law of God? But the truth is that its power and strength are such that it is quite otherwise. The poets tell of one born of the earth who, when someone thought to kill him by throwing him on the ground, gained new strength from every fall and was more vigorous than before. So it is with all the falls and repulses the law gives to indwelling sin. For:

(1.) First, it is not conquered. Conquest implies two things for the one conquered: first, loss of dominion; and secondly, loss of strength. Wherever anyone is conquered, he is stripped of both. He loses both his authority and his power. So the strong man armed, when he is overcome, is tied up, and his goods are plundered. But neither of these comes upon indwelling sin through the assaults of the law. It does not lose one bit of its dominion or strength by all the blows given to it. The law cannot do this (Rom 8:3). It cannot strip sin of its power and dominion; for he who is under the law is also under sin. That is, whatever power the law gains over a man’s conscience, so that he is afraid to sin for fear that its sentence and curse will fall on him, sin still reigns and rules in his heart. That is why the apostle says (Rom 6:14), “Sin shall not have dominion over you+, because you+ are not under law, but under grace.” He plainly implies that however much a person is subject to the authority of the law, that will not exempt and free him from the dominion of sin. Indeed, by all its work on the soul, the law, instead of freeing and releasing it from the reign of sin and bondage to it, incidentally greatly increases its misery and bondage, as the sentence of the judge on the bench against a criminal adds to his misery. The soul is under the dominion of sin, and perhaps remains in its woeful condition in great complacency, fearing neither sin nor judgment. The law attacks him in this condition by all the ways mentioned before, and brings him into great trouble and perplexity, fear and terror; but it does not deliver him at all. So it is with the soul as it was with the Israelites when Moses had delivered his message to Pharaoh. They were so far from gaining liberty by it that their bondage was increased, “and they realized they were in trouble” (Exod 5:19). And we will see that sin acts like Pharaoh. Finding its rule disturbed, it grows more furiously oppressive and doubles the bondage of their souls. It is not the work of the law, then, to destroy sin, or to strip it of the dominion it has by nature.

Nor does sin lose any of its strength through all these blows of the law. It keeps both its authority and its force; it is neither destroyed nor weakened. Indeed,

(2.) Secondly, it is so far from being conquered that it is only enraged. The whole work of the law only provokes and enrages sin, and makes it put out its strength, as it has opportunity, with more power, vigor, and force than before. The apostle shows this at length in Rom 7:9, 10, 11, 12, 13.

But you+ will say, “Do we not see by experience that many are worked on by the preaching of the law so that they give up many sins, amend their lives, and fight hard against the outbreaks of other corruptions they cannot yet mortify? It cannot be denied that the power and efficacy of the law is great when it is preached and applied to the conscience in a fitting way.” I answer:

[1.] First, it is acknowledged that the power of the law of God is very great and effectual. Great are the effects worked by it, and it will surely accomplish every end for which God has appointed it. Yet the subduing of sin is no part of its work; God has not designed it for that purpose. So it is no dishonor to it if it cannot do what is not its own work (Rom 8:3).

[2.] Secondly, whatever effects it has on some, we see that in most the power and prevailing of sin is such that the law makes no impression on them at all. May you not see men everywhere living for many years in congregations where the law is powerfully preached and applied to the conscience for all the ends and purposes the Lord is pleased to use it for, without being moved by it once? They receive no more impression from its blows than an adamant stone would from blows with a straw. They are neither convicted by it, nor terrified, nor awed, nor instructed, but remain deaf, ignorant, senseless, and complacent, as if they had never been told of the guilt of sin or the terror of the Lord. Congregations are full of such people, who proclaim the triumphant power of sin over the ministry of the law.

[3.] Thirdly, when any of the effects mentioned are produced, it is not from the power of the letter of the law, but from the actual efficacy of the Spirit of God putting forth his might and power for that end and purpose. And we do not deny that the Spirit of the Lord is able to restrain and quell the power of lust when he pleases; we have considered before some ways in which he is pleased to do so. But,

[4.] Fourthly, in spite of all that may be observed of the power of the law on the souls of men, it is still most evident that lust is not conquered, subdued, or mortified by it. For,

1st. First, though the course of sin may be driven back for a season by the ministry of the law, its spring and fountain is not dried up by it. Though it withdraws and hides itself for a season, it is only, as I have shown elsewhere, to get out of a storm and then return again. A traveler who meets a violent storm of thunder and rain on his way immediately turns off his road to some house or tree for shelter. Yet this does not make him give up his journey; as soon as the storm is over, he returns to his way and his progress again. So it is with men in bondage to sin. They are pursuing their lusts; the law meets them in a storm of thunder and lightning from heaven, terrifies them, and hinders them on their way. This turns them out of their course for a season; they will run to prayer or amendment of life for some shelter from the storm of wrath they fear is coming on their consciences. But is their course stopped? Are their principles altered? Not at all. As soon as the storm is over, and the sense and terror that was on them begins to wear off, they return to their former course in the service of sin again. This was the state of Pharaoh again and again.

2nd. Secondly, at such seasons sin is not conquered but diverted. When it seems to fall under the power of the law, it is in fact only turned into a new channel; it is not dried up. Suppose you set a dam against the streams of a river, so that you let no water pass in the old course and channel, but it breaks out another way and turns all its streams into a new course. You will not say you have dried up that river, though some who come and look into the old channel may perhaps think the waters are utterly gone. So it is in this case. Perhaps the streams of sin run in open sensuality and profaneness, in drunkenness and vice. The preaching of the law sets a dam against these courses. Conscience is terrified, and the man does not dare to walk in the ways he was engaged in before. His companions in sin, not finding him in his old ways, begin to laugh at him as one who is converted and growing strict. Professing Christians themselves begin to be persuaded that the work of God is on his heart, because they see his old streams dried up. But if there has been only a work of the law on him, a dam has been put to his course, but the spring of sin is not dried up; only its streams are turned another way. Perhaps the man has fallen into other, more secret or more spiritual sins. Or if he is beaten off from them too, the whole strength of lust and sin will take up its residence in self-righteousness, and pour out by it streams as filthy as by any other way whatever. So in spite of the whole work of the law on the souls of men, indwelling sin will still keep alive in them; and this is another evidence of its great power and strength.

I will touch on some other evidences of the same truth I am considering, but I will be brief with them. Next, then, the great efforts of men ignorant of the righteousness of Christ to subdue and mortify sin, which are all fruitless, show its great strength and power.

Men who have no strength against sin may still be made aware of the strength of sin. The way they mostly come to that knowledge is by some prior sense they have of the guilt of sin. Men have this by the light of their consciences; they cannot avoid it. It is not a matter of their choice. Whether they want to or not, they cannot help knowing that sin is evil, and such an evil as makes them liable to the judgment of God. This galls the minds and consciences of some so much that they are kept in awe and do not dare to sin as they would. Awed by a sense of the guilt of sin and the terror of the Lord, men begin to try to abstain from sin, at least from the sins they have been most terrified about. While they have this design in hand, the strength and power of sin begins to show itself to them. They begin to find that there is something in them that is not in their own power. For in spite of their resolutions and purposes, they still sin, and in such a way that their consciences tell them they must therefore perish eternally. This sets them on their own efforts to suppress the outbreaks of sin, because they cannot be quiet, or have any rest or peace within, unless they do. Now they are ignorant of the only way by which sin is to be mortified, that is, by the Spirit of Christ. So they fix on many ways of suppressing it in their own strength, if not of killing it. In the same way, being ignorant of the only way by which consciences burdened with the guilt of sin may be pacified, that is, by the blood of Christ, they try in vain by many other ways to accomplish that end; for no man can obtain peace with God by any efforts of his own. We must look into some of the ways in which they try to suppress the power of sin that throws them into an unquiet condition, and into how insufficient those ways are for that end.

(1.) First, they will promise and bind themselves by vows to keep from the sins they have been most prone to, and so most troubled with. The psalmist shows that this is one great engine by which false and hypocritical persons try to free and deliver themselves from trouble and perplexity. They make promises to God, which he calls “flattering Him with their mouths” (Ps 78:36). So it is in this case. Freshly galled with the guilt of some sin that, by the power of their temptations, they have perhaps often been overtaken in, they vow and promise that they will not commit that sin again, at least for some set period of time that they fix. And some who claim to direct their consciences in this duty prescribe this course of action to them. Their conscience about this vow now makes them watch over themselves as to the outward act of the sin they are galled with. And so it has one of two effects: either they abstain from it for the time they set, or they do not. Suppose they do not abstain, as they seldom do, especially if it is a sin with a special root in their nature and constitution, made into a habit by custom, and some fitting temptation is presented to them. Then their sin is increased, and their terror with it, and they are woefully discouraged from making any opposition to sin. So for the most part, after one or two vain attempts, or perhaps more, knowing no other way to mortify sin than this way of vowing against it and keeping that vow in their own strength, they give up all struggle and become wholly the servants of sin, kept within bounds only by outward considerations, without any serious efforts to recover. Or secondly, suppose they succeed in their resolutions, and abstain from actual sins for their appointed season. Commonly one of two things follows. Either they think they have done their duty well, and so may now indulge their corruptions and lusts a little, at least for a season, and so they are entangled again in the same snares of sin as before. Or else they reckon that their vow and promise has preserved them, and so they “sacrifice to their own net and dragnet,” setting up a righteousness of their own against the grace of God. This is so far from weakening indwelling sin that it strengthens it in its root and principle, so that it may reign securely in the soul afterward. Or at the most, the best success that can be imagined for this way of dealing with sin is merely the restraining of some of its outward outbreaks, which does nothing to weaken its power. So such persons, with all their efforts, are very far from being freed from the inward toiling, burning, disquieting, perplexing power of sin. And this is the state of most men who are kept in bondage under the power of conviction of sin. Hell, death, and the wrath of God are continually set before their consciences. This makes them labor with all their strength against what in sin most enrages their consciences and most increases their fears, that is, its actual outbreak; because, for the most part, while they are free from that, they are safe, though in the meantime sin lies in turmoil in the heart and defiles it continually. With running sores, outward repelling medicines may skin them over and keep their corruption from coming out. But the outcome is that they make them fester inwardly, and so, though perhaps not so foul and offensive as before, they prove far more dangerous. So it is with this repelling of the power of corruption by men’s vows and promises against it. Perhaps the outward outbreaks are restrained for a season, but the inward root and principle is not weakened in the least. And most commonly this is the outcome of this way: sin, having gained more strength and been enraged by its restraint, breaks all its bounds and takes the soul captive to all filthy abominations. As was observed before, this is the principle of most of the visible apostasies we see in the world (2 Pet 2:19, 20).

Because of the hateful, fierce, poisonous nature of this indwelling sin, the Holy Spirit compares sinners to lions, bears, and cobras (Isa 11:6, 7, 8, 9). Now this is the excellence of gospel grace: it changes the nature and inward principles of these otherwise wild and untamed beasts, making the wolf like the young goat, the lion like the lamb, and the bear like the cow. When this is done, they may safely be trusted; “a little child will lead them.” But these efforts of their own do not change the nature at all; they only restrain the outward violence. A man who takes a lion or a wolf and shuts it up to keep it from ravaging, while its inward violence remains, may well expect that at one time or another it will break its bonds and return to its former ways of plunder and violence. Shutting them up, as we see, does not change their nature at all, but only restrains their rage from doing open damage. So it is in this case. It is grace alone that changes the heart and takes away the poison and fierceness that is in men by nature. Men’s efforts of their own only hold them in check as to some outward outbreaks. But,

(2.) Secondly, beyond bare vows and promises, with some watchfulness in keeping them by a reasonable use of ordinary means, men have put themselves, and some still put themselves, on extraordinary ways of mortifying sin. This is the foundation of everything in the papacy that has “an appearance of wisdom” and religion. Their hours of prayer, their fastings, their walling and cloistering themselves up, their pilgrimages, penances, and self-torturing discipline all spring from this root. I will not speak of the countless evils that have gone with these self-invented ways of mortification, and how all of them have been turned into means, occasions, and advantages for sinning. Nor will I speak of the horrible hypocrisy that clearly clings to most of those who observe them; nor of the superstition that gives life to them all, which is riveted into the nature and constitution of some, fixed on others by deep-rooted prejudices, and taken up by others for worldly advantages. But I will suppose the best that can be made of it, and it will be found to be a self-invented design of men ignorant of the righteousness of God, to put a check on this power of indwelling sin we are speaking of. It is almost incredible what fearful self-wasting and horrible sufferings this design has carried men into. And no doubt their blind zeal and superstition will rise up in judgment and condemn the horrible sloth and negligence of most of those to whom the Lord has granted the saving light of the gospel. But what is the end of these things? The apostle gives us a brief account (Rom 9:31, 32). They do not attain the righteousness they aim at; they do not reach conformity to the law; sin is not mortified, and its power is not even weakened. What it loses in sensual, carnal pleasures it makes up with great advantage in blindness, darkness, superstition, self-righteousness and pride of soul, and contempt of the gospel and its righteousness. And it reigns no less than in the most profligate sinners in the world.

(3.) Lastly, the strength, efficacy, and power of this law of sin may be further shown from its life and being in the soul, in spite of the wound given to it at the soul’s first conversion to God, and the continual opposition grace makes to it. But this is the subject and design of another work.

It may now be expected that we should add here the special uses of all this uncovering of the power, deceit, prevailing, and success of this great adversary of our souls. But as for the humility, self-abasement, watchfulness, diligence, and turning to the Lord Christ for relief that befit those who find the power of this law of sin in themselves by experience, these have been mentioned and pressed as occasion arose throughout the whole preceding discourse. And as for the actual mortification of it, I will only recommend to the reader for his direction another small treatise written long ago for that purpose. I suppose he may do well to consider it together with this one, if he finds that these things concern him.

To the only wise God our Savior be glory and majesty, dominion and power, both now and forever. Amen.
